Embedded special Legendrian surfaces in 𝕊 5 \mathbb{S}^{5}
Sebastian Heller, Charles Ouyang, Franz Pedit

Abstract
. We construct the first smooth embedded compact special Legendrian surfaces in 𝕊 5 \mathbb{S}^{5} of genus greater than one. More precisely, for every sufficiently large integer k k , we construct an embedded special Legendrian surface whose conformal structure is the Fermat curve of degree k k and genus 1 2 ​ ( k − 1 ) ​ ( k − 2 ) \tfrac{1}{2}(k-1)(k-2) . Our approach combines an elementary implicit function theorem with the description of special Legendrian surfaces via loop algebra-valued meromorphic connections and a characterization of the unitarizability locus in the S ​ L 3 ​ ( ℂ ) {SL}_{3}(\mathbb{C}) -character variety of the thrice-punctured sphere.



1.
Introduction

Calabi–Yau manifolds have occupied a central role in both differential geometry and theoretical physics. In complex dimension three, they are particularly prominent because the Strominger–Yau–Zaslow conjecture
[
54
]
predicts that, in the large complex structure limit, they admit fibrations by special Lagrangian three-tori.
From this perspective, certain point singularities of Calabi–Yau threefolds can be viewed as arising by “pinching off” a generator of the 3-torus fiber. Upon rescaling near the singularity, one expects to obtain a special Lagrangian cone in
\mathbb{C}^{3}
, whose link is a closed surface. While it is expected that generically the link will have genus one, higher genus links cannot completely be ruled out. Indeed, Joyce
[
42
]
defined a stability index, showing that eigenvalues in a certain range of the induced Laplacian on the link will correspond to how likely its cone will appear as a singularity—regardless of genus. Intersecting the cone with the round 5-sphere
\mathbb{S}^{5}
yields a special Legendrian surface, which, being calibrated, is automatically minimal
[
20
]
. Taking the quotient under the Hopf projection then produces a minimal Lagrangian surface in
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
. Conversely, starting from a minimal Lagrangian surface in
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
, one may lift—possibly after passing to a threefold cover—to a special Legendrian surface in
\mathbb{S}^{5}
, whose cone in
\mathbb{C}^{3}
is special Lagrangian. Hence, constructing special Lagrangian cones in
\mathbb{C}^{3}
, special Legendrian surfaces in
\mathbb{S}^{5}
, and minimal Lagrangian surfaces in
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
is equivalent.

From a differential-geometric point of view, minimal Lagrangian surfaces in
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
are natural objects because they combine the Riemannian and symplectic geometry of the Kähler manifold
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
. In genus zero the theory is rigid: an immersed minimal Lagrangian
2
-sphere in
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
is the double cover of a totally geodesic
\mathbb{RP}^{2}

[
56
]
. More generally, classical holomorphic-differential methods lead to the classification of minimal
2
-spheres in spheres and complex projective spaces
[
7
,
8
,
12
,
16
]
. In genus one, by contrast, integrable-systems methods become effective: minimal Lagrangian tori in
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
admit a complete algebro-geometric description in terms of spectral curves and linear flows in Jacobians
[
50
,
10
,
21
,
44
,
9
]
. The Clifford torus is the simplest example.

Beyond genus one, the theory is far less developed. For minimal
Lagrangian surfaces in
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
, Joyce emphasized the lack of
compact higher-genus examples
[
43
]
. Prior to the present work, the
only smooth compact higher-genus examples known were those of
Haskins and Kapouleas
[
22
]
, obtained by gluing special Legendrian
cylinders to an equatorial
2
-sphere in
\mathbb{S}^{5}
. Their cyclic symmetry
forces the genus to be odd, except for one tetrahedrally symmetric example
of genus four, and embeddedness of the corresponding special Legendrian
surfaces in
\mathbb{S}^{5}
was not established.
One should also mention Wang’s variational construction
[
59
]
, which was
proposed as a method to obtain compact special Legendrian surfaces
of high genus.
However, the result only provides possibly branched
Hamiltonian stationary Lagrangian surfaces in
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
.

The examples constructed in this paper appear to be the first smooth embedded compact
special Legendrian surfaces in
\mathbb{S}^{5}
of genus greater than one.
Our main result is the following.

Theorem
.

There exists
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for every
k\geq k_{0}
there exists
an embedded special Legendrian surface in
\mathbb{S}^{5}
of genus

g=\tfrac{(k-1)(k-2)}{2}
whose conformal type is that of the Fermat curve.

In particular, there are infinitely many new examples of minimal
Lagrangian surfaces in
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
, including infinitely many examples of
even genus. And when
g
is odd, the Fermat-curve symmetries show that our
examples are distinct from those of Haskins–Kapouleas
[
22
]
. In fact, the
areas of our examples grow on the order of
\sqrt{g}
, in contrast with
the linear growth forced by the handle-gluing construction of

[
22
]
. Moreover, the Haskins–Kapouleas construction requires small
neck sizes and hence conformal structures near the boundary of the moduli
space, whereas our surfaces have the conformal structure of the Fermat
curve itself.

Refining our implicit function theorem argument in the parameter
t=1/k
, we expect the threshold in the theorem above to be
k_{0}=3
,
corresponding to the Clifford torus in
\mathbb{S}^{5}
. This expectation is supported both by the analysis in
[
11
]
, which reconstructs Lawson’s
minimal surfaces
\xi_{1,g}
for all
g\geq 3
using Fuchsian systems, and by numerical experiments based on our construction. We also expect that our
Fuchsian system description will lead to power series expansions of the areas in
1/g
, with alternating multiple zeta values as
coefficients, analogous to those found in
[
11
]
.

Overview.

The construction of our surfaces is guided by a general philosophy familiar both from integrable surface geometry
[
33
,
14
,
26
]
and from the twistorial viewpoint on non-Abelian Hodge theory via
\lambda
-connections
[
52
,
53
]
: instead of attacking the nonlinear Gauss-Codazzi type equations directly, one encodes the geometry in a holomorphic family of flat connections depending on a spectral parameter.
For minimal Lagrangian surfaces in
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
, this leads to a cyclic Higgs bundle and an associated
\mathbb{Z}_{6}
-twisted family
of flat connections

(1.1)

d_{\lambda}=D+\lambda^{-1}\Phi-\lambda\Phi^{\dagger},\hskip 20.00003pt\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{\times},

whose reality and symmetry properties encode the minimal Lagrangian condition. The
\tau
-twisted formalism
developed in Section
3
is the natural loop-group framework for families of flat connections with cyclic Higgs data
[
1
,
40
]
: its eigenspace decomposition reflects the cyclic form of
\Phi
together with the additional symmetry coming from the Lagrangian condition. Conversely, such a family reconstructs the immersion once suitable intrinsic and extrinsic closing conditions are imposed.
The surface-theoretic and the basic gauge-theoretic background for this picture is explained in Section
2
.

Despite this formal similarity, the relevant gauge-theoretic equations are not Hitchin’s self-duality equations: the signed self-duality equations differ from Hitchin’s equations by the sign of the quadratic Higgs term. Thus the classical non-Abelian Hodge correspondence does not by itself produce the relevant harmonic map, or the closed minimal Lagrangian immersion, from the holomorphic Higgs data. Instead, the main task is to construct a
\lambda
-family of flat connections with prescribed local asymptotics and monodromy, satisfying the intrinsic unitarization condition for
\lambda\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
and the extrinsic closing condition at a distinguished
Sym point
. Once such a family has been constructed, one applies the DPW recipe
[
14
,
15
]
: loop-group Iwasawa factorization recovers the unitary associated family, and hence the harmonic map and the minimal Lagrangian surface itself.

In this sense, our construction behaves like a local non-Abelian Hodge correspondence for a compact symmetric target: once the implicit function theorem produces a unique meromorphic
\lambda
-family for a given Higgs field which satisfies the required monodromy properties, those properties already determine the
geometry. In the case at hand, the cyclic Higgs field essentially forces the
\mathbb{Z}_{6}
-twisted form of the resulting family of flat connections, and,
after solving the monodromy problem, leads to minimal Lagrangian surfaces—a phenomenon well known in higher Teichmüller theory

[
34
,
51
]
. What fails, in comparison with the classical non-Abelian Hodge correspondence, is that the signed self-duality equations do not arise from Hitchin’s equations, so the relevant harmonic metric is not obtained abstractly from the holomorphic Higgs data, but must instead be constructed indirectly through this monodromy problem. On the other hand, a complex analytic, monodromy-based approach to the non-Abelian Hodge correspondence was already suggested by Deligne and Simpson (see
[
52
]
), and has been partially implemented in certain situations
[
17
,
30
,
28
]
.

The existence problem of minimal Lagrangian surfaces is therefore reformulated
as a non-Abelian period (monodromy) problem. One seeks families of meromorphic
\SL_{3}(\mathbb{C})
-connections whose singularities become apparent on the relevant branched cover, whose monodromy is unitarizable for every
\lambda\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
, and whose monodromy at a distinguished Sym point satisfies the extrinsic closing condition.
For a general higher genus surface this is a difficult global problem,
governed by the Riemann–Hilbert map from the moduli space of flat connections into the

\SL_{3}(\mathbb{C})
-character variety.
We simplify this by imposing the symmetries of the Fermat curve

\Sigma_{k}=\{X^{k}+\zeta Y^{k}+\zeta^{2}Z^{k}=0\}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{2},\hskip 20.00003pt\zeta=\exp(\tfrac{2\pi i}{3}),

whose symmetry group

\Gamma_{sym}=\mathbb{Z}_{k}\times\mathbb{Z}_{k}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{3}

realizes
\Sigma_{k}
as a branched cover of
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
with three branch values. This reduces the global monodromy problem from a higher-genus surface to the relative
\SL_{3}(\mathbb{C})
-character variety of the thrice-punctured sphere

S=\mathbb{CP}^{1}\setminus\{0,1,\infty\}.

We therefore work on
S
and construct a
t
-dependent family, with
t=1/k
, of irreducible Fuchsian systems

D_{\lambda}^{t}=d+\eta_{\lambda}^{t}

with local conjugacy classes prescribed by the covering data. Using Lawton’s description of the relative character variety in trace coordinates
[
37
]
, we identify a unitary locus explicitly and solve the intrinsic unitarization condition, together with the extrinsic closing condition at a distinguished Sym point, by an implicit function theorem starting from explicit initial data at
t=0
. There are two suitable initial conditions, leading to two geometrically distinct families of
minimal Lagrangian surfaces of the same conformal type and with the same ambient symmetries. They can be distinguished by
their area, which can be computed in terms of the residues of the meromorphic connection data.
The character variety analysis and the implicit function theorem argument are carried out in Sections
4
and
5
, respectively.
After pulling back to
\Sigma_{k}
and removing the resulting apparent singularities, we obtain compact minimal Lagrangian immersions

f\colon\Sigma_{k}\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}

for all sufficiently large
k
, carrying the symmetries of the Fermat curve.
These surfaces and their basic geometric properties are studied in Section
6
.

It is worth noting that our construction completely avoids delicate gluing techniques. We exploit the prescribed symmetries of the desired surfaces to construct meromorphic loop Weierstrass data from which the surfaces are then derived. This approach is very much in the spirit of classical minimal surface theory, where one also uses a priori knowledge of symmetries and conformal type to determine the Weierstrass data. Integration of these data in the classical case leads to an Abelian period closing problem which, in our setting, becomes a non-Abelian unitarization problem for representations into the twisted loop group
{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
.

The final part of the paper is devoted to the study of the special Legendrian lifts of the minimal Lagrangian surfaces constructed above. Since topological obstructions prevent higher-genus oriented Lagrangian surfaces from being embedded in
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
, embeddedness can only be expected after passing to the Legendrian lift in
\mathbb{S}^{5}
. We show that for one of the two families, and for all sufficiently large
k
, the corresponding special Legendrian lifts

\hat{f}_{k}\colon\Sigma_{k}\to\mathbb{S}^{5}

are embedded.

Our embeddedness arguments rely on an explicit asymptotic analysis as
t=1/k\to 0
, equivalently as
g\to\infty
, of solutions to the Fuchsian equation

d\Psi_{\lambda}^{t}+\eta_{\lambda}^{t}\Psi_{\lambda}^{t}=0

on the intermediate quotient

\Sigma_{k}/(\mathbb{Z}_{k}\times\mathbb{Z}_{k})\cong\mathbb{CP}^{1},

punctured at the three branch values
\{1,\zeta,\zeta^{2}\}
. Expanding the solutions by iterated integrals, and carrying out the corresponding loop Iwasawa factorization to sufficiently high order, yields precise asymptotic control of the unitary frame. At a distinguished Sym point, this frame closes on
\Sigma_{k}
and gives the special Legendrian lift.
Two asymptotic regimes appear. Away from the branch values of

\Sigma_{k}/(\mathbb{Z}_{k}\times\mathbb{Z}_{k})
, the blow-up limit is an embedded Scherk-type minimal Lagrangian surface in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
, identified with a horizontal tangent space of the Hopf fibration
\mathbb{S}^{5}\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
.
This blow-up occurs at each point
q\in\mathbb{S}^{5}
which lies on the Cliffod torus. Near each of the three branch values of our covering, the surfaces converge locally to special Legendrian spherical caps. The main difficulty is to control uniformly the transition between these two
regions. This is achieved by uniform first-order estimates for the frame in
t
, and it yields embeddedness for all sufficiently large
k
.
The asymptotic analysis and the embeddedness proof are carried out in Section
7
.

2.
The fundamental equations for minimal Lagrangian surfaces

2.1.
Minimal Lagrangian cones in
\mathbb{C}^{3}
, special Legendrian surfaces in
\mathbb{S}^{5}
, and minimal Lagrangian surfaces in
\mathbb{CP}^{2}

For background on calibrations, special Lagrangian geometry, and Legendrian geometry, see
[
20
,
35
,
43
]
. We restrict attention to the three-dimensional case and follow the exposition in
[
21
,
22
]
.

Consider
\mathbb{C}^{3}
with complex coordinates
z=(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})

equipped with the standard Euclidean metric. The corresponding Kähler form is

\omega_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}=\tfrac{i}{2}\sum_{j}dz_{j}\wedge d\bar{z}_{j}=\sum_{j}dx_{j}\wedge dy_{j}

where

z_{j}=x_{j}+iy_{j}
, and the holomorphic volume form is

\Omega_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}=dz_{1}\wedge dz_{2}\wedge dz_{3}.

The real 3-form
\operatorname{Re}\Omega_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}
is a calibration on
\mathbb{C}^{3}
, that is,
\operatorname{Re}\Omega_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}
is closed and for every oriented real 3-dimensional subspace
W\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}=T_{z}\mathbb{C}^{3}
one has

(\operatorname{Re}\Omega_{\mathbb{C}^{3}})|_{W}\leq\det_{W}

where
\det_{W}\in\Lambda^{3}W^{*}
is the oriented volume form induced from the Euclidean metric. An immersion
f\colon N^{3}\to\mathbb{C}^{3}
of a connected, oriented real 3-manifold
N
is
special Lagrangian
if it is Lagrangian, that is
f^{*}\omega_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}=0
, and
f^{*}\operatorname{Im}\Omega_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}=0
. This is equivalent
[
20
]
to

f^{*}\Omega_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}={\det}_{N}

with
\det_{N}
the volume form of the induced metric on
N
.
Thus
f^{*}\operatorname{Re}\Omega_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}=\det_{N}
, so
f
is calibrated by
\operatorname{Re}\Omega_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}
and hence is minimal.

On the other hand, if
f\colon N\to\mathbb{C}^{3}
is a Lagrangian immersion then
f^{*}\Omega_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}=\varphi\det_{N}
for a phase
\varphi\colon N\to\mathbb{S}^{1}\subset\mathbb{C}
. Now

i\,d\log\varphi=\omega_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}(H,df)

by
[
20
]
with
H
the mean curvature of
f
and thus the Lagrangian immersion
f\colon N\to\mathbb{C}^{3}
is minimal if and only if its phase
\varphi
is constant. Therefore, up to a
\mathrm{U}_{3}
-transformation, a minimal Lagrangian immersion
f\colon N\to\mathbb{C}^{3}
is special Lagrangian.

Now consider the cone
C\colon(0,\infty)\times\Sigma\to\mathbb{C}^{3}

over an immersed surface
\hat{f}\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{S}^{5}
.
Then
C
is Lagrangian if and only if
\hat{f}
is Legendrian, that is,
\hat{f}^{*}\alpha=0
, where

\alpha=\omega_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}(X,\cdot)\big|_{\mathbb{S}^{5}}

is the standard contact form on
\mathbb{S}^{5}
and

X=\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{j}\left(x_{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}+y_{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{j}}\right)

is the Liouville vector field on
\mathbb{C}^{3}
.
In this case the phase
\varphi\colon(0,\infty)\times\Sigma\to\mathbb{S}^{1}
only depends on
\Sigma
, and
\varphi\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{S}^{1}
satisfies

(2.1)

\hat{f}^{*}(\Omega_{\mathbb{C}^{3}}(X,\cdot))=\varphi\,{\det}_{\Sigma}\,.

As in the Lagrangian case, the Legendrian immersion
\hat{f}\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{S}^{5}
is minimal if and only if
\varphi
is constant.
Therefore, the link in
\mathbb{S}^{5}
of a special Lagrangian cone in
\mathbb{C}^{3}
is special Legendrian, and conversely the cone over a special Legendrian surface is special Lagrangian.

The Hopf fibration
\pi\colon\mathbb{S}^{5}\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
can be seen as the unit circle bundle in the tautological line bundle
L\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
. Its connection 1-form is the contact structure
\alpha
on
\mathbb{S}^{5}
with curvature a multiple of the Kähler form
\omega
of
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
.
In particular, parallel unit length sections of
L
take values in the horizontal subbundle
\ker\alpha\subset T\mathbb{S}^{5}
. Therefore, a Legendrian immersion
\hat{f}\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{S}^{5}
projects via
\pi
to a Lagrangian immersion
f\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
. Since the Hopf fibration
\pi\colon\mathbb{S}^{5}\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
is a Riemannian submersion, minimal Legendrians in
\mathbb{S}^{5}
project to minimal Lagrangians in
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
.

Lifting a Lagrangian immersion
f\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
via
\pi
is somewhat more subtle: being Lagrangian,
f^{*}\omega=0
implies that the pullback of the tautological bundle, again denoted by
L\to\Sigma
, is a flat Hermitian line bundle.
Prescribing a unit-length vector at some fiber defines, via parallel transport, a unique Legendrian immersed lift
\tilde{f}\colon\tilde{\Sigma}\to\mathbb{S}^{5}
on the universal cover of
\Sigma
. If one additionally assumes
f
to be minimal, then
L\to\Sigma
satisfies
L^{3}=\underline{\mathbb{C}}
and thus the Legendrian lift of a minimal Lagrangian immersion closes on at most a threefold cover

\hat{\Sigma}
of
\Sigma
. To summarize:

Proposition 2.1
.

Minimal Legendrian immersions, and in particular special Legendrian immersions,

\hat{f}\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{S}^{5}
project via the Hopf fibration
\pi\colon\mathbb{S}^{5}\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
to a minimal Lagrangian immersion
f\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
. Conversely, a minimal Lagrangian immersion
f\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}

horizontally lifts, on at most a threefold cover
\hat{\Sigma}
, to a minimal, not necessarily special, Legendrian immersion
\hat{f}\colon\hat{\Sigma}\to\mathbb{S}^{5}
. Choosing the initial condition for the lift appropriately, one can arrange for
\hat{f}
to be special Legendrian.

2.2.
The fundamental equations of minimal Lagrangian surfaces in
\mathbb{CP}^{2}

Let
L\subset\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{3}
denote the tautological line bundle over the complex projective plane
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
.
Then the trivial bundle

\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{3}=L\oplus L^{\perp}

splits as an orthogonal sum with respect to the standard Hermitian product
\langle\,,\,\rangle
on
\mathbb{C}^{3}
, which we assume to be sesquilinear in the first entry. In this decomposition, the trivial connection has the form

(2.2)

d=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla&-\delta^{\dagger}\\
\delta&\nabla^{\perp}\end{pmatrix}

with
\nabla
,
\nabla^{\perp}
Hermitian connections on
L
,
L^{\perp}
and

\displaystyle\delta\colon T\mathbb{CP}^{2}

\displaystyle\to\Hom(L,L^{\perp}),\hskip 10.00002ptv\mapsto(d_{v})^{\perp}

the bundle isomorphism identifying the tangent bundle of
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
with the bundle
\Hom(L,L^{\perp})
. The Kähler structure of
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
is given by the complex structure
J(v)=iv
and the Hermitian metric

(2.3)

h_{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}(v,w)=\tr({v^{\dagger}w}),\hskip 20.00003ptv,w\in T\mathbb{CP}^{2}=\Hom(L,L^{\perp})\,.

The real and imaginary parts

h_{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}=g_{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}+i\,\omega

yield the Fubini-Study metric
g_{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}
and the Kähler 2-form
\omega
. The Levi-Civita connection of
g_{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}
is the connection
\nabla^{*}\otimes\nabla^{\perp}
on
\Hom(L,L^{\perp})
.

A map
f\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
from a Riemann surface
\Sigma
corresponds—via pullback of the tautological bundle over
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
—to a line subbundle
L\subset\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{3}
of the trivial bundle over
\Sigma
. Then
df=f^{*}\delta
is the derivative of
f
and we have the type decomposition

(2.4)

\begin{gathered}df=\partial f+\bar{\partial}f\in\Gamma(K\Hom(L,L^{\perp}))\oplus\Gamma(\bar{K}\Hom(L,L^{\perp}))\,,\\
\partial f=\tfrac{1}{2}(df-i*df)\,,\qquad\bar{\partial}f=\tfrac{1}{2}(df+i*df)\,.\end{gathered}

Here
K
,
\bar{K}
denote the canonical and anti-canonical line bundles of
\Sigma
and

*=(J_{\Sigma})^{*}\colon T\Sigma^{*}\to T\Sigma^{*}
is the complex structure of
\Sigma
.
The Hermitian connections
\nabla
,
\nabla^{\perp}
induce – via type decomposition – holomorphic and anti-holomorphic structures on the bundles
L
,
L^{\perp}
. We have the following characterizations:

(2.5a)

f
is (branched) conformal if and only if
h_{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}(\bar{\partial}f,\partial f)=0
, in which case

2|\partial f|^{2}=|df|^{2}+\omega(df,*df)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt2|\bar{\partial}f|^{2}=|df|^{2}-\omega(df,*df)\,;

(2.5b)

f
is Lagrangian, that is
f^{*}\omega=0
, if and only if
|\partial f|^{2}=|\bar{\partial}f|^{2}
;

(2.5c)

f
is (branched) conformal and Lagrangian if and only if
|\partial f|^{2}=|\bar{\partial}f|^{2}=\tfrac{1}{2}|df|^{2}
.

Here
|\alpha|^{2}=g_{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}(\alpha,\alpha)\in\Gamma(|K|^{2})
denotes the
quadratic form
|\alpha|^{2}_{v}=g_{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}(\alpha(v),\alpha(v))
,
v\in T\Sigma
, induced via
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(\Sigma,f^{*}T\mathbb{CP}^{2})
.

The following properties of harmonic maps into
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
are well-documented in the literature
[
4
,
3
]
:

(1)

A map
f\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
is
harmonic
if and only if
\partial f\in\Gamma(K\Hom(L,L^{\perp}))
is holomorphic, equivalently,

\bar{\partial}f\in\Gamma(\bar{K}\Hom(L,L^{\perp}))
is anti-holomorphic. If neither of those bundle maps is trivial, that is,
f
is neither anti-holomorphic nor holomorphic, then there exist unique line subbundles
L_{\pm}\subset L^{\perp}
with
\partial f\in\Gamma(K\Hom(L,L_{+}))
and
\bar{\partial}f\in\Gamma(\bar{K}\Hom(L,L_{-}))
. The corresponding maps
f_{\pm}\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
are again harmonic and
\bar{\partial}f=-(\partial f_{-})^{\dagger}
,
\bar{\partial}f_{+}=-(\partial f)^{\dagger}
.

(2)

For a harmonic map
f\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
, the quadratic Hopf differential

Q_{2}=(f^{*}g_{\mathbb{CP}^{2}})^{2,0}=h_{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}(\bar{\partial}f,\partial f)\in H^{0}(K^{2})

is holomorphic. By (
2.5a
), its vanishing
Q_{2}\equiv 0
characterizes the conformality of the harmonic map
f\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
, which thus becomes a branched minimal immersion. Assuming that
f
is neither holomorphic nor anti-holomorphic, we have the orthogonal splitting

\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{3}=L_{-}\oplus L\oplus L_{+}

of the trivial
\mathbb{C}^{3}
-bundle over
\Sigma
. The derivatives of the harmonic maps
f_{\pm},f
give rise to the harmonic sequence

(2.6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{-}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces KL}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{2}L_{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{3}L_{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial f_{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial f_{+}}

and the cubic differential

(2.7)

Q=-h_{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}(\bar{\partial}f,\partial f_{+}\partial f)=\tr(\partial f_{+}\partial f\partial f_{-})\in H^{0}(K^{3})

is holomorphic. Either
Q\equiv 0
, in which case
f_{+}
is anti-holomorphic and the sequence (
2.6
) terminates – the
superminimal

case
[
4
]
– or
Q\not\equiv 0
, and the sequence (
2.6
) is periodic with period three – the
superconformal
case
[
3
]
.
Since the main focus of this paper is on minimal Lagrangian immersions

f\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
from a Riemann surface
\Sigma
, we collect the basic characteristics of their harmonic sequences (
2.6
).

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
f\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
be a minimal Lagrangian immersion. Then the following properties hold:

(i)

The holomorphic cubic differential (
2.7
) vanishes identically if and only if
f(\Sigma)\subset\mathbb{RP}^{2}
. If
\Sigma
is compact and of nonzero genus, the minimal Lagrangian immersion
f
is superconformal,
Q\not\equiv 0
, and the harmonic sequence (
2.6
) is periodic.

(ii)

The quadratic forms

\tfrac{1}{2}|df|^{2}=|\partial f|^{2}=|\bar{\partial}f|^{2}=|\partial f_{-}|^{2}=|\bar{\partial}f_{+}|^{2}

are nowhere vanishing.

(iii)

The bundle maps

(2.8)

\partial f_{-}\colon L_{-}\cong KL\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\partial f\colon L\cong KL_{+}

are holomorphic isometries if the Hermitian metric on
K
comes from the induced metric
\tfrac{1}{2}f^{*}g_{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}
on
\Sigma
.

(iv)

The zeros of
Q
are the same as the zeros of
\partial f_{+}
,
i.e. the divisors
(Q)=(\partial f_{+})
agree.

Proof.

As shown in
[
41
]
, a minimal Lagrangian immersion
f
has
Q=ig_{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}(S,Jdf)^{(3,0)}
, where
S
denotes the normal bundle valued second fundamental form of
f
. Since
g_{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}(S,Jdf)^{(2,1)}=0
by minimality,
Q\equiv 0
is equivalent to the vanishing of the symmetric 3-form
g_{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}(S,Jdf)=0
. The Lagrangian condition then implies
S\equiv 0
and the map
f
is totally geodesic which shows (i).

The relations
\tfrac{1}{2}|df|^{2}=|\partial f|^{2}=|\bar{\partial}f|^{2}
follow from (
2.5b
), (
2.5c
). Furthermore,

\bar{\partial}f_{+}=-(\partial f)^{\dagger}
and
\partial f_{-}=-(\bar{\partial}f)^{\dagger}
, which provide the remaining identities in (ii).
Since
f
is assumed to be immersed,
|df|^{2}
is nowhere vanishing.

Item (iii) follows from (ii), and item (iv) follows from

Q=\tr(\partial f_{+}\partial f\partial f_{-})

together with (iii).
∎

The holomorphic isomorphisms (
2.8
) identify
L_{-}\cong KL
and
L_{+}\cong K^{-1}L
so that the orthogonal decomposition

(2.9)

\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{3}=L_{-}\oplus L\oplus L_{+}

gives rise to the trivial rank
3
bundle

(2.10)

V=KL\oplus L\oplus K^{-1}L\,.

Via these identifications, the Hermitian metric on
V
is diagonal: the metrics on
KL
and
K^{-1}L
are the product metrics coming from the Hermitian metrics on
K
and
K^{-1}
, respectively, induced by
\tfrac{1}{2}f^{*}g_{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}

1
1

1

The factor
1/2
is due to item (ii) of Lemma
2.2
.
, together with the induced Hermitian metric on
L\subset\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{3}
.
The Hermitian connections on
L_{\pm}
hence become
\nabla_{\pm}=\nabla^{K^{\mp 1}}\otimes\nabla
with
\nabla^{K^{\pm 1}}
induced by the Levi-Civita connection of
\tfrac{1}{2}f^{*}g_{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}
on
\Sigma
and
\nabla
is the Hermitian connection on
L
induced via the decomposition (
2.9
).

We can now reformulate and refine the decomposition (
2.2
) of the flat connection
d
on the trivial

\mathbb{C}^{3}
-bundle over
\Sigma
in the orthogonal splitting (
2.10
) of
V
as

(2.11)

d_{V}=D+\Phi-\Phi^{\dagger}\,.

Here
D=\nabla_{-}\oplus\nabla\oplus\nabla_{+}
is the Hermitian diagonal connection of the splitting (
2.10
) and

(2.12)

\Phi=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&Q\\
{\bf 1}&0&0\\
0&{\bf 1}&0\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma(\Sigma,K{\mathfrak{sl}}(V))

is a cyclic Higgs field, see
[
1
,
40
]
. Note that due to (
2.10
), the entry
{\bf 1}\in\mathbb{C}
is the constant section of the trivial bundle
\underline{\mathbb{C}}=K\Hom(KL,L)=K\Hom(L,K^{-1}L)
and the entry
Q\in H^{0}(K^{3})
is the holomorphic cubic differential (
2.7
) since
K^{3}=K\Hom(K^{-1}L,KL)
. The adjoint of the Higgs field
\Phi
, with respect to the splitting (
2.10
), is given by

(2.13)

\Phi^{\dagger}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\tfrac{1}{2}h&0\\
0&0&\tfrac{1}{2}h\\
\tfrac{4\bar{Q}}{h^{2}}&0&0\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma(\Sigma,\bar{K}{\mathfrak{sl}}(V))\,,

where we view the conformal metric
h=|df|^{2}\in\Gamma(\Sigma,\bar{K}K)
on
\Sigma
as a quadratic form, i.e. as a section of

|K|^{2}=\bar{K}\Hom(L,KL)=\bar{K}\Hom(K^{-1}L,L).

Similarly,
4\bar{Q}h^{-2}
is a section of
\bar{K}^{3}(K\bar{K})^{-2}=\bar{K}\Hom(KL,K^{-1}L)
as required.
From (
2.12
) and (
2.13
) we see that branch points of
f
are characterized by the vanishing of
\Phi^{2}
. Since
f
is assumed to be immersed,
\Phi^{2}\in\Gamma(K^{2}\mathfrak{sl}(V))
is nowhere vanishing.

The zero curvature equation for the connection
d_{V}
splits into diagonal and off-diagonal parts

(2.14)

F^{D}=[\Phi\wedge\Phi^{\dagger}]\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptd^{D}(\Phi-\Phi^{\dagger})=0\,.

The holomorphicity of the cubic differential
Q
is equivalent to the holomorphicity of the Higgs field,
\bar{\partial}^{\,D}\Phi=0
. The latter implies the off-diagonal part of the zero curvature equation since the diagonal connection
D
is Hermitian. Thus a minimal Lagrangian immersion
f\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
gives rise to a solution to a special reduction of the
signed self-duality equations

(2.15)

F^{D}=[\Phi\wedge\Phi^{\dagger}]\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\bar{\partial}^{\,D}\Phi=0\,

for the group
\SU_{3}
.
Despite their formal similarity, these equations are not Hitchin’s self-duality equations
[
32
]
due to the opposite sign in the quadratic term of the first equation.
To unravel the first equation of (
2.15
), we identify 2-forms with quadratic forms via

(2.16)

\alpha\wedge\beta=\alpha(*\beta)-(*\alpha)\beta

for 1-forms
\alpha,\beta
on
\Sigma
. In particular, under this prescription the area 2-form of a conformal metric on
\Sigma
gets identified with the quadratic form of the metric. We thus obtain

(2.17)

[\Phi\wedge\Phi^{\dagger}]=(-2i)[\Phi,\Phi^{\dagger}]=i\bigl((|df|^{2}-8|Q|^{2}|df|^{-4})\oplus 0\oplus(-|df|^{2}+8|Q|^{2}|df|^{-4})\bigr)

and the diagonal connection
D
has curvature

F^{D}=F^{\nabla_{-}}\oplus F^{\nabla}\oplus F^{\nabla_{+}}\,.

Hence the first equation of (
2.15
) is equivalent to

(2.18)

F^{\nabla}=0\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptF^{\nabla^{K}}=i(|df|^{2}-8|Q|^{2}|df|^{-4})\,,

where we used that
F^{\nabla^{K^{-1}}}=-F^{\nabla^{K}}
and
F^{\nabla_{-}}=F^{\nabla{{}^{K}}\otimes\nabla}=F^{\nabla^{K}}
since
F^{\nabla}=0
.
The curvature of the Hermitian line bundle
K
is
F^{\nabla^{K}}=iG|df|^{2}
, where
G
denotes the Gauss curvature of the induced metric
f^{*}g
.
We obtain

G+|Q|^{2}_{f^{*}g}-1=0\,.

A more familiar form of this equation may be obtained by expressing the induced metric
f^{*}g_{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}=e^{2u}g_{0}
in a fixed conformal background metric
g_{0}
on
\Sigma
. Then
G=(-\triangle_{g_{0}}u+G_{g_{0}})e^{-2u}
and the first equation of (
2.15
) becomes the well-known (signed)
Tzitzéica equation

(2.19)

\triangle_{g_{0}}u-|Q|^{2}_{g_{0}}e^{-4u}+e^{2u}-G_{g_{0}}=0

for the conformal factor
u\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{R}
.

It is worth pointing out that the flatness of
\nabla
on
L
should not come as a surprise: the Lagrangian condition
f^{*}\omega=0
is equivalent to the vanishing of the curvature of the pullback, via
f\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
, of the
dual tautological bundle over
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
. Moreover, by construction of
V
its determinant bundle
\Lambda^{3}V
is trivial as a line bundle with Hermitian connection and therefore

(2.20)

L^{3}=\Lambda^{3}V=\underline{\mathbb{C}}

is trivial. We conclude that a Lagrangian minimal immersion
f\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
yields a 3-torsion point in the Jacobian of
\Sigma
.
The special Legendrian lift to
\mathbb{S}^{5}
is then given by a parallel section of unit length with specific phase, unique up to multiplication with
\exp(\pm\tfrac{2\pi i}{3})
.
Geometrically this means (as in Proposition
2.1
) that in general one has to pass to a threefold cover
\hat{\Sigma}\to\Sigma
in order for the Legendrian lift
\hat{f}\colon\hat{\Sigma}\to\mathbb{S}^{5}\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}
to close, since
\hat{f}
is given as a unit length parallel section of
L\subset\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{3}
with its induced Hermitian structure.

Lastly, the induced area of a minimal Lagrangian immersion
f\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
can be calculated in terms of the Higgs field
\Phi
. Since
\tr\Phi\wedge\Phi^{\dagger}=\tfrac{1}{2}\tr[\Phi\wedge\Phi^{\dagger}]
, equations (
2.17
) and (
2.18
) combine to

(2.21)

3|df|^{2}=i\,\tr\,\Phi\wedge\Phi^{\dagger}-iF^{\nabla^{K}}.

The line bundle decomposition (
2.9
) defines a lift
\psi\colon\Sigma\to\SU_{3}/T
into the full flag variety (which happens to be a primitive harmonic map, see
[
5
]
). The energy density of
\psi
calculates to

(2.22)

\tfrac{1}{2}\langle d\psi\wedge*d\psi\rangle=i\,\tr\,\Phi\wedge\Phi^{\dagger}

where
\langle\,\,,\,\rangle
is the invariant Riemannian metric on
\SU_{3}/T
coming from the trace form on
\mathfrak{su}_{3}
. If
\Sigma
is compact, we thus obtain

(2.23)

\mathrm{Area}(f)=\tfrac{2\pi}{3}(\tfrac{1}{2\pi}E(\psi)+(2-2g))

for the induced area of a minimal Lagrangian immersion
f\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
.

2.3.
The fundamental theorem for minimal Lagrangian surfaces

Our construction of higher genus minimal Lagrangian surfaces is based on solving the signed self-duality equations (
2.15
).
This requires one to reconstruct a minimal Lagrangian surface
f\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
from the
Gauss-Codazzi
data
(h,Q)
, where
h\in\Gamma(\Sigma,\bar{K}K)
is a conformal metric, and
Q
is a holomorphic cubic differential.

We begin by choosing a 3-torsion point
L\in\Jac(\Sigma)
in the Jacobian of
\Sigma
and consider the rank three holomorphic bundle

V=KL\oplus L\oplus K^{-1}L

which has trivial determinant line bundle
\Lambda^{3}V=L^{3}=\underline{\mathbb{C}}
over
\Sigma
. The holomorphic line bundle
L
has a unique (up to constant scaling) Hermitian metric
h_{L}
with flat Chern connection
\nabla
. We equip the line bundles
K
,
K^{-1}
with the Hermitian structures given by the conformal metric
h
so that their Hermitian connections
\nabla^{K^{\pm 1}}
arise from the Levi-Civita connection of
h
. Thus,
V
obtains the structure of a Hermitian rank three bundle with diagonal connection
D=(\nabla^{K}\otimes\nabla)\,\oplus\nabla\oplus\,(\nabla^{K^{-1}}\otimes\nabla)
compatible with its holomorphic structure, in other words,
D
is the Chern connection for the diagonal Hermitian metric
h^{-1}\oplus h_{L}\oplus h
on
V
.
Furthermore, consider the holomorphic cyclic Higgs field
\Phi
as in (
2.12
), which is uniquely determined by
Q
,
and its adjoint
\Phi^{\dagger}
with respect to the diagonal Hermitian metric. It is given by (
2.13
)
Then the Hermitian connection

d_{V}=D+\Phi-\Phi^{\dagger}

is flat if and only if the pair
(D,\Phi)
solves the signed self-duality equations (
2.15
).

Let

\Upsilon\colon(V,d_{V})\to(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{3},d)

be the trivialization of the flat Hermitian connection
d_{V}
on the universal cover
\tilde{\Sigma}\to\Sigma
. In other words,
\Upsilon
satisfies
d.\Upsilon=d_{V}
on
\tilde{\Sigma}
, and
\Upsilon
is equivariant

(2.24)

\gamma^{*}\Upsilon=\rho_{\gamma}^{-1}\Upsilon

with respect to the right action of deck transformations
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p_{0})
on
\tilde{\Sigma}
and the monodromy representation
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p_{0})\to\SU_{3}
of
d_{V}
.
Then the image under
\Upsilon
of the pullback
\tilde{L}
of
L\subset V
to
\tilde{\Sigma}
defines a minimal Lagrangian immersion

(2.25)

f\colon\tilde{\Sigma}\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}\,,\hskip 10.00002ptf(p)=\Upsilon_{p}(\tilde{L}_{p})\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}

with cubic holomorphic differential
Q\in H^{0}(K^{3})
. The equivariance of
\Upsilon
implies that
f
has the same equivariance property

(2.26)

\gamma^{*}f=\rho_{\gamma}^{-1}f

with respect to the monodromy representation
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\SU_{3}
of
d_{V}
.

The immersion
f\colon\tilde{\Sigma}\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
descends to
\Sigma
if and only if the monodromy representation
\rho
of the flat connection
d_{V}
is projectively trivial, that is
\rho=c\,\Id
is a multiple of the trivial representation. The Abelian representation
c\colon\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\mathbb{S}^{1}
has to satisfy
c^{3}=1
due to
L^{3}=\underline{\mathbb{C}}
. We will utilize this projective freedom and assume for the remainder of the paper that
L=\underline{\mathbb{C}}
is the trivial bundle. We summarize the discussion so far in the form of a “fundamental theorem for minimal Lagrangian surfaces”.

Theorem 1
.

Let
\Sigma
be a Riemann surface with a conformal metric
h
,
and let
Q\in\Gamma(K^{3})
be a
cubic differential. Consider the holomorphic rank three bundle

V=K\oplus\underline{\mathbb{C}}\oplus K^{-1}

over
\Sigma
equipped with the diagonal Hermitian metric
h^{-1}\oplus h_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus h
, where
h_{\mathbb{C}}
denotes the standard Hermitian structure on
\mathbb{C}
, and diagonal Hermitian connection
D=\nabla^{K}\oplus d_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus\nabla^{K^{-1}}
, which is the Chern connection for the holomorphic and Hermitian structures on
V
. Let
\Phi\in\Gamma(K{\mathfrak{sl}}(V))
and its adjoint
\Phi^{\dagger}\in\Gamma(\bar{K}{\mathfrak{sl}}(V))
be defined as in (
2.12
) and (
2.13
). Then we have the following equivalent statements:

(i)

f\colon\tilde{\Sigma}\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
is a minimal Lagrangian immersion with induced metric
\pi^{*}h
and holomorphic cubic differential
\pi^{*}Q\in H^{0}(K^{3})
on the universal covering
\pi\colon\tilde{\Sigma}\to\Sigma
equivariant
with respect to a representation
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\SU_{3}
, that is,
\gamma^{*}f=\rho_{\gamma}^{-1}f
.

(ii)

The Hermitian connection
d_{V}=D+\Phi-\Phi^{\dagger}
on
V\to\Sigma
satisfies the signed self-duality equations (
2.15
)—in particular is flat—with monodromy representation
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\SU_{3}
.

The minimal Lagrangian immersion satisfies the extrinsic closing condition, in other words, descends to a minimal Lagrangian immersion
f\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
if and only if
\rho=c\,\Id
with the Abelian representation
c\colon\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\mathbb{S}^{1}
satisfying
c^{3}=1
.
Furthermore,
c
is trivial, equivalently
c\equiv 1
, if and only if the minimal Lagrangian immersion
f\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
lifts to a minimal Legendrian immersion
\hat{f}\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{S}^{5}
.

2.4.
The associated family of flat connections

Our approach for solving the signed self-duality equations (
2.15
) is based on the well-known observation that
the signed self-duality equations are equivalent to the flatness of a
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-family of connections. These connections enjoy certain symmetry properties which in turn characterize them.

As in Theorem
1
, we work on the bundle
V=K\oplus\underline{\mathbb{C}}\oplus K^{-1}
over a Riemann surface
\Sigma
with conformal metric
h
and cubic differential
Q\in\Gamma(K^{3})
. These data equip
V
with a Hermitian bundle metric, the Chern connection
D
, and the Higgs field
\Phi\in\Gamma(K{\mathfrak{sl}}(V))
together with its adjoint
\Phi^{\dagger}\in\Gamma(\bar{K}{\mathfrak{sl}}(V))
as given in
(
2.12
) and (
2.13
).

Lemma 2.3
.

The

\SU_{3}
-connection
d=D+\Phi-\Phi^{\dagger}
on
V
solves the signed self-duality equations (
2.15
) if and only if the
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-family of
{SL}_{3}(\mathbb{C})
-connections

(2.27)

d_{\lambda}=D+\lambda^{-1}\Phi-\lambda\Phi^{\dagger}

on
V
is flat for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.

The family of flat connections
d_{\lambda}
has the following properties:

(i)

d_{1/\bar{\lambda}}=d_{\lambda}^{\dagger}
, in other words,
d_{\lambda}
is an
\SU_{3}
-connection for unimodular
\lambda\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
.

(ii)

d_{\lambda}
has a simple pole at
\lambda=0
with residue
\Res_{0}d_{\lambda}=\Phi\in\Gamma(K{\mathfrak{sl}}(V))
. By (i) this is equivalent to
d_{\lambda}
having a simple pole at
\lambda=\infty
with
\Res_{\infty}d_{\lambda}=-\Phi^{\dagger}\in\Gamma(\bar{K}{\mathfrak{sl}}(V))
.

(iii)

\Phi^{2}\in\Gamma(K^{2}{\mathfrak{sl}}(V))
is nowhere vanishing.

(iv)

d_{\zeta\lambda}=d_{\lambda}.\diag(\zeta^{2},1,\zeta)
for the cube root of unity
\zeta=\exp(2\pi i/3)
.

(v)

d_{-\lambda}=d_{\lambda}^{*}.b
for the orthogonal structure
b\colon V\to V^{*}
,
b^{*}=b
given by

b=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&{1}\\
0&{1}&0\\
{1}&0&0\end{pmatrix}
.

For unimodular
\lambda\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
the flat connection
d_{\lambda}
defines a minimal Lagrangian immersion
f_{\lambda}\colon\tilde{\Sigma}\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
on the universal cover
\pi\colon\tilde{\Sigma}\to\Sigma
equivariant (
2.26
) with respect to the monodromy representation
\rho_{\lambda}\colon\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\SU_{3}
of
d_{\lambda}
. The immersion
f_{\lambda}
induces the metric
\pi^{*}h
and holomorphic cubic differential
\lambda^{-3}\pi^{*}Q
. In particular,
\rho_{\lambda_{0}}=c\,\Id
for
\lambda_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
is projectively trivial with
c\colon\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\mathbb{S}^{1}
satisfying
c^{3}=1
, if and only if the corresponding minimal Lagrangian immersion
f_{\lambda_{0}}\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
is defined on
\Sigma
(rather than just on the universal cover
\tilde{\Sigma}
). Furthermore,
\rho_{\lambda_{0}}=\Id
if and only if the minimal Lagrangian immersion
f_{\lambda_{0}}\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
lifts to a minimal Legendrian immersion
\hat{f}_{\lambda_{0}}\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{S}^{5}
.

Proof.

The first assertion follows by expanding the flatness condition of
d_{\lambda}
in powers of
\lambda
. Properties (i)-(iii) are seen by inspection and (iv) follows from a direct calculation. For property (v) note that
V^{*}=K^{-1}\oplus\underline{\mathbb{C}}\oplus K
so that the orthogonal structure
b
is well-defined with
{\bf 1}
denoting the identity maps on the respective line bundles. But
d_{\lambda}^{*}=D^{*}-\lambda^{-1}\Phi^{*}+\lambda(\Phi^{\dagger})^{*}
with

\Phi^{*}=\begin{pmatrix}0&{\bf 1}&0\\
0&0&{\bf 1}\\
Q&0&0\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma(K{\mathfrak{sl}}(V^{*}))
and likewise for
(\Phi^{\dagger})^{*}
, whence we have (v).

The remaining statements
follow from the discussion leading up to Theorem
1
: the flat connection
d_{\lambda}
, for unimodular
\lambda\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
, can be trivialized by an isometric bundle isomorphism

(2.28)

\Upsilon_{\lambda}\colon(V,d_{\lambda})\to(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{3},d)

on the universal cover
\tilde{\Sigma}
giving rise, via (
2.25
), to the immersion

(2.29)

f_{\lambda}=\Upsilon_{\lambda}(\underline{\mathbb{C}})\colon\tilde{\Sigma}\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}\,

equivariant with respect to the monodromy representation
\rho_{\lambda}\colon\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\SU_{3}
of
d_{\lambda}
on
V
.
But
d_{\lambda}=\tilde{d}_{\lambda}.\diag(\lambda^{-1},1,\lambda)
is gauge equivalent to

\tilde{d}_{\lambda}=D+\tilde{\Phi}-\tilde{\Phi}^{\dagger}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\tilde{\Phi}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&\lambda^{-3}Q\\
{\bf 1}&0&0\\
0&{\bf 1}&0\end{pmatrix}

via the
\SU_{3}
-gauge
\diag(\lambda^{-1},1,\lambda)
which leaves
f_{\lambda}=\Upsilon_{\lambda}(\underline{\mathbb{C}})
unchanged.
Theorem
1
then implies that
f_{\lambda}
is a minimal Lagrangian immersion with induced metric
\pi^{*}h
and cubic differential
\lambda^{-3}\pi^{*}Q
. The condition for
f_{\lambda_{0}}
to be defined on
\Sigma
(rather than on
\tilde{\Sigma}
), namely
\rho_{\lambda_{0}}=c\,\Id
with

c\colon\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\mathbb{S}^{1}
satisfying
c^{3}=1
, follows from the last part of Theorem
1
as does the assertion about the minimal Legendrian lift into
\mathbb{S}^{5}
in the case
c=1
.
∎

The next lemma shows that any
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-family of flat connections
d_{\lambda}
satisfying properties (i)-(v) on a Hermitian rank three bundle
V\to\Sigma
with trivial determinant
\Lambda^{3}V=\underline{\mathbb{C}}
gives rise to a minimal Lagrangian immersion. To formulate the symmetries (iv), (v) of Lemma
2.3
, we assume the existence of bundle isomorphisms
g\colon\Sigma\to\SU(V)
,
g\neq\mu\Id\,
and
b\colon V\to V^{*}
,
b=b^{*}
,
\det b=1
satisfying the compatibility
g^{*}bg=b
.

Then
V
splits orthogonally into the eigenline bundles

V=E_{-1}\oplus E_{0}\oplus E_{1}

of
g
, and with respect to this splitting one has

g=\diag(\zeta^{-1},1,\zeta),

where
\zeta=\exp(2\pi i/3)
.
Using the compatibility condition,
b=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&a\\
0&{a_{0}}&0\\
a^{*}&0&0\end{pmatrix}
and thus
E_{0}=E_{0}^{*}
via
a_{0}
and
E_{-1}=E_{1}^{*}
via
a
.
Since
E_{0}\simeq E_{0}^{*}
and

\Lambda^{3}V=E_{-1}\otimes E_{0}\otimes E_{1}\simeq E_{0}

is trivial, we conclude that
E_{0}=\underline{\mathbb{C}}
.
Thus
b=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&{1}\\
0&{1}&0\\
{1}&0&0\end{pmatrix}
on
V=E\oplus\mathbb{C}\oplus E^{-1}
.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
V\to\Sigma
be a Hermitian rank three bundle with trivial determinant bundle
\Lambda^{3}V=\underline{\mathbb{C}}
and an orthogonal splitting into line bundles
V=E\oplus\underline{\mathbb{C}}\oplus E^{-1}
. Let
d_{\lambda}
be a
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-family of flat
\SL(V)
-connections satisfying the properties (i)-(v) of Lemma
2.3
.
Then
E\cong K
holomorphically, so that

V=K\oplus\underline{\mathbb{C}}\oplus K^{-1},

and

d_{\lambda}=D+\lambda^{-1}\Phi-\lambda\Phi^{\dagger},

where
D
is a diagonal Hermitian connection and
\Phi\in\Gamma(K\mathfrak{sl}(V))
has the form (
2.12
). Moreover,
d_{\lambda}
defines an
\mathbb{S}^{1}
-family of minimal Lagrangian immersions on the universal cover
\pi\colon\tilde{\Sigma}\to\Sigma

with induced metric
\pi^{*}h
and holomorphic cubic differential
\lambda^{-3}\pi^{*}Q
, where
h
is the induced metric on
K^{-1}
and
Q=\det\Phi\in H^{0}(K^{3})
.
The immersion
f_{\lambda}
is equivariant (
2.26
) with respect to the monodromy representation
\rho_{\lambda}
of
d_{\lambda}
. If
\rho_{\lambda_{0}}=\Id
for some
\lambda_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
then
f_{\lambda_{0}}\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
and its Legendrian lift
\hat{f}_{\lambda_{0}}\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{S}^{5}
are both defined on
\Sigma
.

Proof.

From property (i) of Lemma
2.3
, we know that
d_{\lambda}
are
\SU(V)
-connections for
\lambda\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
. Let
D
be the diagonal Hermitian connection obtained from
d_{1}
via orthogonal projection onto the line bundles

V=E\oplus\underline{\mathbb{C}}\oplus E^{-1}
. Then property (ii) gives

d_{\lambda}=D+\lambda^{-1}\Phi-\lambda\Phi^{\dagger}

with
\Phi\in\Gamma(K\mathfrak{sl}(V))
.
The gauge
g=\diag(\zeta^{2},1,\zeta)
is
D
-parallel and property (iv) yields

\Ad g\,\Phi=\zeta\Phi

which implies

\Phi=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&\gamma\\
\alpha&0&0\\
0&\beta&0\end{pmatrix}.

Property (v) gives us
\alpha=\beta\in\Gamma(KE^{-1})
and thus
\det\Phi=\alpha^{2}\gamma
.
The flatness of the family
d_{\lambda}
implies that
\Phi
is
\bar{\partial}^{D}
-holomorphic, hence

\Phi\in H^{0}(K\mathfrak{sl}(V)).

Condition (iii) then shows that
\alpha\in H^{0}(KE^{-1})
is nowhere vanishing, thus
E=K
holomorphically, and
\det\Phi\in H^{0}(K^{3})
is a holomorphic cubic differential.

The
\mathbb{S}^{1}
-family of minimal Lagrangian immersions
f_{\lambda}
can now be constructed as in the proof of Lemma
2.3
.
∎

Remark 2.5
.

Lemmas
2.3
and
2.4
show that the construction of a minimal Lagrangian immersion
f\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
is
equivalent to the construction of a
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-family of flat connections
d_{\lambda}
on the Hermitian rank three bundle
V=K\oplus\mathbb{C}\oplus K^{-1}
satisfying the properties (i)-(v).

The geometric origin of the family of flat connections
d_{\lambda}
stems from the fact that the Tzitzéica equation (
2.19
) is invariant under a scaling of the cubic differential
Q
by a unimodular factor. As we showed in the previous Lemma, scaling
Q
by
\lambda^{-3}
is equivalent to scaling the Higgs field
\Phi
by
\lambda
. The resulting
\mathbb{S}^{1}
-family of flat connections
d_{\lambda}
—or the
\rho_{\lambda}
-equivariant minimal Lagrangian immersions
f_{\lambda}
—is traditionally called the
associated family
. A point
\lambda_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
at which
\rho_{\lambda_{0}}=c\,\Id
with
c^{3}=1
is called a
Sym point
.
The condition
\rho_{\lambda_{0}}=c\,\Id
with
c^{3}=1
is, in our context, frequently referred to as
extrinsic closing
, since it implies that the minimal Lagrangian immersion
f_{\lambda_{0}}
is defined on
\Sigma
, that is, the “periods of
f_{\lambda_{0}}
in
\mathbb{CP}^{2}

close
”. If
\rho_{\lambda_{0}}=\Id
is trivial, also the periods of the Legendrian lift
\hat{f}_{\lambda_{0}}
in
\mathbb{S}^{5}
close.

Finally, the
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
-symmetry in Lemma
2.3
(iv) is already present
[
5
]
in the case of minimal immersions
f\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
reflecting the periodicity of its harmonic sequence (
2.6
). The additional
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-symmetry in Lemma
2.3
(v) comes from the Lagrangian condition. Since the two symmetries commute, they combine to the
\mathbb{Z}_{6}
-symmetry of the associated family of flat connections

(2.30)

d_{\xi\lambda}=d_{\lambda}^{*}.a\,,\hskip 20.00003pta=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&\zeta^{2}\\
0&{1}&0\\
\zeta&0&0\end{pmatrix}\colon V\to V^{*}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\xi=\exp(\pi i/3)\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\xi^{2}=\zeta

for a minimal Lagrangian immersion
f\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}

[
46
,
15
]
.
These symmetries have been used extensively (see for example
[
44
,
21
,
9
]
) in the construction of minimal (Lagrangian) tori in
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
for which
Q=dz^{3}
,
since they allow one to lift into associated twistor spaces and apply the integrable-systems machinery of
[
6
]
.

3.
Loop groups and the global DPW construction

In Section
2.4
we saw that constructing minimal Lagrangian immersions into
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
is equivalent to constructing flat
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-families of connections of the form (
2.27
) satisfying the reality, symmetry, and asymptotic properties described in Lemma
2.3
; equivalently, one seeks families carrying the
\mathbb{Z}_{6}
-symmetry (
2.30
) together with the prescribed behaviour at
\lambda=0
and
\lambda=\infty
. The local DPW construction
[
14
]
shows that, on simply connected domains, such families can be generated from meromorphic
\lambda
-connections
D_{\lambda}
. We next adapt this method to the global setting.

A natural framework for treating
\lambda
-families of connections is that of loop groups: one packages such a family as a single connection with values in a loop algebra, or equivalently as a connection with loop-group structure group.

3.1.
Loop groups

Let
W
be a finite-dimensional Hermitian vector space,
\tau\colon\mathrm{SL}(W)\to\mathrm{SL}(W)
an order
r
Lie group automorphism preserving the Hermitian structure, and
\xi=\exp(2\pi i/r)
a primitive
r
-th root of unity. The (
\tau
-twisted) loop group

(3.1)

{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})=\{g\colon\mathbb{S}^{1}\to\mathrm{SL}(W)\,;\,\text{$g$ real analytic},\,\tau g(\lambda)=g(\xi\lambda)\}

is a smooth Banach Lie group via the norm

||g||=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}|g^{(k)}|\,.

Here
g=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}g^{(k)}\lambda^{k}
is the Fourier decomposition of
g
and
|g^{(k)}|
is a matrix norm for
g^{(k)}\in\mathfrak{gl}(W)
. The loop group
{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
has several Banach Lie subgroups relevant to our constructions: the group of
unitary loops

(3.2)

{\bf\SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})=\{g\in{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})\,;\,g(\lambda)^{-1}=g(1/{\bar{\lambda}})^{\dagger}\},

where
g(\lambda)\in\SU(W)
,
the group of
positive loops

(3.3)

{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D})=\{g\in{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})\,;\,\text{$g$ extends holomorphically to $\mathbb{D}$}\}

where
g
extends holomorphically into the unit disk
\mathbb{D}=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\,;\,|\lambda|<1\}
,
and the group

{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D}^{\times})=\{g\in{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})\,;\,\text{$g$ extends holomorphically to $\mathbb{D}^{\times}$}\}

of loops extending holomorphically into the punctured unit disk
\mathbb{D}^{\times}
. For the trivial automorphism
\tau=\Id
, we write
{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})={\bf\SL}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
and correspondingly so for the various subgroups.

The basic ingredient of the DPW method is the loop Iwasawa decomposition
[
49
,
14
]
. To guarantee the uniqueness of this decomposition, we choose a solvable subgroup
B\subset\mathrm{SL}(W)^{\tau}
in the fixed point subgroup under
\tau
such that
\mathrm{SL}(W)^{\tau}=\SU(W)^{\tau}B
and
B\cap\SU(W)^{\tau}=\{\Id\}
. Define

(3.4)

{\bf\SL}^{\tau}_{B}(\mathbb{D}):=\{g\in{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D})\,;\,g(0)\in B\}\subset{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D})

the Banach Lie subgroup of loops
g
extending holomorphically to the disk
\mathbb{D}
with
g(0)\in B
.

Theorem 2
.

The multiplication maps

{\bf\SL}^{\tau}_{B}(\mathbb{D})\times{\bf\SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})\to{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt{\bf\SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})\times{\bf\SL}^{\tau}_{B}(\mathbb{D})\to{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})

are smooth diffeomorphisms. We can thus decompose every loop
g\in{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
uniquely as

g=g_{+}g_{u}

with
g_{+}\in{\bf\SL}^{\tau}_{B}(\mathbb{D})
a positive loop with
g(0)\in B
and
g_{u}\in{\bf\SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
a unitary loop, and analogously for the reversed order.

Replacing
\mathrm{SL}(W)
by its Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sl}(W)
, we obtain the loop Lie algebras of the various loop groups under consideration. For example,

{\bf sl}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})=\{x\colon\mathbb{S}^{1}\to\mathfrak{sl}(W)\,;\,\text{$x$ real analytic},\,\tau x(\lambda)=x(\xi\lambda)\}

is the Banach loop Lie algebra of
{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
. Let
\mathfrak{g}_{j}\subset\mathfrak{sl}(W)
denote the
\xi^{j}
-eigenspace of
\tau
with commutator relations
[\mathfrak{g}_{j},\mathfrak{g}_{l}]\subset\mathfrak{g}_{j+l}
where
j,l\in\mathbb{Z}_{r}
. The condition
\tau x(\lambda)=x(\xi\lambda)
then translates to
x^{(k)}\in\mathfrak{g}_{k}
where
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{r}
and
x=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}x^{(k)}\lambda^{k}
. Furthermore, the loop
x\in{\bf su}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
is unitary if and only if

x^{(-k)}=-(x^{(k)})^{\dagger}\in\mathfrak{g}_{-k}
.

Example 3.1
.

To define the twisted loop groups relevant for minimal Lagrangian surfaces, consider

W=\mathbb{C}^{3}
and identify
W\cong W^{*}
via the standard complex bilinear inner product so that the dual of
A\in\mathfrak{sl}_{3}(\mathbb{C})
is given by
A^{*}=-A^{t}
.
The order 6 automorphism corresponding to the
\mathbb{Z}_{6}
-symmetry (
2.30
) is given by

\tau(g)=\mathfrak{a}^{-1}(g^{-1})^{t}\mathfrak{a}

with

\mathfrak{a}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&\zeta^{2}\\
0&{1}&0\\
\zeta&0&0\end{pmatrix}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\zeta=\exp(2\pi i/3)\,.

The corresponding Lie algebra automorphism
\tau\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{sl}_{3}(\mathbb{C}))
is given by

\tau(A)=-\mathfrak{a}^{-1}A^{t}\mathfrak{a}\,.

For computational purposes in Section
7
, we rearrange the line bundle decomposition (
2.10
) to

(3.5)

L\oplus KL\oplus K^{-1}L,

which amounts to conjugating
\tau
by the inner automorphism
\varphi(g)=c^{-1}gc
with

c=\begin{pmatrix}0&i&0\\
i&0&0\\
0&0&-1\end{pmatrix}
. The factors of
i
are chosen so that the natural Legendrian lift
\hat{f}\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{S}^{5}
of a minimal Lagrangian immersion
f\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
is
special
Legendrian (rather than just minimal Legendrian). The resulting order
r=6
automorphism
\hat{\tau}=\varphi^{-1}\circ\tau\circ\varphi
is then given by

(3.6)

\hat{\tau}(g)=\mathfrak{\hat{a}}^{-1}(g^{-1})^{t}\mathfrak{\hat{a}}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\mathfrak{\hat{a}}=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0&0\\
0&0&i\zeta^{2}\\
0&i\zeta&0\end{pmatrix}.

The
\xi^{k}=\exp(2\pi ik/6)
eigenspaces
{\mathfrak{\hat{g}}}_{k}
under the action of
{\hat{\tau}}
on the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sl}_{3}(\mathbb{C})
are

(3.7)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{\hat{g}}}_{-1}

\displaystyle=\{\begin{pmatrix}0&a&0\\
0&0&b\\
ia&0&0\end{pmatrix}\,;\,a,b\in\mathbb{C}\},\hskip 10.00002pt{\mathfrak{\hat{g}}}_{0}=\{\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&a&0\\
0&0&-a\end{pmatrix}\,;\,a\in\mathbb{C}\},\hskip 10.00002pt{\mathfrak{\hat{g}}}_{1}=\{\begin{pmatrix}0&0&ia\\
-a&0&0\\
0&b&0\end{pmatrix}\,;\,a,b\in\mathbb{C}\}

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{\hat{g}}}_{2}

\displaystyle=\{\begin{pmatrix}0&a&0\\
0&0&0\\
-ia&0&0\end{pmatrix}\,;\,a\in\mathbb{C}\},\hskip 10.00002pt{\mathfrak{\hat{g}}}_{3}=\{\begin{pmatrix}-2a&0&0\\
0&a&0\\
0&0&a\end{pmatrix}\,;\,a\in\mathbb{C}\},\hskip 10.00002pt{\mathfrak{\hat{g}}}_{4}=\{\begin{pmatrix}0&0&a\\
-ia&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix}\,;\,a\in\mathbb{C}\}.

3.2.
Loop connections

Definition 3.2
.

Let
V=N\times W
be the trivial Hermitian bundle over a Riemann surface
N
. An
{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection
is given by

(3.8)

D_{\lambda}=d+\eta_{\lambda}

with
d
the trivial connection on
V
and
\eta_{\lambda}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\eta^{(k)}\lambda^{k}\in\Omega^{1}(N,{\bf sl}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1}))
, that is,
\eta_{\xi\lambda}=\tau\eta_{\lambda}
equivalently
\eta^{(k)}\in\Omega^{1}(N,\mathfrak{g}_{k})
. Note that the connection
D=d+\eta^{(0)}
is an
\mathrm{SL}(W)^{\tau}
-connection.

A smooth map
g_{\lambda}\colon N\to{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
is called an
{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-gauge.

The corresponding notations are used for the various subgroups of
{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
introduced above. In particular, an
{\bf\SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection
D_{\lambda}
has
D=d+\eta^{(0)}
as an
\SU(W)^{\tau}
-connection and
\eta^{(-k)}=-(\eta^{(k)})^{\dagger}\in\Omega^{1}(N,\mathfrak{g}_{-k})
for all
k\neq 0
. We often refer to
\eta_{\lambda}
as the
potential
of the connection
D_{\lambda}
.

An
{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection
D_{\lambda}=d+\eta_{\lambda}
is called a (
\tau
-twisted)
DPW-connection
if
D_{\lambda}
is a flat
{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D}^{\times})
-connection with a simple pole at
\lambda=0
. In other words, the potential
\eta_{\lambda}=\sum_{k\geq-1}\eta^{(k)}\lambda^{k}\in\Omega^{1}(N,{\bf sl}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D}^{\times}))
with residue
\eta^{(-1)}\in\Omega^{(1,0)}(\mathfrak{g}_{-1})
and
d\eta_{\lambda}+\eta_{\lambda}\wedge\eta_{\lambda}=0
.

Remark 3.3
.

Since
\tau
is parallel with respect to the trivial connection
d
, an
{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection
D_{\lambda}=d+\eta_{\lambda}
may also be characterized by the relation

D_{\xi\lambda}=\tau D_{\lambda}\,.

where
\tau D_{\lambda}:=d+\tau(\eta_{\lambda})
.

Remark 3.4
.

DPW-connections
D_{\lambda}=d+\sum_{k\geq-1}\eta^{(k)}\lambda^{k}
are preserved under
{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D})
-gauges
b_{\lambda}
:

D_{\lambda}.b_{\lambda}=d+b_{\lambda}^{-1}d\,b_{\lambda}+\Ad b^{-1}_{\lambda}\eta_{\lambda}=d+\sum_{k\geq-1}\tilde{\eta}^{(k)}\lambda^{k}

with
\tilde{\eta}^{(-1)}=\Ad(b^{(0)})^{-1}\eta^{(-1)}
.
In particular, a DPW connection
D_{\lambda}
is an
{\bf\SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection if and only if

D_{\lambda}=D+\lambda^{-1}\eta^{(-1)}-\lambda(\eta^{(-1)})^{\dagger}

where
D=d+\eta^{(0)}
is an
\SU(W)^{\tau}
-connection.

Since the families of flat connections (
2.27
) that we seek are unitary along
\mathbb{S}^{1}
, we first need to understand when an
{\bf SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection can be unitarized, that is, when is there an
{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-gauge
g_{\lambda}
such that
D_{\lambda}.g_{\lambda}
is an
{\bf\SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
D_{\lambda}
be a flat
{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection on
V=N\times W
and assume that its monodromy representation
\rho_{\lambda}\colon\pi_{1}(N,q_{0})\to\mathrm{SL}(W)
is irreducible and unitarizable for every
\lambda\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
where
q_{0}\in N
is some base point.
Additionally, assume that there exists a subgroup
\hat{B}\subset\SL(W)
satisfying

\tau(\hat{B})\subset\hat{B},

\mathrm{SL}(W)=\SU(W)\hat{B}
and
\SU(W)\cap\hat{B}=\{\Id\}.

Then there exists an
{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D})
-gauge
b_{\lambda}
such that
D_{\lambda}.b_{\lambda}
is a flat
{\bf\SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection.

In particular, if
D_{\lambda}=d+\sum_{k\geq-1}\eta^{(k)}\lambda^{k}
is a DPW-connection, then

D_{\lambda}.b_{\lambda}=D+\lambda^{-1}\Psi-\lambda\Psi^{\dagger}

with
D
an
\SU(W)^{\tau}
-connection and
\Psi=\Ad(b^{(0)})^{-1}\,\eta^{(-1)}\in\Omega^{(1,0)}(\mathfrak{g}_{-1})
where
\mathfrak{sl}(W)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{r}}\mathfrak{g_{j}}
is the
\tau
-eigenspace decomposition.

Proof.

As
\rho_{\lambda}
is unitarizable for each
\lambda\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
, there exists
a_{\lambda}\in\mathrm{SL}(W)
such that

a_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}a_{\lambda}^{-1}\colon\pi_{1}(N,q_{0})\to\SU(W)

is a unitary representation. Since
\rho_{\lambda}
is irreducible for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
, the unitarizer
a_{\lambda}
is unique up to left multiplication by
\SU(W)
. Consider the real-analytic finite-dimensional Iwasawa splitting
\mathrm{SL}(W)=\SU(W)\hat{B}
.
Thus, demanding that
a_{\lambda}\in\hat{B}
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
provides a unique choice of unitarizer.
Since
\rho_{\lambda}
depends real-analytically on
\lambda
, and the normalization
a_{\lambda}\in\hat{B}
makes the unitarizer unique, the map
\lambda\mapsto a_{\lambda}
is real analytic; hence
a_{\lambda}\in{\bf\SL}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
. To verify that
a_{\lambda}\in{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
is twisted, we use that
D_{\lambda}
is an
{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection and thus
\rho_{\xi\lambda}=\tau\rho_{\lambda}
. Therefore

a_{\xi\lambda}^{-1}\tau a_{\lambda}\rho_{\xi\lambda}(a_{\xi\lambda}^{-1}\tau a_{\lambda})^{-1}=\rho_{\xi\lambda}

and, by irreducibility of
\rho_{\xi\lambda}
, we obtain

a_{\xi\lambda}^{-1}\tau a_{\lambda}=c\,\Id\,,\hskip 10.00002ptc^{\dim W}=1\,.

Since
\tau(\hat{B})\subset\hat{B}
the left hand side lies in
\hat{B}
whereas the right hand side lies in
\SU(W)
, hence

c=1
. Hence
a_{\xi\lambda}=\tau a_{\lambda}
, i.e.
a\in{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
.

Let
H\colon V\times V\to\mathbb{C}
be the Hermitian bundle metric on
V=N\times W
given by the Hermitian structure on
W
.
We may assume — after gauging by the constant-in-
N
twisted loop
a_{\lambda}
— that
\rho_{\lambda}
is unitary.
Let
\hat{H}_{\lambda}\colon V\times V\to\mathbb{C}
be the Hermitian bundle metric obtained via parallel transport of
H
by the connection
D_{\lambda}
for
\lambda\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
. Since
\rho_{\lambda}
is unitary,
\hat{H}_{\lambda}
is well-defined on
N
, that is, has trivial monodromy. Thus there is a smooth map
g_{\lambda}\colon N\to\mathrm{SL}(W)
with
g_{\lambda}^{*}H=\hat{H}_{\lambda}
for
\lambda\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
. Utilizing the unique splitting
\mathrm{SL}(W)=\SU(W)\hat{B}
, we also have
(g_{\lambda})_{\hat{B}}^{*}H=\hat{H}_{\lambda}
. This implies that we can choose
g_{\lambda}
uniquely by requiring it to take values in
\hat{B}

for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{S}^{1}.

Then,
g_{\lambda}
again depends real-analytically on
\lambda
. In other words,
g_{\lambda}
is an
{\bf\SL}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-gauge and, by construction,
D_{\lambda}.g_{\lambda}
is an
{\bf\SU}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection.

It remains to show that
g_{\xi\lambda}=\tau g_{\lambda}
is twisted. By construction,
g_{\lambda}
is the unique real analytic
\hat{B}
-valued map such that
D_{\lambda}.g_{\lambda}
is an
{\bf\SU}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection. Using the irreducibility of the

{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection

D_{\lambda}
once more, we obtain that

D_{\xi\lambda}.g_{\xi\lambda}=(\tau D_{\lambda}).g_{\xi\lambda}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt(\tau D_{\lambda}).(\tau g_{\lambda})

are both
{\bf\SU}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connections. By irreducibility, this implies

\tau g_{\lambda}=g_{\xi\lambda}\,c\,\Id\,,

c^{\dim W}=1
. As before, we obtain
c=1
and therefore
\tau g_{\lambda}=g_{\xi\lambda},

i.e.
g_{\lambda}
is twisted.

We can now apply Theorem
2
to factorize

g_{\lambda}=b_{\lambda}u_{\lambda}

pointwise on
N
into a positive
\tau
-twisted and unitary
\tau
-twisted loop.
Then also
D_{\lambda}.b_{\lambda}
is an
{\bf\SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection via the
{\bf\SL}^{\tau}_{B}(\mathbb{D})
-gauge
b_{\lambda}
.
Finally, if
D_{\lambda}=d+\eta_{\lambda}
is a DPW-connection, then Remark
3.4
implies that

D_{\lambda}.b_{\lambda}=D+\lambda^{-1}\Psi-\lambda\Psi^{\dagger}

is a flat
{\bf\SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection with
\Psi=\Ad(b^{(0)})^{-1}\,\eta^{(-1)}
.
∎

Remark 3.6
.

In the minimal Lagrangian case of Example
3.1
, one may take
\hat{B}\subset\SL_{3}(\mathbb{C})
to be the subgroup of upper triangular matrices with positive real diagonal entries, and
B\subset\SL_{3}(\mathbb{C})^{\tau}
the corresponding solvable subgroup appearing in Theorem
2
. Then

\SL_{3}(\mathbb{C})=\SU_{3}\,\hat{B},\hskip 20.00003pt\SU_{3}\cap\hat{B}=\{\Id\},\hskip 20.00003pt\tau(\hat{B})=\hat{B}.

Hence the hypotheses of Lemma
3.5
are satisfied. The same is true for the conjugated automorphism
\hat{\tau}
of Example
3.1
, after conjugating
\hat{B}
by the matrix
c
in (
3.6
).

Corollary 3.7
.

Let
\tilde{D}_{\lambda}
be a flat, not necessarily
\tau
-twisted,
{\bf\SL}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection on
V=N\times W
and assume that its monodromy representation
\tilde{\rho}_{\lambda}\colon\pi_{1}(N,q_{0})\to\mathrm{SL}(W)
is irreducible and unitarizable for every
\lambda\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
. Assume that there exists an
{\bf\SL}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-gauge

g_{\lambda}
such that

D_{\lambda}:=\tilde{D}_{\lambda}.g_{\lambda}=d+\eta_{\lambda}

is a
\tau
-twisted DPW-connection. Then there exists
an
{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D})
-gauge
b_{\lambda}
so that

D_{\lambda}.b_{\lambda}=D+\lambda^{-1}\Psi-\lambda\Psi^{\dagger}

is a flat
{\bf\SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
connection with
\Psi=\Ad(b^{(0)})^{-1}\,\eta^{(-1)}\in\Omega^{(1,0)}(N,\mathfrak{g}_{-1})
and
D
an
\SU(W)^{\tau}
-connection.

Proof.

Because
D_{\lambda}
is
{\bf\SL}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-gauge equivalent to
\tilde{D}_{\lambda}
, the monodromy
of
D_{\lambda}
is irreducible and unitarizable for every
\lambda\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
. Applying the previous Lemma proves the claim.
∎

3.3.
\tau
-structures

As observed in Remark
3.3
, a
\tau
-twisted connection

D_{\lambda}=d+\eta_{\lambda}

on a trivial bundle
N\times W
is defined using a fixed automorphism
\tau
that is parallel with respect to the trivial connection
d
. This formulation is inherently local. On a compact Riemann surface, the associated family of a harmonic map typically lives on a nontrivial bundle, and the existence of a global trivialization in which the twisting automorphism is constant would force the underlying
\SU(W)^{\tau}
-bundle
to be trivial.

This is already too restrictive in the minimal Lagrangian case. There the natural bundle is

V=K\oplus\underline{\mathbb{C}}\oplus K^{-1}.

Although
V
is topologically trivial as a complex rank-three bundle, its
\SU(\mathbb{C}^{3})^{\tau}
-reduction involves the canonical bundle
K
and is therefore nontrivial unless
K
itself is trivial. On a compact Riemann surface of genus
g\neq 1
one
cannot
expect a global
\tau
-twisted DPW potential on the surface itself. This is the basic reason for introducing
\tau
-structures: they provide the intrinsic global replacement for a fixed twisted trivialization.

The passage from local twisted potentials to global objects has been carried out in special cases, such as minimal and CMC surfaces in the
3
-sphere (see for example
[
25
]
), but a general framework for more complicated target spaces does not seem to be available in the literature.

Definition 3.8
.

Let
W
be a finite-dimensional Hermitian vector space,
\tau\colon\mathrm{SL}(W)\to\mathrm{SL}(W)
an order
r
Lie group automorphism preserving the Hermitian structure, and
\xi=\exp(2\pi i/r)
a primitive
r
-th root of unity. A Hermitian vector bundle
V\to M
is an
\SU(W)^{\tau}
-bundle (or equivalently admits a
\tau
-structure)
provided there exist isometric trivializations whose transition functions take values in
\SU(W)^{\tau}
. An
\mathbb{S}^{1}
-family
\nabla_{\lambda}
on an
\SU(W)^{\tau}
-bundle
V
is called an
{\bf\SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection if
\nabla_{\lambda}
is an
{\bf\SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection in every
\tau
-trivialization.
The same definition applies with
{\bf SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
replaced by any of the twisted loop groups introduced in Section
3
.

Remark 3.9
.

An
\SU(W)^{\tau}
-bundle is the global replacement for a trivial bundle endowed with a fixed
d
-parallel twisting automorphism
\tau
. In the minimal Lagrangian case, the corresponding
\SU(\mathbb{C}^{3})^{\tau}
-bundle is
K\oplus\underline{\mathbb{C}}\oplus K^{-1}
; it is trivial as an
\SU(\mathbb{C}^{3})^{\tau}
-bundle only if
K
is trivial.

Example 3.10
.

We recall from Lemma
2.4
that given a Riemann surface
M
every minimal Lagrangian immersion
f\colon M\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}

is obtained from a
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-family of flat connections
d_{\lambda}
on the Hermitian rank three bundle
V=K\oplus\underline{\mathbb{C}}\oplus K^{-1}
satisfying the properties (i)-(v) in Lemma
2.3
.

The Hermitian bundle
V=K\oplus\underline{\mathbb{C}}\oplus K^{-1}
with diagonal metric gives rise to an
\SU(W)^{\tau}
-structure with
W=\mathbb{C}^{3}
and
\tau
as in Example
3.1
. Choosing a local trivialization
\psi
of
K
and corresponding trivialization
\psi^{-1}
of
K^{-1}
, we locally trivialize
V
by

(3.9)

(\psi,1,\psi^{-1})\mapsto(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3})

where
e_{k}
denote the standard basis in
\mathbb{C}^{3}
. These trivializations are isometric and their transition functions take values in
\SU(\mathbb{C}^{3})^{\tau}
.
Moreover, the family of flat connections
d_{\lambda}=D+\lambda^{-1}\Phi-\lambda\Phi^{\dagger}
is an
{\bf\SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection on
V
.
The local trivializations (
3.9
) map
d_{\lambda}
to
d+\omega^{(0)}+\lambda^{-1}\omega-\lambda\omega^{\dagger}
and
D
to
d+\omega^{(0)}
with
\omega^{(0)}\in\mathfrak{g}_{0}
,
\omega\in\mathfrak{g}_{-1}
,
\omega^{\dagger}\in\mathfrak{g}_{1}
as required for an
{\bf\SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection on
W
.

In fact, every
\SU(\mathbb{C}^{3})^{\tau}
-bundle,
with
\tau
as in Example
3.1
,
splits orthogonally as
V=E\oplus\underline{\mathbb{C}}\oplus E^{-1}
for a unique line bundle
E\to M
.
For a
\tau
-trivialization
F\colon V\to\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{3}
, we locally define an orthogonal decomposition
V=E_{1}\oplus E_{2}\oplus E_{3}
via
F(E_{k})=\mathbb{C}e_{k}
. Since the transition functions of such trivializations take values in
\SU(\mathbb{C}^{3})^{\tau}
they are of the form
\diag(a,1,a^{-1})
. Therefore, the sub-bundles
E_{k}\subset V
are globally well-defined. Moreover,
E_{3}=E_{1}^{-1}
and
E_{2}=\underline{\mathbb{C}}
.

These observations provide a useful variant of Lemma
2.4
.

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
M
be a Riemann surface,
V\to M
a rank three Hermitian
\SU(\mathbb{C}^{3})^{\tau}
-bundle with trivial determinant bundle, and let
d_{\lambda}=D+\lambda^{-1}\Phi-\lambda\Phi^{\dagger}
be a flat
{\bf\SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection with
\Phi^{2}\in\Gamma(M,K^{2}\mathfrak{sl}(V))
nowhere vanishing.
Then
V=K\oplus\underline{\mathbb{C}}\oplus K^{-1}
,
D
is the diagonal connection, the Higgs field
\Phi\in\Gamma(K\mathfrak{sl}(V))
is of the form (
2.12
)
with respect to the induced splitting.
Moreover,
d_{\lambda}
describes an
\mathbb{S}^{1}
-family of minimal Lagrangian immersions
f_{\lambda}\colon\tilde{M}\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
equivariant (
2.26
) with respect to the monodromy
\rho_{\lambda}
of
d_{\lambda}
. If for some
\lambda_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
the representation
\rho_{\lambda_{0}}=\Id
is trivial, then
f_{\lambda_{0}}\colon M\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
and its special Legendrian lift
\hat{f}_{\lambda_{0}}\colon M\to\mathbb{S}^{5}
descend to
M
.

Proof.

We have already seen in the discussion above that the
\SU(\mathbb{C}^{3})^{\tau}
-bundle splits orthogonally as
V=E\oplus\underline{\mathbb{C}}\oplus E^{-1}
for a line bundle
E\to M
. If we can verify the properties (i)-(v) of Lemma
2.3
then Lemma
2.4
proves our claims. Now (i), (ii), and (iii) hold by assumption. The discussion above also shows that
in any local
\tau
-trivialization
F\colon V\to\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{3}

the
{\bf\SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection
d_{\lambda}
takes the form
d+\omega^{(0)}+\lambda^{-1}\omega-\lambda\omega^{\dagger}
with
\omega_{0}\in\mathfrak{g}_{0}
,
\omega\in\mathfrak{g}_{-1}
and
\omega^{\dagger}\in\mathfrak{g}_{1}
. In particular,
\omega^{(0)}
preserves the orthogonal decomposition
\mathbb{C}^{3}=\mathbb{C}e_{1}\oplus\mathbb{C}e_{2}\oplus\mathbb{C}e_{3}
and thus
D
, whose local expression is
d+\omega^{(0)}
, preserves the decomposition
V=E\oplus\underline{\mathbb{C}}\oplus E^{-1}
.
Define the
D
-parallel gauges
g=\diag(\zeta^{2},1,\zeta)
for the cube root
\zeta=\exp(2\pi i/3)
and
b=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&1\\
0&1&0\\
1&0&0\end{pmatrix}\colon V\to V^{*}

which satisfy properties (iv) and (v) of Lemma
2.3
since they do so in the
\tau
-trivializations. We can now apply Lemma
2.4
.
∎

Remark 3.12
.

The minimal Lagrangian immersion
f_{\lambda_{0}}\colon M\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
from the previous Lemma can be viewed as the line subbundle
\underline{\mathbb{C}}\subset V
in the decomposition
V=K\oplus\underline{\mathbb{C}}\oplus K^{-1}
with respect to the trivial background connection
d_{\lambda_{0}}
as explained in (
2.25
). Thus, in every
\tau
-trivialization
\Upsilon\colon V\to\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{3}
, the minimal Lagrangian immersion is given as the preimage
f_{\lambda_{0}}=\Upsilon(\mathbb{C}e_{2})
of the line
\mathbb{C}e_{2}\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}
. Moreover,
\hat{f}_{\lambda_{0}}=\Upsilon(e_{2})
is a minimal Legendrian lift into
\mathbb{S}^{5}
.

Remark 3.13
.

In later parts of the paper, we shall work with the conjugated order six symmetry
\hat{\tau}
of Example
3.1
. In that case, the minimal Lagrangian immersion is given as
f_{\lambda_{0}}=\Upsilon(\mathbb{C}e_{1})
and the minimal Legendrian lift

\hat{f}_{\lambda_{0}}=\Upsilon(e_{1})=F^{-1}(e_{1})

into
\mathbb{S}^{5}
is in fact
special Legendrian:

with
\omega_{-1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&a&0\\
0&0&b\\
ia&0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)dz
and
\omega_{1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0&i\bar{a}\\
-\bar{a}&0&0\\
0&-\bar{b}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)d\bar{z}
for a holomorphic coordinate
z=x+iy
and local functions
a,b
,
the inverse of the
\hat{\tau}
-trivialization
F=\Upsilon^{-1}
satisfies

-dFF^{-1}=\omega_{\lambda_{0}}=\lambda_{0}^{-1}\omega^{(-1)}+\omega^{(0)}+\lambda_{0}\omega^{(1)}\,.

Thus, the holomorphic volume form evaluates to

\Omega(\tfrac{1}{2}F^{-1}e_{1},\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z}F^{-1}e_{1},\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}}F^{-1}e_{1})dz\wedge d\bar{z}=\tfrac{1}{2}\det(e_{1},iae_{3},-\bar{a}e_{2})dz\wedge d\bar{z}=|a|^{2}dx\wedge dy>0.

Thus, the phase
\varphi
in (
2.1
) is a real positive constant. As
\varphi
is by construction of length
1
, we obtain

\varphi=1

and the surface
F^{-1}(e_{1})
is special Legendrian.

Remark
.

Our focus lies on the construction of minimal Lagrangian surfaces, and we do not aim to discuss further the general theory for harmonic maps into compact symmetric spaces. For the case of negatively curved symmetric spaces, a general theory has been developed, see
e.g.
[
18
]
.

3.4.
Removable singularities

We now pass to compact Riemann surfaces with punctures. The aim is to remove
the singularities of meromorphic DPW-connections in a way compatible with the underlying
\tau
-structure.

Definition 3.14
.

An
{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection
D_{\lambda}=d+\eta_{\lambda}
is
meromorphic
if
\eta_{\lambda}
is an

{\bf sl}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-valued meromorphic 1-form on
N
with poles contained in the punctures
\{q_{1},\dots,q_{n}\}
. Thus,
if
\eta_{\lambda}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\eta^{(k)}\lambda^{k}
then
\eta^{(k)}
are meromorphic
\mathfrak{g}_{k}
-valued 1-forms on
N
whose poles are contained in the punctures. Since each coefficient
\eta^{(k)}
is meromorphic, hence of type
(1,0)
, such a connection is automatically flat on
\mathring{N}
.

An
{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-gauge
g_{\lambda}
is
meromorphic
if
g_{\lambda}\colon N\to{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
is meromorphic on
N
with poles contained in the punctures
\{q_{1},\dots,q_{n}\}
. In other words, if
g_{\lambda}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}g^{(k)}\lambda^{k}
then
g^{(k)}\colon N\to\mathfrak{gl}(W)
are meromorphic with poles contained in the punctures.

Definition 3.15
.

Let
\mathring{M}=M\setminus\{p_{0}\}
be a punctured surface and
D_{\lambda}
an
{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection on
\mathring{M}\times W
.
We call
p_{0}\in M
an
{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D})
-
apparent singularity
if there exists a gauge
g_{\lambda}\colon\mathring{M}\to{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D})
such that the
{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection

D_{\lambda}.g_{\lambda}
extends smoothly into
p_{0}\in M
.

The following lemma links the removal of singularities with
\SU(W)^{\tau}
-bundles.

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
\mathring{M}=M\setminus\{p_{0}\}
, and let
\mathring{\nabla}_{\lambda}
be an
{\bf\SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection on
\mathring{M}\times W
, and
\tilde{\nabla}_{\lambda}
an
{\bf\SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection on
M\times W
. Assume there exists an
{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D})
-gauge
h_{\lambda}\colon\mathring{M}\to{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D})
with
h_{\lambda}(p_{1})\in\SU(W)^{\tau}
for
p_{1}\in\mathring{M}
such that
\mathring{\nabla}_{\lambda}=\tilde{\nabla}_{\lambda}.h_{\lambda}
are gauge equivalent over
\mathring{M}
.
Then

(i)

h_{\lambda}=h_{0}\colon\mathring{M}\to\SU(W)^{\tau}
.

(ii)

There exists an
\SU(W)^{\tau}
-bundle
V\to M
with an
{\bf\SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection
\nabla_{\lambda}
for which

\nabla_{\lambda}=\mathring{\nabla}_{\lambda}.\Upsilon

in a
\tau
-trivialization
\Upsilon\colon V_{|\mathring{M}}\to\mathring{M}\times W
.

Proof.

Since
\mathring{\nabla}_{\lambda}=d+\mathring{\omega}_{\lambda}
with
\mathring{\omega}_{\lambda}\in\Omega^{1}(\mathring{M},{\bf su}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1}))
and
\tilde{\nabla}_{\lambda}=d+\tilde{\omega}_{\lambda}
with
\tilde{\omega}_{\lambda}\in\Omega^{1}(M,{\bf su}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1}))
, the gauge
h_{\lambda}
satisfies

dh_{\lambda}=h_{\lambda}\mathring{\omega}_{\lambda}-\tilde{\omega}_{\lambda}h_{\lambda}.

Using
\mathring{\omega}_{\lambda}^{\dagger}=-\mathring{\omega}_{1/\bar{\lambda}}
and likewise for
\tilde{\omega}_{\lambda}
, we see that
h_{\lambda}^{-1}
and
h_{1/\bar{\lambda}}^{\dagger}
satisfy the same linear differential equation with the same initial condition at
p_{1}\in\mathring{M}
. Therefore,
h_{\lambda}^{-1}=h_{1/\bar{\lambda}}^{\dagger}
and since
h_{\lambda}
is an
{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D})
-gauge, we conclude
h_{\lambda}=h_{0}
is independent of
\lambda
and thus takes values in
\SU(W)^{\tau}
.
We now glue the trivial
W
-bundles over
\mathring{M}
and
M
using
h_{0}
as the transition function to obtain the
\SU(W)^{\tau}
-bundle
V\to M
with
{\bf\SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection
\nabla_{\lambda}
as stated.
∎

With this at hand, we have the following removable singularity result:

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
\mathring{M}=M\setminus\{p_{0}\}
with
M
simply connected and
D_{\lambda}
a flat
{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection on
\mathring{M}\times W
for which
p_{0}\in M
is an
{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D})
-apparent singularity. Further assume there exists an
{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D})
gauge
b_{\lambda}\colon\mathring{M}\to{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D})
so that
D_{\lambda}.b_{\lambda}
is a flat
{\bf\SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection on
\mathring{M}\times W
. Then there exists an
\SU(W)^{\tau}
-bundle
V\to M
and a flat
{\bf\SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection
\nabla_{\lambda}
on
V\to M
such that

\nabla_{\lambda}=(D_{\lambda}.b_{\lambda}).\Upsilon

for a
\tau
-trivialization
