\Upsilon\colon V_{|\mathring{M}}\to\mathring{M}\times W
.

Proof.

Since
p_{0}\in M
is an
{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D})
-apparent singularity, there is a gauge
g_{\lambda}\colon\mathring{M}\to{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D})
with

D_{\lambda}.g_{\lambda}
defined on
M
. Now
M
is simply connected and thus we have a gauge
k_{\lambda}\colon M\to{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
such that

D_{\lambda}.g_{\lambda}=d.k_{\lambda}

with
d
the trivial connection on
M\times W
.
Thus,

D_{\lambda}.b_{\lambda}=(D_{\lambda}.g_{\lambda}).(g_{\lambda}^{-1}b_{\lambda})=d.(k_{\lambda}g_{\lambda}^{-1}b_{\lambda})\,.

Applying the Iwasawa decomposition in Theorem
2
, we factorize
k_{\lambda}=(k_{\lambda})_{u}(k_{\lambda})_{+}
with

(k_{\lambda})_{+}\colon M\to{\bf\SL}_{B}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D})
and
(k_{\lambda})_{u}\colon M\to{\bf\SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
. Then

D_{\lambda}.b_{\lambda}=(d.(k_{\lambda})_{u}).h_{\lambda}

with
h_{\lambda}:=(k_{\lambda})_{+}g_{\lambda}^{-1}b_{\lambda}\colon\mathring{M}\to{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D})
. Since the gauge
k_{\lambda}
can be chosen to satisfy
k_{\lambda}(p_{1})=b_{\lambda}^{-1}(p_{1})g_{\lambda}(p_{1})
at some fixed
p_{1}\in\mathring{M}
, we have

1=k_{\lambda}(p_{1})g_{\lambda}^{-1}(p_{1})b_{\lambda}(p_{1})=(k_{\lambda})_{u}(p_{1})(k_{\lambda})_{+}(p_{1})g_{\lambda}^{-1}(p_{1})b_{\lambda}(p_{1})

with
(k_{\lambda})_{u}(p_{1})\in{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D})\cap{\bf\SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})=SU(W)^{\tau}
. Thus
h_{\lambda}(p_{1})\in\SU(W)^{\tau}
and we can apply Lemma
3.16
to
\mathring{\nabla}_{\lambda}=D_{\lambda}.b_{\lambda}
and
\tilde{\nabla}_{\lambda}=d.(k_{\lambda})_{u}
to obtain the desired result.
∎

In the setting of this paper, we will have to pass to a suitable covering in order to be able to remove singularities.

Theorem 3
.

Let
\mathring{N}=N\setminus\{q_{1},\dots,q_{n}\}
be a punctured Riemann surface and
D_{\lambda}=d+\eta_{\lambda}
a DPW-connection on
N\times W
with poles
q_{j}\in N
. Assume there exists an
{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D})
-gauge
b_{\lambda}\colon\mathring{N}\to{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D})
so that
D_{\lambda}.b_{\lambda}
is a flat
{\bf\SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection on
\mathring{N}\times W
necessarily of the form

D_{\lambda}.b_{\lambda}=D+\lambda^{-1}\Psi-\lambda\Psi^{\dagger}

with
\Psi=\Ad b_{0}^{-1}\eta_{-1}\in\Omega^{1,0}(\mathring{N},\mathfrak{g}_{-1})
.

If there exists a Riemann surface
M
and a holomorphic covering
\pi\colon M\to N
branched over
\{q_{1},\dots,q_{n}\}
all of whose branch points are
{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D})
-apparent for
\pi^{*}D_{\lambda}
, then there exists an
\SU(W)^{\tau}
-bundle
V\to M
and a flat
{\bf\SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection

\nabla_{\lambda}=\nabla+\lambda^{-1}\Phi-\lambda\Phi^{\dagger}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{
with}\hskip 20.00003pt\nabla_{\lambda}=\pi^{*}(D_{\lambda}.b_{\lambda}).\Upsilon

for a
\tau
-trivialization
\Upsilon\colon V_{|\mathring{M}}\to\mathring{M}\times W
.

Proof.

Apply Lemma
3.17
on punctured disks around the branch points of
\pi
, and glue the resulting local
\SU(W)^{\tau}
-bundles by the induced
\SU(W)^{\tau}
-valued transition functions to obtain
V
and
\nabla_{\lambda}
on
M
.
∎

The following definition specifies under which condition we can apply Theorem
3
on a covering.

Definition 3.18
.

Let
D_{\lambda}=d+\eta_{\lambda}
be a meromorphic DPW-connection on
V=N\times W
with singularity at
q.
Assume there exists
a local branched covering
\pi\colon U\to N
of degree
k
totally branched over
q
. Assume that there exists an

{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D})
-gauge
G
on
U\setminus\{\pi^{-1}(q)\}

such that
(\pi^{*}D_{\lambda}).G
extends smoothly to
\pi^{-1}(q)
. We then call
q
a
k
-
removable
singularity.

3.5.
Minimal Lagrangian surfaces from meromorphic potentials

From now on we restrict to the minimal Lagrangian case, so that
W=\mathbb{C}^{3}
and
\tau
is one of the two order-six automorphisms from Example
3.1
.
The following definition specifies the conditions to get an immersion from a meromorphic DPW-connection with removable singularity.

Definition 3.19
.

Let
M
be a Riemann surface. Then
\Phi\in H^{0}(M,K\mathfrak{sl}(3,\mathbb{C}))
is called
regular
at
p\in M
if
\Phi_{p}^{2}\neq 0.
It is called
regular
if it is regular at all points
p\in M.

Let
D_{\lambda}=d+\eta_{\lambda}
be a meromorphic DPW-connection on
V=N\times W
with singular points at
q_{1},\dots,q_{n}.

Assume that for all
j=1,\dots,n
there is
k_{j}\in\mathbb{N}^{>0}

such that
q_{j}
is a
k_{j}
-removable singularity. Assume that
\mathrm{Res}_{\lambda=0}\eta_{\lambda}\in H^{0}(\mathring{N},K\mathfrak{sl}(3,\mathbb{C}))
is regular on
\mathring{N}
.
Further, assume that
\mathrm{Res}_{\lambda=0}(\mathrm{Ad}(G_{j}^{-1})\eta_{\lambda})
is regular at
\pi_{j}^{-1}(q_{j})
,
where
G_{j}
and the local covering
\pi_{j}
are as in the definition of a
k_{j}
-removable singularity.
Then,
\eta_{\lambda}
is called
immersive
.

A priori, being immersive (at the points
\pi_{j}^{-1}(q_{j})
) depends on the choice of
G_{j}
. That this property is actually independent of the choice
can be shown similarly to the proof of Lemma
3.17
.

To construct compact minimal Lagrangian immersions, we will use the following theorem.
The statement holds literally in the same way when replacing
\tau
by
\hat{\tau}.

Theorem 4
.

Let
D_{\lambda}=d+\eta_{\lambda}
be a
\tau
-twisted meromorphic DPW-connection on
V=N\times W
.
Assume that

(1)

each singular point
q_{j}
is a
k_{j}
-removable singularity for some
k_{j}\in\mathbb{N}^{>0};

(2)

for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
the monodromy
\rho_{\lambda}
of
D_{\lambda}
is unitary up to conjugation and irreducible;

(3)

there is
\lambda_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
such that the monodromy
\rho_{\lambda_{0}}

has finite image;

(4)

the local monodromies around the singular points
q_{j}
are of order

k_{j};

(5)

\eta_{\lambda}
is immersive.

Then, there
exists a finite degree holomorphic covering
\pi\colon M\to N
such that
\pi^{*}D_{\lambda}
is gauge equivalent on
\pi^{-1}(\mathring{N})
to the associated family
d_{\lambda}
of a minimal Lagrangian immersion
f\colon M\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}.

Furthermore,
\Gamma:=\pi_{1}(\mathring{N},z_{0})/\ker(\rho_{\lambda_{0}})
acts on
M
by deck transformations, and
f
is
\Gamma
-equivariant, i.e.
\rho_{\lambda_{0}}(\gamma^{-1})(f)=\gamma^{*}f
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma.

Proof.

By (2), we can apply Corollary
3.7
on the punctured surface
\mathring{N}
. We thus obtain a
{\bf SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D})
-gauge
b_{\lambda}
such that

\widetilde{D}_{\lambda}:=D_{\lambda}.b_{\lambda}=D+\lambda^{-1}\Psi-\lambda\Psi^{\dagger}

is a flat
{\bf SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection on
\mathring{N}\times W
; since
D_{\lambda}
is meromorphic DPW, the form
\eta^{(-1)}
is holomorphic on
\mathring{N}
, hence
\Psi=\operatorname{Ad}(b^{(0)})^{-1})\eta^{(-1)}\in\Omega^{1,0}(\mathring{N},\mathfrak{g}_{-1})
is smooth.

Consider the branched holomorphic covering
\pi\colon M\to N
specified by
\ker(\rho_{\lambda_{0}})\subset\pi_{1}(\mathring{N},z_{0})
for some fixed
z_{0}\in\mathring{N}.
In particular, by point (3), the branched covering
\pi
has finite degree. In fact, the degree is the order of the finite group
\pi_{1}(\mathring{N},z_{0})/\ker(\rho_{\lambda_{0}})
.
The map
\pi

is branched exactly over the singular points
q_{j}
. By point (4), the branch order of points lying over
q_{j}
is
k_{j}-1
.
Set
\mathring{M}=\pi^{-1}(\mathring{N})
.
By the definition of the covering, the monodromy of the
{\bf\SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
connection
\pi^{*}\widetilde{D}_{\lambda}
on
\mathring{M}
is trivial for
\lambda=\lambda_{0}.

By Conditions (1) and (4), each branch point of
\pi
is
{\bf SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D})
-apparent for
\pi^{*}D_{\lambda}
, so Theorem
3
applies.
We thus obtain
the existence of a

{\bf\SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection
\nabla_{\lambda}
on a
\SU(W)^{\tau}
-bundle
V\to M
and

\tau
-trivialization
\Upsilon\colon V_{|\mathring{M}}\to\mathring{M}\times W
such that

(3.10)

\nabla_{\lambda}=\pi^{*}\widetilde{D}_{\lambda}.\Upsilon.

Then
\nabla_{\lambda_{0}}
has trivial monodromy as well.
By point (5), the Higgs field
\Phi
of
\nabla_{\lambda}=\lambda^{-1}\Phi+D-\lambda\Phi^{\dagger}
satisfies
\Phi_{p}^{2}\neq 0
for all
p\in M.

Thus, by Lemma
3.11
we obtain a well-defined minimal Lagrangian immersion
f_{\lambda_{0}}\colon M\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}

which admits a special Legendrian lift
\hat{f}\colon M\to\mathbb{S}^{5}.

It remains to show that
f=f_{\lambda_{0}}
is
\Gamma
-equivariant.
To show this, apply Lemma
3.11
directly to
\widetilde{D}_{\lambda}
on
\mathring{N}
to
obtain a
\tilde{\rho}_{\lambda_{0}}
-equivariant minimal Lagrangian immersion
\tilde{f}\colon\widetilde{\mathring{N}}\to\mathbb{C}P^{2}
.
Because of (
3.10
), we obtain,
up to an
\mathrm{\SU}_{3}
-transformation,
\tilde{f}=f\circ\tilde{\pi},
where

(3.11)

\tilde{\pi}\colon\widetilde{\mathring{N}}\to\mathring{M}=\widetilde{\mathring{N}}/\ker(\tilde{\rho}_{\lambda_{0}})\subset M

is the induced covering. Since
\ker(\tilde{\rho}_{\lambda_{0}})\subset\pi_{1}(\mathring{N},z_{0})
is normal, the result follows.
∎

Remark 3.20
.

Theorem
4
is largely independent of the specific finite order automorphism
\tau
or
\hat{\tau}
relevant for minimal Lagrangian surfaces. More generally, the same bundle-theoretic argument applies to meromorphic DPW-connections twisted by an arbitrary finite-order automorphism
\tau
, provided one has a reconstruction result identifying the resulting
{\bf SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-family with a primitive harmonic map into the corresponding
r
-symmetric space.

In geometric situations where this primitive harmonic map encodes a distinguished surface class – such as minimal Lagrangian surfaces in the present paper, or hyperbolic affine spheres as in
[
31
]
– one must in addition impose the appropriate nondegeneracy condition ensuring that the resulting map is an immersion, equivalently that no branch points occur. In the present setting this is precisely the regularity condition
\Phi^{2}\neq 0
.

The following corollary gives a convenient reconstruction formula for later use.

Corollary 3.21
.

Let
D_{\lambda}=d+\eta_{\lambda}
be a
\tau
-twisted meromorphic DPW-connection on
N\times\mathbb{C}^{3}
satisfying the hypotheses of Theorem
4
, and set

\mathring{N}:=N\setminus\{q_{1},\dots,q_{n}\}.

Let

b_{\lambda}\colon\mathring{N}\to{\bf SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D})

be the positive gauge given by Corollary
3.7
, so that
D_{\lambda}.b_{\lambda}
is a flat
{\bf SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection on
\mathring{N}
.
Fix a lift
\hat{z}_{0}\in\widetilde{\mathring{N}}
of
z_{0}\in\mathring{N}
, and let

\Omega_{\lambda}\colon\widetilde{\mathring{N}}\to{\bf SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D}^{\times})

be the
\tau
-twisted parallel frame of
D_{\lambda}
normalized by

\Omega_{\lambda}(\hat{z}_{0})=b_{\lambda}(z_{0}).

Write its Iwasawa decomposition as

\Omega_{\lambda}=B_{\lambda}F_{\lambda},\hskip 20.00003ptB_{\lambda}\colon\widetilde{\mathring{N}}\to{\bf SL}^{\tau}_{B}(\mathbb{D}),\hskip 10.00002ptF_{\lambda}\colon\widetilde{\mathring{N}}\to{\bf SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1}).

Then
B_{\lambda}
is single-valued on
\mathring{N}
(more precisely, after pullback to
\widetilde{\mathring{N}}
one has
B_{\lambda}=b_{\lambda}
).
Moreover, if
\pi\colon M\to N
is the branched covering from Theorem
4
and
\mathring{M}:=\pi^{-1}(\mathring{N})
, then
F_{\lambda_{0}}
has trivial monodromy on
\mathring{M}
. The reconstructed minimal Lagrangian immersion is given, up to an ambient
\SU_{3}
-transformation, by

(f_{\lambda_{0}})_{|\mathring{M}}=F_{\lambda_{0}}^{-1}(\mathbb{C}e_{2}).

If one works with the conjugated symmetry
\hat{\tau}
, then
\mathbb{C}e_{2}
is replaced by
\mathbb{C}e_{1}
; in particular, the special Legendrian lift is

(3.12)

\hat{f}_{\lambda_{0}}=F_{\lambda_{0}}^{-1}(e_{1}).

Proof.

We identify
b_{\lambda}
with its pullback to
\widetilde{\mathring{N}}
, and set

U_{\lambda}:=b_{\lambda}^{-1}\Omega_{\lambda}.

Then
U_{\lambda}
is a parallel
{\bf SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D}^{\times})
-frame of the flat
{\bf SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connection

D_{\lambda}.b_{\lambda}

on
\widetilde{\mathring{N}}
, and by the normalization of
\Omega_{\lambda}
we have

U_{\lambda}(\hat{z}_{0})=\Id.

Since
D_{\lambda}.b_{\lambda}
is unitary, it follows that
U_{\lambda}\in{\bf SU}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
pointwise on
\widetilde{\mathring{N}}
. Hence

\Omega_{\lambda}=b_{\lambda}U_{\lambda}

is already an Iwasawa decomposition of
\Omega_{\lambda}
. By uniqueness of the Iwasawa decomposition,
i.e.

B_{\lambda}=b_{\lambda},
and
F_{\lambda}=U_{\lambda}.

In particular,
B_{\lambda}
is single-valued on
\mathring{N}
.
Since
B_{\lambda}
is single-valued,
F_{\lambda}
has the same monodromy as
\Omega_{\lambda}
, namely the monodromy of
D_{\lambda}
. By construction of the covering
\pi\colon M\to N
in Theorem
4
, this monodromy is trivial at
\lambda=\lambda_{0}
on
\mathring{M}
. Thus
F_{\lambda_{0}}
descends to
\mathring{M}
.

The reconstruction formula now follows from Remark
3.12
; in the
\hat{\tau}
-twisted normalization one uses Remark
3.13
. This gives

(f_{\lambda_{0}})_{|\mathring{M}}=F_{\lambda_{0}}^{-1}(\mathbb{C}e_{2}),

and in the
\hat{\tau}
-case the special Legendrian lift is
F_{\lambda_{0}}^{-1}(e_{1})
.
∎

3.6.
The energy of harmonic maps in terms of meromorphic potentials

We conclude the general discussion by deriving a residue formula for the energy of a harmonic map from a Riemann surface into a symmetric space which is obtained from a meromorphic DPW-connection.
Similar formulas have been obtained for minimal CMC surfaces in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
, see
[
26
, Theorem 8]
and
[
29
, Corollary 4.3]
, and for harmonic maps to
hyperbolic 3-space
[
2
,
30
]
.
In the special case of the primitive harmonic map associated with a minimal Lagrangian immersion, this combines with (
2.23
) to give an area formula in terms of the residues of the potential.

Let
\Sigma
be a compact Riemann surface, and let

D_{\lambda}=d+\xi_{\lambda},\hskip 20.00003pt\xi_{\lambda}=\sum_{j\geq-1}\lambda^{j}\xi^{(j)}

be a meromorphic DPW-connection on the trivial bundle
\Sigma\times W
with singularities in
p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\in\Sigma
.
Set

\mathring{\Sigma}:=\Sigma\setminus\{p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\}.

Assume that there exists a gauge

B_{\lambda}\colon\mathring{\Sigma}\to{\bf SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D})

such that

\nabla_{\lambda}:=D_{\lambda}.B_{\lambda}

is the associated family of flat connections of a (possibly equivariant) harmonic map
\psi
.
In a
\tau
-trivialization of the corresponding
\SU(W)^{\tau}
-bundle, we may write

\nabla_{\lambda}=d+\lambda^{-1}\omega^{(-1)}+\omega^{(0)}+\lambda\omega^{(1)},

and obtain

(3.13)

E(\psi)=i\int_{\Sigma}\tr(\omega^{(-1)}\wedge\omega^{(1)}).

Theorem 5
.

Assume that for every singular point
p_{k}
there exists a gauge

g_{k}=g_{k}^{(0)}+\lambda g_{k}^{(1)}+O(\lambda^{2})\colon U_{k}\to{\bf SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D})

defined on a punctured neighbourhood
U_{k}
of
p_{k}
such that

D_{\lambda}.g_{k}

extends smoothly across
p_{k}
. Then

(3.14)

E(\psi)=2\pi\sum_{k=1}^{n}\Res_{p_{k}}\tr\bigl((g_{k}^{(1)}(g_{k}^{(0)})^{-1})\,\xi^{(-1)}\bigr).

Proof.

Choose disjoint coordinate discs
U_{k}\subset\Sigma
around the poles
p_{k}
. After shrinking
U_{k}
if necessary, choose a smooth gauge

g\colon\mathring{\Sigma}\to{\bf SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D})

which agrees with
g_{k}
near
p_{k}
for every
k
and is smooth on
\mathring{\Sigma}
away from the poles. Write

B_{\lambda}=g_{\lambda}\,b_{\lambda},

where
b_{\lambda}\colon\Sigma\to{\bf SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{D})
. Then

D_{\lambda}.g_{\lambda}=\nabla_{\lambda}.b_{\lambda}^{-1}

is a DPW-connection on
\Sigma
.
Write

g_{\lambda}=g^{(0)}+\lambda g^{(1)}+\lambda^{2}g^{(2)}+O(\lambda^{3})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003ptg_{\lambda}=(\Id+\lambda X+\lambda^{2}Y+O(\lambda^{3}))\,g^{(0)},

with

X=g^{(1)}(g^{(0)})^{-1}
and
Y=g^{(2)}(g^{(0)})^{-1}.

Then

D_{\lambda}.g_{\lambda}=\Bigl(\lambda^{-1}\xi^{(-1)}+d+\xi^{(0)}+[\xi^{(-1)},X]+\lambda\bigl(\xi^{(1)}+dX+[\xi^{(0)},X]+[\xi^{(-1)},Y]\bigr)+O(\lambda^{2})\Bigr).g^{(0)}

from which we obtain

\omega^{(-1)}=\Ad_{(g^{(0)})^{-1}}\xi^{(-1)}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{
and}\hskip 20.00003pt\omega^{(1)}=\Ad_{(g^{(0)})^{-1}}\bigl(\xi^{(1)}+dX+[\xi^{(0)},X]+[\xi^{(-1)},Y]\bigr).

Using invariance of the trace, the fact that
\xi^{(-1)}
is a meromorphic
(1,0)
-form, and the coefficient of
\lambda^{-1}
in the flatness equation

d\xi_{\lambda}+\xi_{\lambda}\wedge\xi_{\lambda}=0,

namely

d\xi^{(-1)}+[\xi^{(0)}\wedge\xi^{(-1)}]=0,

we obtain

\begin{split}\tr(\omega^{(-1)}\wedge\omega^{(1)})&=\tr\bigl(\xi^{(-1)}\wedge(\xi^{(1)}+dX+[\xi^{(0)},X]+[\xi^{(-1)},Y])\bigr)=\tr(\xi^{(-1)}\wedge dX)=-\,d\,\tr(X\,\xi^{(-1)}).\end{split}

Therefore (
3.13
) gives

E(\psi)=-i\int_{\mathring{\Sigma}}d\,\tr\bigl(g^{(1)}(g^{(0)})^{-1}\xi^{(-1)}\bigr).

Applying Stokes’ theorem on
\Sigma\setminus\bigcup_{k}U_{k}
and the residue theorem yields (
3.14
)
as claimed.
∎

4.
The relative character variety

4.1.
Motivation

In the previous section, we explained how to construct minimal Lagrangian immersions
f\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
from meromorphic
{\bf\SL}^{\tau}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-connections
d_{\lambda}=d+\eta_{\lambda}
which are required to be
unitarizable for each
\lambda\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
. To achieve this, we start with highly symmetric Riemann surfaces, namely the Fermat curves
\Sigma_{k}
given by the smooth plane algebraic curve

\Sigma_{k}:=\{[x,y,z]\in\mathbb{CP}^{2}\mid x^{k}+\zeta y^{k}+\zeta^{2}z^{k}=0\}

of genus
\frac{1}{2}(k-1)(k-2)
, where
\zeta=\exp(2\pi i/3)
. Consider the automorphisms

(4.1)

\begin{split}\varphi_{1}&\colon[x,y,z]\mapsto[\exp(\tfrac{2\pi i}{k})x,y,z]\\
\varphi_{2}&\colon[x,y,z]\mapsto[x,\exp(\tfrac{2\pi i}{k})y,z]\\
\sigma&\colon[x,y,z]\mapsto[y,z,x].\\
\end{split}

A standard Riemann–Hurwitz calculation shows the quotient map
\Sigma_{k}\to\Sigma_{k}/\langle\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\rangle\cong\mathbb{CP}^{1}
is a
k^{2}
-fold covering with three branch values.
The automorphism
\sigma
naturally acts on the quotient
\Sigma_{k}/\langle\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\rangle
and permutes the three branch values.
Thus, the full quotient
\Sigma_{k}\to\mathbb{CP}^{1}
is a
3k^{2}
-fold covering with three branch values at
0,1,\infty
of branch orders
2,k-1,2
respectively.

Let
t\in(0,\tfrac{1}{3})
. Consider the subgroup
\Gamma_{t}\subset\SU_{3}
generated by

(4.2)

\begin{split}M_{0}&:=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&1\\
1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
\end{pmatrix},\quad M_{1}:=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&e^{-2i\pi t}&0\\
0&0&e^{2i\pi t}\\
\end{pmatrix},\quad M_{\infty}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&e^{2i\pi t}&0\\
0&0&e^{-2i\pi t}\\
1&0&0\\
\end{pmatrix}\end{split}

satisfying
M_{\infty}M_{1}M_{0}=\Id.
For
t=1/k
their orders are
3,k,
and
3
, respectively, and the subgroup
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1/k}
is finite (see for example
[
57
]
).
Let

\Lambda
be the kernel of the
representation

(4.3)

\rho\colon\pi_{1}(S,z_{0})\to\SU_{3}\subset\SL_{3}(\mathbb{C});\hskip 10.00002pt\gamma_{0}\mapsto M_{0},\,\gamma_{1}\mapsto M_{1}

of the fundamental group of the thrice-punctured sphere
S=\mathbb{CP}^{1}\setminus\{0,1,\infty\}
with natural generators
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}
(see (
4.6
) below).
Then

\Gamma_{deck}\,\cong\,\pi_{1}(S,z_{0})/\Lambda\cong\langle\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\sigma\rangle

acts on
\Sigma_{k}

from the right
by holomorphic automorphisms such that

(4.4)

\pi\colon\Sigma_{k}\to\Sigma_{k}/\Gamma_{deck}=\mathbb{CP}^{1}

is a branched covering
with branch points at
0,1
and
\infty
and branch orders
2,k-1,2
, respectively.

By the Riemann existence theorem (see, e.g.,
[
13
, Chapter 4]
)
the compact Riemann surface is uniquely specified by the fact that the (topological) covering

\pi\colon\mathring{\Sigma}_{k}:=\Sigma_{k}\setminus\pi^{-1}(\{0,1,\infty\})\to S

has monodromy
\rho.
Moreover, the fundamental group of
\mathring{\Sigma}_{k}
is isomorphic to
\Lambda
.
Let
\hat{z}_{0}
be a lift of
z_{0},
i.e.
\pi(\hat{z}_{0})=z_{0}.

There is a natural surjective homomorphism
i:\Lambda\to\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{k},\hat{z}_{0})
induced by the inclusion
\mathring{\Sigma}_{k}\to\Sigma_{k}
. Its kernel is generated by the simple loops around the branch points
\Sigma_{k}\setminus\mathring{\Sigma}_{k}
. We have the following classical fact, which we state as a lemma.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
\tilde{\rho}\colon\pi_{1}(S,z_{0})\to\SL_{3}(\mathbb{C})
be a representation of the thrice-punctured sphere into
\SL_{3}(\mathbb{C})
such that the local conjugacy classes of
\tilde{\rho}
are the same as those of
\rho
. Then
\tilde{\rho}
yields, up to conjugation, a unique representation

\hat{\rho}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{k},\hat{z}_{0})\to\SL_{3}(\mathbb{C})

such that

(4.5)

\hat{\rho}\circ i=\tilde{\rho}|_{\Lambda}.

The representation of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{k})
corresponding to
\rho
in (
4.3
) is the trivial representation.

Proof.

Since
\tilde{\rho}|_{\Lambda}
vanishes on the simple curves around the removed points
p\in\Sigma_{k}\setminus\mathring{\Sigma}_{k}
, the existence of
\hat{\rho}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{k},\hat{z}_{0})\to\SL_{3}(\mathbb{C})
satisfying (
4.5
) is clear.
The final statement, namely that the lift of the representation
\rho
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{k})
is trivial, follows directly from (
4.4
).
∎

4.2.
Relative character varieties

Let
\Sigma_{g,n}
be a surface of genus
g
with
n
punctures. If
n
is at least one, then its fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g,n})
is a free group on
2g+n-1
generators. Let
G=\SL_{3}(\mathbb{C})
, and consider the representation variety Hom
(\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g,n}),G)
consisting of group homomorphisms of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g,n})
to the group
G
.
This is identified with the product
G^{2g+n-1}
.
For a fixed element
g\in G
, define
\mathcal{C}(g)
to consist of all elements
h\in G
so that the closures of the conjugacy orbits of
g
and
h
have nonempty intersection. If
\gamma_{i}
denotes a peripheral curve oriented counterclockwise around the puncture
p_{i}
and if
h_{i}
is an element of
G
, then the relative representation variety is the subset

\mathrm{Hom}^{rel}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g,n}),G):=\{\rho\in\text{Hom}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g,n}),G):\rho(\gamma_{i})\in\mathcal{C}(h_{i})\}.

There is a natural action by conjugation on the space, whose quotient is not necessarily Hausdorff. The relative character variety then is

\chi^{rel}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g,n}),G):=\mathrm{Hom}^{rel}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g,n}),G)\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}G,

where
\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}G
is the so-called GIT quotient that identifies two representations
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
whenever the closures of their orbits under conjugation intersect. The resulting space is thus the largest Hausdorff quotient of the relative representation variety. In the situations relevant here, the representations are generically irreducible, and hence their conjugation orbits are closed.

\gamma_{0}
\gamma_{1}
\gamma_{\infty}

Figure 1.
Curves representing the homotopy classes
\gamma_{0}
,
\gamma_{1},
and
\gamma_{\infty}.

We take our underlying Riemann surface to be the thrice-punctured sphere
S=\mathbb{CP}^{1}\setminus\{0,1,\infty\}=\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,1\}
. Choosing the base point
z_{0}=\tfrac{1}{2}
,
we choose representatives
\gamma_{j}
of the homotopy classes in
S
by the following closed curves.

(4.6)

\begin{split}&\gamma_{0}\colon[0,1)\mapsto\tfrac{1}{2}e^{2\pi is}\,,\qquad\gamma_{1}\colon[0,1)\mapsto 1-\tfrac{1}{2}e^{2\pi is}\,\\
&\gamma_{\infty}=\begin{cases}\tfrac{1}{2}+4is:\quad s\in[0,\tfrac{1}{4}]\\
\tfrac{1}{2}+e^{-4\pi is-\tfrac{\pi i}{2}}:\quad s\in[\tfrac{1}{4},\tfrac{3}{4}]\\
\tfrac{1}{2}+4i(1-s):\quad s\in[\tfrac{3}{4},1]\end{cases},\\
\end{split}

so that
\gamma_{\infty}*\gamma_{1}*\gamma_{0}=\Id

is the trivial loop, as shown in Figure
1
.
We will use the convention that concatenation
(\beta*\alpha)(s)
is given by
\alpha(2s)
for
s\leq\tfrac{1}{2}
and
\beta(2s-1)
if
s\in[\tfrac{1}{2},1]
, so that the fundamental group acts from the right on the universal covering, and the monodromy of a connection is a representation of the fundamental group.

Motivated by the fact that (
4.2
) describes the covering monodromy of the Fermat curves,
we fix the following three conjugacy classes for the local monodromies
M_{0},M_{1},M_{\infty}
of
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\gamma_{\infty}
, respectively, satisfying

(4.7)

\begin{split}&M_{1}\in\mathcal{C}(\text{diag}(1,\exp(2\pi it),\exp(-2\pi it))),\quad M_{0},M_{\infty}\in\mathcal{C}(\text{diag}(\zeta,1,1/\zeta))\\
&M_{\infty}M_{1}M_{0}=\Id\end{split}

where
t\in(0,\tfrac{1}{3}).
As each local monodromy has distinct eigenvalues, the conjugacy orbits are themselves closed.

Fixing the conjugacy classes of
M_{0}
,
M_{1}
, and
M_{\infty}
–
which are determined by their traces, determinants, and the traces of their squares – we consider their
global trace coordinates

(4.8)

\begin{split}X&:=\tr(M_{1}M_{0}^{-1}),\qquad Y=\tr(M_{1}^{-1}M_{0}),\qquad\text{and}\qquad Z=\tr(M_{1}M_{0}M_{1}^{-1}M_{0}^{-1}).\\
\end{split}

By applying the formulas of Lawton
[
38
,
37
]
with the conjugacy classes as in (
4.7
), the global traces
satisfy
P(X,Y,Z)=0
, where
P
is
Lawton’s polynomial

(4.9)

\begin{split}P:=&\,\,2\,(2\cos(2\pi t)+\cos(4\pi t))(XY-Z)+4\cos(6\pi t)\\
&+X^{3}-XY(Z+3)+Y^{3}+Z^{2}+5.\end{split}

This equation can also be obtained by a case-by-case analysis using elementary linear algebra; see, e.g.,
[
31
]
for a related case on a three-punctured sphere with different local conjugacy classes. Lawton shows these global traces determine points on the relative character variety.
Thus

F=\{(X,Y,Z)\in\mathbb{C}^{3}\mid\;P(X,Y,Z)=0\}

is naturally identified with the relative character variety. In what follows, we need to determine the irreducible representations that are unitarizable. A first step is the following lemma.

Lemma 4.2
.

For
M_{0},M_{1},M_{\infty}
and
X,Y
as above, consider
the quadratic polynomial (
4.9
)
in the variable
Z
. Its two zeros are given by

Z=\tr(M_{1}M_{0}M_{1}^{-1}M_{0}^{-1})\hskip 10.00002pt\mathrm{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\tilde{Z}=\tr(M_{0}M_{1}M_{0}^{-1}M_{1}^{-1}).

Proof.

This follows by explicit inspection of all possible cases, or by a direct computation using
[
37
, Equation 3.1.1]
.
∎

Example 4.3
.

Let
t=1/k
for
k\in\mathbb{N}^{>3}.

The representation
\rho
given by (
4.3
)
with finite image
\Gamma
has trace coordinates

X=Y=0\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptZ=2\exp(-2\pi it)+\exp(4\pi it).

Note that the contragredient representation
\tilde{\rho}=(\rho^{-1})^{T}
has trace coordinates
X=Y=0
and
\tilde{Z}=2\exp(2\pi it)+\exp(-4\pi it).

It generates a finite group
\tilde{\Gamma}\leq\SU_{3}
which is isomorphic to
\Gamma
even though
\rho
and
\tilde{\rho}

are not conjugate. Furthermore

\ker(\rho\colon\pi_{1}(S,z_{0})\to\SU_{3})=\ker((\rho^{-1})^{T}\colon\pi_{1}(S,z_{0})\to\SU_{3}),

and both representations become trivial on the same covering surface (
4.4
).

4.3.
Reducible and irreducible representations

Let

\rho
be a reducible representation with

M_{0}=\rho(\gamma_{0})
and
M_{1}=\rho(\gamma_{1}).

We want to determine its trace coordinates.

The first case is that of a common eigenline of
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
. Let
0<t<\tfrac{1}{3}
.
Since
M_{\infty}M_{1}M_{0}=\Id
, the product of the three eigenvalues along a common eigenline is 1.
This implies that the eigenvalues on this eigenline are either all 1, or the eigenvalue of
M_{0}
is
\zeta^{\pm 1}
, of
M_{1}
is 1, and of
M_{\infty}
is
\zeta^{\mp 1},
where
\zeta=\exp(\tfrac{2\pi i}{3}).

We first diagonalize
M_{0}
. By a direct computation, up to conjugation, this leads in the first case to

\begin{split}M_{0}&=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&\zeta&0\\
0&0&\zeta^{-1}\\
\end{pmatrix}\qquad\text{and}\quad M_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}1&b_{11}&b_{12}\\
0&\left(1-\frac{i}{\sqrt{3}}\right)\cos(2\pi t)+\frac{i}{\sqrt{3}}&\frac{1}{3}(-4)\sin^{2}(\pi t)(2\cos(2\pi t)+1)\\
0&1&\left(1+\frac{i}{\sqrt{3}}\right)\cos(2\pi t)-\frac{i}{\sqrt{3}}\\
\end{pmatrix}\end{split}

for some
b_{11},b_{12}\in\mathbb{C}
.
The trace coordinates are

(4.10)

\begin{split}X_{1}&=4\sin^{2}(\pi t),\qquad Y_{1}=4\sin^{2}(\pi t),\qquad\text{and}\qquad Z_{1}=-2\cos(2\pi t)+2\cos(4\pi t)+3=\tilde{Z}_{1}.\\
\end{split}

The other two cases yield the trace coordinates
(X_{2},Y_{2},Z_{2})
and
(X_{3},Y_{3},Z_{3})
with

(4.11)

\begin{split}X_{2}&=-2i\left(\sqrt{3}-i\right)\sin^{2}(\pi t)=Y_{3}\\
Y_{2}&=2i\left(\sqrt{3}+i\right)\sin^{2}(\pi t)=X_{3}\\
Z_{2}=\tilde{Z}_{2}&=-2\cos(2\pi t)+2\cos(4\pi t)+3=Z_{3}=\tilde{Z}_{3}.\\
\end{split}

In the case of a two-dimensional invariant subspace of the representation generated by
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
, we obtain analogously (or by looking at the inverse transpose representation)
the same possible traces as in the case of a 1-dimensional invariant subspace.

Note that the three points given by (
4.10
) and (
4.11
)
are precisely the singular points of the relative character variety
F.

We will denote by

F^{*}=F\setminus\{(X_{1},Y_{1},Z_{1}),(X_{2},Y_{2},Z_{2}),(X_{3},Y_{3},Z_{3})\}

the set of nonsingular points, so that
F^{*}
corresponds to irreducible representations only.

The following theorem is a special case of
[
38
, Theorem 8]
(see also
[
37
]
).
It can be proved by elementary methods, e.g. as in
[
31
, Theorem 4 (2)]
for a different relative character variety for the three-punctured sphere.
Note that the “only if” part of the first part of the following theorem was just proven above.

Theorem 6
.

[
38
, Theorem 8]

A representation with
M_{0},M_{1},M_{\infty}
satisfying (
4.7
)
is reducible if and only if the trace coordinates are given by (
4.10
) or (
4.11
).

For every
p=(X,Y,Z)\in F
, there exists a representation of the fundamental group of the thrice-punctured sphere
with prescribed local conjugacy classes (
4.7
) and global traces
X,

Y,
and
Z.

Moreover, two irreducible representations have the same traces
X,Y,Z

if and only if they are conjugate to each other.

As a consequence of the preceding Theorem, we do not distinguish between points in
F^{*}
and conjugacy classes of irreducible representations.

4.4.
The unitary component

To solve the intrinsic closing conditions for minimal Lagrangian surfaces, we derive conditions on
(X,Y,Z)\in F
corresponding to unitarizable representations.

For any
A\in\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{C})
with 1 as an eigenvalue, we have

\tr(A)=\tr(A^{-1}).

Thus, a unitarizable representation given by
M_{0},M_{1},M_{\infty}\in\SU_{3}

satisfying (
4.7
)
also satisfies for all
k=1,2
and
j=0,1,\infty

\begin{split}\tr(M_{j}^{k})=\tr(\bar{M}_{j}^{k})=\tr((\bar{M}_{j}^{T})^{-k}).\\
\end{split}

In particular, all the
local traces
are real.
Additionally, we have

Lemma 4.4
.

If a representation is unitarizable then its trace coordinates satisfy

(4.12)

\bar{X}=Y\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\tilde{Z}=\bar{Z}

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume that all matrices are unitary. Then

\begin{split}X=&\tr(M_{1}M_{0}^{-1})=\tr((\bar{M}^{T}_{1})^{-1}\bar{M}^{T}_{0})=\tr(\bar{M}_{0}(\bar{M}_{1})^{-1})=\bar{Y},\end{split}

and analogously for
Z
and
\tilde{Z}.

∎

Note that the polynomial
P(X,Y,Z)
(
4.9
) in the variable
Z
has real coefficients if
\bar{X}=Y
. Therefore,
Lemma
4.4
implies that the discriminant

(4.13)

\begin{split}D:=\,\,&(4\cos(2\pi t)+2\cos(4\pi t)+XY)^{2}\\
&-4\left(2XY(2\cos(2\pi t)+\cos(4\pi t))+4\cos(6\pi t)+X^{3}-3XY+Y^{3}+5\right)\\
\end{split}

of the quadratic polynomial
P
in
Z

must be nonpositive in order to have a unitarizable representation.
We
denote by
F^{\mathbb{R}}
the set of points satisfying (
4.12
), i.e.,

F^{\mathbb{R}}=\{(X,Y,Z)\in F\mid\bar{X}=Y,\bar{Z}=\tilde{Z}\}

where
\tilde{Z}
is uniquely determined by
(X,Y,Z)
by Lemma
4.2
.

Lemma 4.5
.

For a connected component of the set of nonsingular real points
(X,Y,Z)\in F^{\mathbb{R}}\cap F^{*}
,
either all or none of the elements in that component correspond to unitarizable representations.

Proof.

Using Lemma
4.2
, we obtain from Theorem
6
that
the representation
(\bar{\rho}^{T})^{-1}

determined by

\tilde{M}_{j}:=(\bar{M}_{j}^{T})^{-1}
,
j=0,1,\infty
,
is conjugate to
\rho.
Thus, there is a (unique up to scale by a third root of unity) conjugator
C\in\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{C})
satisfying

C^{-1}\rho C=(\bar{\rho}^{T})^{-1}.

Applying this relation twice we get

C(\bar{C}^{T})^{-1}=\hat{\zeta}\,\Id\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ with}\hskip 10.00002pt\hat{\zeta}^{3}=1.

After scaling with a third root of unity, we can assume without loss of generality that

C=\bar{C}^{T}

is Hermitian. The signature of the associated quadratic form is either
(3,0)
or
(1,2).
In the first case,

C
is positive definite and can be written as
D\bar{D}^{T}.
Then, conjugating yields a unitary representation
D^{-1}\rho D.

The Hermitian choice
C
is unique, hence it depends continuously along paths in a real component of irreducible representations, and the signature does not change. For a unitarizable representation, the signature is
(3,0)
.
∎

To analyze the different connected components of
F^{\mathbb{R}}
we substitute

(4.14)

X=A+iB,\hskip 10.00002ptY=A-iB\hskip 20.00003pt\text{with}\hskip 10.00002ptA,B\in\mathbb{R}.

Then, the discriminant
(
4.13
) is a real polynomial of degree 4

(4.15)

\begin{split}D^{r}=&-8\cos(2\pi t)\left(A^{2}+B^{2}-1\right)-4\cos(4\pi t)\left(A^{2}+B^{2}-2\right)-8\cos(6\pi t)+2\cos(8\pi t)\\
&+A^{4}-8A^{3}+12A^{2}+2(A(A+12)+6)B^{2}+B^{4}-10.\end{split}

For a given real
A
, there are at most four real solutions of
D^{r}=0
in
B
, counted with multiplicity. They are given by

\begin{split}B_{1}=&\sqrt{-4H+4\cos(2\pi t)+2\cos(4\pi t)-A^{2}-12A-6}\\
B_{2}=&\sqrt{4H+4\cos(2\pi t)+2\cos(4\pi t)-A^{2}-12A-6}\\
B_{3}=&-\sqrt{-4H+4\cos(2\pi t)+2\cos(4\pi t)-A^{2}-12A-6}\\
B_{4}=&-\sqrt{4H+4\cos(2\pi t)+2\cos(4\pi t)-A^{2}-12A-6},\\
\end{split}

where
H:=\sqrt{(-\cos(2\pi t)+A+1)^{2}(4\cos(2\pi t)+2A+5)}.

From this, one sees that
there are four different connected components inside
F^{\mathbb{R}}\cap F^{*}
, see Figure
2
.
Their closures are obtained by adding the three singular points of
F
. Explicitly, these are given by the following four closed and connected sets

(4.16)

\begin{split}\mathcal{C}_{-1}^{+}&:=\{(A+iB,A-iB,Z)\in F\mid(A,B)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},-2\cos(2\pi t)-\frac{5}{2}\leq A\leq\cos(2\pi t)-1,\;B_{1}\leq B\leq B_{2}\}\\
\mathcal{C}_{-1}^{-}&:=\{(A+iB,A-iB,Z)\in F\mid(A,B)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},-2\cos(2\pi t)-\frac{5}{2}\leq A\leq\cos(2\pi t)-1,\;B_{3}\leq B\leq B_{4}\}\\
\mathcal{C}_{+}&:=\{(A+iB,A-iB,Z)\in F\mid(A,B)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},4\sin^{2}(\pi t)\leq A\leq 8\cos^{2}\left(\frac{\pi t}{2}\right)\cos(\pi t),\;B_{4}\leq B\leq B_{2}\}.\end{split}

and

(4.17)

\begin{split}\mathcal{C}_{u}&:=\{(A+iB,A-iB,Z)\in F\mid(A,B)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\cos(2\pi t)-1\leq A\leq-8\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\pi t}{2}\right)\cos(\pi t),\;B_{1}\leq B\leq B_{2}\}\\
&\cup\{(A+iB,A-iB,Z)\in F\mid(A,B)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\cos(2\pi t)-1\leq A\leq-8\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\pi t}{2}\right)\cos(\pi t),\;B_{4}\leq B\leq B_{3}\}\\
&\cup\{(A+iB,A-iB,Z)\in F\mid(A,B)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},-8\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\pi t}{2}\right)\cos(\pi t)\leq A\leq 4\sin^{2}(\pi t),\;B_{4}\leq B\leq B_{2}\}.\\
\end{split}

Figure 2.
The vanishing locus of
D^{r}
, for
t=1/6
and
t=1/12
. The unitary component
\mathcal{C}_{u}
is the small component in the center.

The intersections of these components consist of singular points of
F
(i.e., corresponding to reducible representations) only.
Furthermore, if

(X,Y,Z)\in\mathcal{C}\subset F^{\mathbb{R}}
is contained in some real component
\mathcal{C}
, then automatically
(X,Y,\tilde{Z})\in\mathcal{C}\subset F^{\mathbb{R}}
.

We are now able to characterize the relevant unitarizable representations through their trace coordinates.

Theorem 7
.

Let
0<t<\tfrac{1}{3},
and let
\mathcal{C}_{u}\subset F^{\mathbb{R}}
be as in (
4.17
).
Then
\mathcal{C}_{u}

is a connected component
which consists of unitarizable
representations only.

Proof.

The representation
given by (
4.3
)
is obviously unitary. It has trace coordinates
X=0=Y
and
Z=2e^{-2i\pi t}+e^{4i\pi t}
is therefore contained in
\mathcal{C}_{u}
. By Theorem
6
,
\rho
is irreducible.
By Lemma
4.5
,
\mathcal{C}_{u}
entirely consists of unitarizable representations.
∎

4.5.
Derivative of trace coordinates at
t=0

We will make use of the following lemma.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
\epsilon>0
, and, for
j=0,1,\infty
, let

M_{j}\colon[0,\epsilon)\to\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{C})

be smooth such that

M_{\infty}M_{1}M_{0}=\Id.

Assume that for all
t
,
M_{0}(t)
and
M_{\infty}(t)
are both conjugate to

\text{diag}(1,\zeta,\zeta^{-1}).

Moreover, assume

(4.18)

M_{0}(0)=\text{diag}(1,\zeta,\zeta^{-1})\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ and}\hskip 10.00002ptM_{1}(0)=\Id.

Let

X(t)=\tr(M_{1}(t)M_{0}(t)^{-1})
,
Y(t)=\tr(M_{0}(t)M_{1}(t)^{-1})

and

M^{\prime}_{1}(0)=\begin{pmatrix}p_{i\,j}\end{pmatrix}_{i,j}.

Then we have

(4.19)

\begin{split}X(0)&=Y(0)=0,\quad\quad X^{\prime}(0)=Y^{\prime}(0)=0\\
X^{\prime\prime}(0)&=-\zeta\,p_{12}\,p_{21}-\zeta^{-1}\,p_{13}\,p_{31}-p_{23}\,p_{32}\\
Y^{\prime\prime}(0)&=-\zeta^{-1}\,p_{12}\,p_{21}-\zeta\,p_{13}\,p_{31}-p_{23}\,p_{32}.\\
\end{split}

Proof.

That
X(0)=0=Y(0)
directly follows from (
4.18
).
For the remaining formulas, we first consider the case where
M_{0}(t)=M_{0}(0)
is constant in time.
From the conditions on the conjugacy class of
M_{\infty}(t)=(M_{1}(t)M_{0}(t))^{-1}
, we obtain
the equations

\tr(M_{1}M_{0})^{\prime}(0)=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\tr(M_{1}M_{0}M_{1}M_{0})^{\prime}(0)=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\tr(M_{1}M_{0}M_{1}M_{0}M_{1}M_{0})^{\prime}(0)=0.

This is a nondegenerate homogeneous linear system on
p_{11},p_{22},p_{33}
, i.e.,

(4.20)

0=p_{11}=p_{22}=p_{33}.

By direct computation, one then obtains

X^{\prime}(0)=0=Y^{\prime}(0).

Let
M^{\prime\prime}_{1}(0)=\begin{pmatrix}q_{i\,j}\end{pmatrix}_{i,j}.

From

\tr(M_{1}M_{0})^{\prime\prime}=0=\tr(M_{1}M_{0}M_{1}M_{0})^{\prime\prime}

we obtain (using (
4.20
))

(4.21)

\begin{split}q_{11}+\zeta\,q_{22}+\zeta^{-1}\,q_{33}&=0\\
q_{11}+\zeta^{-1}\,q_{22}+\zeta\,q_{33}&=-\zeta\,p_{12}p_{21}-\zeta^{-1}\,p_{13}p_{31}-p_{23}p_{32}.\end{split}

Plugging (
4.21
) into

X^{\prime\prime}(0)=q_{11}+\zeta^{-1}\,q_{22}+\zeta\,q_{33}

one obtains (
4.19
). Similarly one can derive the formula for
Y^{\prime\prime}(0).

The general case

M_{0}(t)=C(t)M_{0}(0)C^{-1}(t)

is simply reduced to the special case by conjugating with
C(t)
: Using
M_{1}(0)=\Id
and the Leibniz rule we obtain

\frac{d}{dt}_{\mid t=0}(C^{-1}(t)M_{1}(t)C(t))=M_{1}^{\prime}(0).

Therefore, (
4.19
) is true in general.
∎

5.
Construction of unitarizable DPW potentials

In this section we construct, for sufficiently small
t\geq 0
, a family of Fuchsian potentials on the thrice-punctured sphere whose gauged DPW potentials satisfy the hypotheses of Theorem
4
.
The monodromy will be unitarizable for every
\lambda\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
; the local conjugacy classes are prescribed so that, after pullback to the relevant covering, the singularities become removable; and at one Sym point
\lambda_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
the pulled-back monodromy becomes trivial.

5.1.
Basic definitions

Fix
r>1
and let
\xi=\exp(\pi i/3)
.
Denote by

\mathcal{W}:=\{h\colon\mathbb{D}_{r}\to\mathbb{C}\mid h\text{ is holomorphic}\}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\mathring{\mathcal{W}}:=\{h\colon\mathbb{A}_{r}\to\mathbb{C}\mid h\text{ is holomorphic}\}

the Banach spaces of bounded holomorphic functions on the disk
\mathbb{D}_{r}:=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\mid|\lambda|<r\}

and on the annulus
\mathbb{A}_{r}:=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\mid\tfrac{1}{r}<|\lambda|<r\}
, respectively.
Elements of
\mathcal{W}
and
\mathring{\mathcal{W}}
restrict to real-analytic functions on
\mathbb{S}^{1}\subset\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.
Define the bounded (real) linear maps

(5.1)

\begin{split}(.)^{+}&\colon\mathring{\mathcal{W}}\to\mathcal{W};\quad f=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}f^{(k)}\lambda^{k}\mapsto\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{>0}}f^{(k)}\lambda^{k}.\\
(.)^{*}&\colon\mathring{\mathcal{W}}\to\mathring{\mathcal{W}};\quad f=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}f^{(k)}\lambda^{k}\mapsto\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\bar{f}^{(k)}\lambda^{-k}.\end{split}

Clearly,
f\in\mathring{\mathcal{W}}
is real along
\mathbb{S}^{1}
if and only if
f^{*}=f.
Set

\begin{split}&\mathring{\mathcal{W}}^{\tau}:=\{f\in\mathring{\mathcal{W}}\mid f(\xi\lambda)=f(\lambda)\ \text{for all}\ \lambda\in\mathbb{A}_{r}\},\qquad\quad\mathcal{W}^{\tau}:=\mathcal{W}\cap\mathring{\mathcal{W}}^{\tau},\\
&\mathring{\mathcal{W}}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}:=\{f\in\mathring{\mathcal{W}}^{\tau}\mid\overline{f(\bar{\lambda})}=f(\lambda)\ \text{for all}\ \lambda\in\mathbb{A}_{r}\},\qquad\quad\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}:=\mathcal{W}\cap\mathring{\mathcal{W}}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}.\end{split}

These spaces are (real) closed subspaces of
\mathring{\mathcal{W}}
respectively
\mathcal{W}
, hence Banach spaces. Elements of

\mathring{\mathcal{W}}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}
and of
\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}
are real-valued along the real axis.
Since
\mathbb{A}_{r}
contains
\mathbb{S}^{1}
, all these functions restrict to
real-analytic loops on
\mathbb{S}^{1}.
Thus the Laurent
expressions in
\lambda
used in the potentials below, with coefficients
in
\mathcal{W}^{\tau}
, define loop-algebra-valued meromorphic
potentials as in Section
3
.

For a smooth Banach space
\mathcal{E}
-valued map
f\colon[0,\epsilon)\to\mathcal{E}
with
f(0)=f^{\prime}(0)=0
, define

(5.2)

\widehat{f}(t):=\begin{cases}t^{-2}f(t),&t>0,\\[4.0pt]
\tfrac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(0),&t=0.\end{cases}

5.2.
The potential

Let
t\in[0,\tfrac{1}{3})\subset\mathbb{R}
. We consider Fuchsian potentials of the form

(5.3)

\eta=\eta_{t,a,b,c}=R_{0}\frac{dz}{z}+R_{1}\frac{dz}{z-1},

where

(5.4)

\begin{split}R_{0}&=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&-\tfrac{t\left(a+\lambda^{3}b\right)}{\lambda^{3}}&0\\
0&-\tfrac{1}{3}&0\\
t\left(i\lambda^{3}b-ia\right)&ict&\tfrac{1}{3}\\
\end{array}\right),\\
R_{1}&=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&\tfrac{t\left(a+\lambda^{3}b\right)}{\lambda^{3}}&\tfrac{t\left(i\lambda^{3}b-ia\right)}{\lambda^{3}}\\
-t\left(a+\lambda^{3}b\right)&0&ict\\
-t\left(i\lambda^{3}b-ia\right)&-ict&0\\
\end{array}\right),\quad R_{\infty}=-R_{0}-R_{1}\end{split}

are the residues at
0
,
1
, and
\infty
, respectively.
The connection
d+\eta

is not yet in DPW form, since its coefficients may have higher order poles in
\lambda
. The next lemma shows that, under mild conditions,
d+\eta
can be gauged into a
\hat{\tau}
-twisted DPW potential.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
t\in(0,\tfrac{1}{3})
and
r>1
. Suppose
a,b,c\in\mathcal{W}^{\tau}
satisfy the following:

(i)

4ab-c^{2}=-1
;

(ii)

a(\lambda)\neq 0
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}_{r}
.

Then there exists a
{\bf\SL}(\mathbb{D}^{\times})
-gauge
g
such that the gauged potential
(d+\eta).g
is a
\hat{\tau}
-twisted DPW potential and has the local monodromies (
4.7
).

The poles
z=0
and
z=\infty
of
(d+\eta).g
are
3
-removable, and if
t=\tfrac{1}{k}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}^{>3}
, then pole
z=1
is
k
-removable. Moreover, the DPW potential
(d+\eta).g
is immersive.

Proof.

For a non-resonant Fuchsian system, the conjugacy classes of the local monodromies are determined by the eigenvalues of the residues. The eigenvalues of
R_{0}
and
R_{\infty}
are
\tfrac{1}{3}
,
0
, and
-\tfrac{1}{3}
. By condition (i), the eigenvalues of
R_{1}
are
t
,
0
,
-t
. Thus the local monodromies satisfy (
4.7
).

Consider the non-positive gauge

(5.5)

g:=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{\lambda}&0&0\\
0&\lambda&iz\\
0&0&1\\
\end{pmatrix}.

After gauging, one obtains

(5.6)

d+\omega:=(d+\eta).g=d+\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&\frac{t\left(a+\lambda^{3}b\right)}{\lambda(z-1)z}&i\tfrac{2\lambda bt}{z-1}\\
-\frac{2\lambda bt}{z-1}&\frac{1-z(1+3ct)}{3(z-1)z}&i\frac{-3ct+1}{3\lambda}\\
i\frac{t\left(a-\lambda^{3}b\right)}{\lambda(z-1)z}&i\frac{c\lambda t}{z-z^{2}}&-\frac{1-z(1+3ct)}{3(z-1)z}\\
\end{array}\right)dz.

Because
a,b,c\in\mathcal{W}^{\tau}
are
\mathbb{Z}_{6}
-invariant and due to (
3.7
), the connection
d+\omega
is a
\hat{\tau}
-twisted DPW potential.

It remains to desingularize
d+\omega
on suitable coverings by
\hat{\tau}
-twisted positive gauges. For
z=0
and
z=\infty
, we use the
3
-fold covering

\pi_{3}\colon z\mapsto z^{3}

totally branched over
0
and
\infty
. Pulling back (
5.6
) gives

(5.7)

\pi_{3}^{*}(d+\omega)=d+\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&\frac{3t\left(a+\lambda^{3}b\right)}{\lambda z\left(z^{3}-1\right)}&i\frac{6\lambda btz^{2}}{z^{3}-1}\\
-\frac{6\lambda btz^{2}}{z^{3}-1}&\frac{1+z^{3}(-1-3ct)}{z\left(z^{3}-1\right)}&i\frac{z^{2}(-3ct+1)}{\lambda}\\
i\frac{3t\left(a-\lambda^{3}b\right)}{\lambda z\left(z^{3}-1\right)}&i\frac{3c\lambda t}{z-z^{4}}&-\frac{1+z^{3}(-1-3ct)}{z\left(z^{3}-1\right)}\\
\end{array}\right)\,dz.

For
z=0
, consider the
\hat{\tau}
-twisted positive gauge

(5.8)

g_{0}:=\mathrm{diag}(1,z,\tfrac{1}{z}).

Then

(5.9)

(\pi_{3}^{*}(d+\omega)).g_{0}=d+\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&\frac{3t\left(a+\lambda^{3}b\right)}{\lambda\left(z^{3}-1\right)}&i\frac{6\lambda btz}{z^{3}-1}\\
-\frac{6\lambda btz}{z^{3}-1}&\frac{-3ctz^{2}}{z^{3}-1}&i\frac{-3ct+1}{\lambda}\\
i\frac{3t\left(a-\lambda^{3}b\right)}{\lambda\left(z^{3}-1\right)}&-i\frac{3c\lambda tz}{z^{3}-1}&\frac{3ctz^{2}}{z^{3}-1}\\
\end{array}\right)dz

is nonsingular at
z=0
. The
\lambda^{-1}
-part
\Phi_{0}
of (
5.9
) satisfies
\Phi_{0}^{2}\neq 0
since
a(0)\neq 0

by condition (ii). Hence
d+\omega
has a
3
-removable singularity at
z=0
, and the resulting DPW potential is immersive there.

For
z=\infty
, consider the
\hat{\tau}
-twisted positive gauge

(5.10)

g_{\infty}:=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&-iz^{2}&0\\
0&\frac{\lambda}{z}&\frac{i}{z^{2}}\\
\end{pmatrix}.

We obtain

(\pi_{3}^{*}(d+\omega)).g_{\infty}=d+\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&\frac{3itz\left(\lambda^{3}b-a\right)}{\lambda\left(z^{3}-1\right)}&-\frac{6\lambda bt}{z^{3}-1}\\
-\frac{6i\lambda bt}{z^{3}-1}&-\frac{3ict}{z-z^{4}}&\frac{3ct-i}{\lambda z^{2}}\\
\frac{3tz\left(a+\lambda^{3}b\right)}{\lambda\left(z^{3}-1\right)}&-\frac{3c\lambda t}{z^{3}-1}&\frac{3ict}{z-z^{4}}\\
\end{array}\right)dz,

which is smooth at
z=\infty
, i.e.
z=\infty
is a
3
-removable singularity.
Moreover, we obtain as in the case of
z=0
that the
\lambda^{-1}
-part
\Phi_{\infty}
satisfies
\Phi_{\infty}^{2}\neq 0,
i.e.

z=\infty
is also immersive.

For
z=1
and
t=\tfrac{1}{k}
with
k\in\mathbb{N}^{>3}
, consider the
k
-fold covering

\pi_{k}(w)=w^{k}+1.

Then

\pi_{k}^{*}(d+\omega)=d+\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&\frac{a+\lambda^{3}b}{\lambda w^{k+1}+\lambda w}&i\frac{2\lambda b}{w}\\
-\frac{2\lambda b}{w}&\frac{-\frac{kw^{k}}{w^{k}+1}-3c}{3w}&i\frac{(-3c+k)w^{k-1}}{3\lambda}\\
i\frac{\left(a-\lambda^{3}b\right)}{\lambda w\left(w^{k}+1\right)}&-i\frac{c\lambda}{w^{k+1}+w}&\frac{\frac{kw^{k}}{w^{k}+1}+3c}{3w}\\
\end{array}\right)dw.

Define

(5.11)

g_{k}:=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&\frac{(1-c)\lambda^{2}(w-1)}{2aw}&-i\frac{(c-1)\lambda(w-1)}{aw}\\
\frac{(c-1)\lambda(w-1)}{a}&\frac{4a^{2}w^{2}-(c-1)^{2}\lambda^{3}(w-1)^{2}}{4a^{2}w}&-i\frac{(c-1)^{2}\lambda^{2}(w-1)^{2}}{2a^{2}w}\\
i\frac{(c-1)\lambda^{2}(w-1)}{2a}&\frac{i\lambda(w-1)\left(4a^{2}(w+1)-(c-1)^{2}\lambda^{3}(w-1)\right)}{8a^{2}w}&\frac{4a^{2}+(c-1)^{2}\lambda^{3}(w-1)^{2}}{4a^{2}w}\\
\end{array}\right).

Then
\det(g_{k})=1
, and
g_{k}
is
a
{\bf\SL}^{\hat{\tau}}(\mathbb{D})
-gauge since

a(\lambda)\neq 0
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}_{r}
, and
because
a,b,c\in\mathcal{W}^{\tau}
. A direct computation using condition (i) shows that

\pi_{k}^{*}(d+\omega).g_{k}

is holomorphic at
w=0
. Moreover, the Higgs field is

\Phi_{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&\frac{a}{w^{k}+1}&0\\
0&0&\frac{i}{3}(-3c+k)w^{k-3}\\
\frac{ia}{w^{k}+1}&0&0\\
\end{array}\right).

At
w=0
the Higgs field
\Phi_{1}
is
3
-step nilpotent, i.e
\Phi_{1}^{2}\neq 0
but
\Phi_{1}^{3}=0
, provided
k\geq 4
. Therefore, for
t=\tfrac{1}{k}
with
k\in\mathbb{N}^{>3}
, the point
z=1
is an immersive,
k
-removable singularity of
d+\omega
.
∎

5.3.
The monodromy functions

Let
\eta=\eta_{t,a,b,c}
with
a,b,c\in\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}
as above.
Let
\Psi=\Psi_{t,a,b,c}
be a solution of

(5.12)

d\Psi_{t,a,b,c}+\eta_{t,a,b,c}\Psi_{t,a,b,c}=0,\hskip 20.00003pt\Psi_{t,a,b,c}\bigl(\tfrac{1}{2}\bigr)=\Id.

Then
\Psi_{t,a,b,c}
depends real-analytically on
(t,a,b,c).

We denote by
M_{0}=M_{0}(t,a,b,c)
,
M_{1}=M_{1}(t,a,b,c)
, and
M_{\infty}=M_{\infty}(t,a,b,c)
the monodromies around
0
,
1
, and
\infty
, respectively. We also write

X=X_{t,a,b,c}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptY=Y_{t,a,b,c}

for the corresponding global trace coordinates,
but we usually omit the dependence on
(t,a,b,c)
, or on
(a,b,c)
, to keep the notation light.

Lemma 5.2
.

Assume that
a,b,c\in\mathcal{W}^{\tau}
. Then, for all
t\in[0,\tfrac{1}{3})
and for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}_{r}^{\times}
,

X(\zeta\lambda)=X(\lambda),\hskip 20.00003ptY(\zeta\lambda)=Y(\lambda),\hskip 20.00003ptX(-\lambda)=Y(\lambda).

Proof.

The first two identities follow directly from
f(\zeta\lambda)=f(\lambda)
for all
f\in\mathcal{W}^{\tau}
and all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}_{r}.

For the last one, we use
f(-\lambda)=f(\lambda)
for all
f\in\mathcal{W}^{\tau}
and all
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}_{r},
and consider

h=\lambda\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}}&0&0\\
0&0&i\\
0&-i&0\\
\end{pmatrix}.

Then, using
\eta_{\lambda}=\eta_{t,a(\lambda),b(\lambda),c(\lambda)}
for
\lambda\in\mathbb{D}_{r}^{\times}
,

(d+\eta_{\lambda}).h=d-\eta_{-\lambda}^{T}.

Since gauge-equivalent connections have conjugate monodromy, and the monodromy of
d-\eta_{-\lambda}^{T}
along
\delta
is the transpose inverse of the monodromy of
d+\eta_{-\lambda}
along
\delta^{-1}
, the trace coordinates satisfy
X(-\lambda)=Y(\lambda).

∎

Motivated by (
4.14
), we define for
t\in[0,\tfrac{1}{3})
,

A=A_{t,a,b,c}:=\frac{1}{2}(X+Y),\hskip 20.00003ptB=B_{t,a,b,c}:=\frac{i}{2}(-X+Y).

Lemma 5.3
.

Assume that
a,b,c\in\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}
and
t\in[0,\tfrac{1}{3}).

Then,
A\in\mathring{\mathcal{W}}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}
and
\lambda^{3}B\in\mathring{\mathcal{W}}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}.

Proof.

The Riemann-Hilbert map from the space of Fuchsian systems to its monodromy is a complex analytic map (to its image).
Taking traces, and composition with the map
(a,b,c)\mapsto\eta_{t,a,b,c}
thus shows that

A,B\in\mathring{\mathcal{W}}.
In particular,
A
and
B
are bounded on the annulus
\mathbb{A}_{r}.

It then follows directly from Lemma
5.2
and their definition that
A,\lambda^{3}B\in\mathring{\mathcal{W}}^{\tau}.
To show the
reality properties,
consider

\tilde{g}=\mathrm{diag}(i,i,-1)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003ptd+\tilde{\eta}:=(d+\eta).\tilde{g}.

Let
\kappa(z)=\bar{z}
. A direct computation using
a,b,c\in\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}
gives

\kappa^{*}\overline{(d+\tilde{\eta})}=d+\tilde{\eta}.

Since complex conjugation sends the standard loops
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}
to their inverses, the corresponding monodromies satisfy

\overline{M_{j}}\sim M_{j}^{-1}.
It follows from the definition of
X
and
Y
that
\overline{X}=Y,
hence
A
and
B
are real-valued on the real axis.

Since the paths defining
X
and
Y
are exchanged by
\kappa
, it follows from the definitions that
A,B\in\mathring{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}
, i.e.
A,\lambda^{3}B\in\mathring{\mathcal{W}}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}
as claimed.
∎

Remark 5.4
.

The same antiholomorphic symmetry
\kappa
used in the proof of Lemma
5.3
also implies

\overline{Z(\lambda)}=Z(\bar{\lambda}),

hence
Z\in\mathring{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}
and, in particular,
Z
is real along the real axis.
This reality condition should not be confused with unitarity: along the real axis it comes from the involution
z\mapsto\bar{z}
, whereas the unitary condition relevant on
\mathbb{S}^{1}
is
\bar{X}=Y
and
\bar{Z}=\tilde{Z}
.

Set
\lambda_{0}=\exp(\tfrac{2\pi i}{12})
, and define

(5.13)

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{t,a,b,c}&:=(A-A^{*})^{+}\\
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}_{t,a,b,c}&:=(B-B^{*})^{+}\\
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{t,a,b,c}&:=4ab-c^{2}+1\\
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}_{t,a,b,c}&:=A(\lambda_{0}).\\
\end{split}

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
t\in(0,\tfrac{1}{3})
be fixed.
Let
a,b,c\in\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Assume that
a(\lambda)\neq 0
and
c(\lambda)\neq 0
for all
|\lambda|\leq 1
.
Assume that

(5.14)

\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{H}=0\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathcal{S}=0.

Then, the monodromy of

d+\eta_{t,a,b,c}
is unitarizable for every
\lambda\in\mathbb{S}^{1},
and the
monodromy is irreducible for every
\lambda
with
0<|\lambda|\leq 1
.
Moreover,
B(\lambda_{0})=0,
and
if
t
is rational, the monodromy at
\lambda_{0}
is
\rho
or
(\rho^{-1})^{T}
defined in (
4.3
),
and generates a finite subgroup isomorphic to
\Gamma
or
\tilde{\Gamma}

as in Example
4.3
.

Proof.

We first show that
A
and
B
are real along the unit circle
\lambda\in\mathbb{S}^{1}.
This is equivalent to
A^{*}=A
and
B^{*}=B.

Let
A=\sum_{k}A^{(k)}\lambda^{6k}.

By reality
A\in\mathring{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}
,
\bar{A}^{(k)}=A^{(k)}
for all
k.

In particular,
A^{(0)}\in\mathbb{R}
, and
therefore
A-A^{*}
has no constant part.
Since
(A-A^{*})^{+}=\mathcal{F}=0
, its negative part also vanishes by applying
(\cdot)^{*}
, hence
A-A^{*}=0.

Analogously, one obtains
B=B^{*}.

Hence,
A
and
B
are real along the unit circle
\lambda\in\mathbb{S}^{1}.

With
X=A+iB
and
Y=A-iB
, this
implies the necessary
unitarity condition that

X(\lambda)=\overline{Y(\bar{\lambda}^{-1})}
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
.
We have to show that for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
the monodromy
(X(\lambda),Y(\lambda),Z(\lambda))

is unitarizable.
By Theorem
7
it is enough to show that

(X(\lambda),Y(\lambda),Z(\lambda))\in\mathcal{C}_{u}
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
.

We first show
that
B(\lambda_{0})=0
.
In fact,
\lambda^{3}B\in\mathring{\mathcal{W}}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}
implies

\overline{B(\lambda)}=B(\bar{\lambda})

for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{A}_{r}.

Using
\bar{\lambda}_{0}=-\zeta^{-2}\lambda_{0}
and
\lambda^{3}B\in\mathring{\mathcal{W}}^{\tau}
, in particular
B
is an odd function in
\lambda
, we therefore obtain

(5.15)

\overline{B(\lambda_{0})}=B(\bar{\lambda}_{0})=B(\zeta^{-2}(-\lambda_{0}))=B(-\lambda_{0})=-B(\lambda_{0}).

Since
B
is real along the unit circle,
B(\lambda_{0})=0
as claimed.
We obtain that
X(\lambda_{0})=0=Y(\lambda_{0}),
and
this implies

(X(\lambda_{0}),Y(\lambda_{0}),Z(\lambda_{0}))\in\mathcal{C}_{u}
.
For rational
t
, the image of the monodromy representation is therefore conjugate to
\Gamma
or
\tilde{\Gamma},

and in particular finite.

We next show that
\left(X(\lambda),Y(\lambda),Z(\lambda)\right)
remains in the unitary component
\mathcal{C}_{u}
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{S}^{1}.

From Section
4.4
this can happen only if the discriminant (
4.13
)
has an odd order zero along the unit circle, or if we pass through one of the three reducible boundary points.
Since the representation depends holomorphically on
\lambda
,
also the functions
Z=Z(\lambda)
and
\tilde{Z}=\tilde{Z}(\lambda)
depend holomorphically on
\lambda
.
The discriminant (
4.13
) of the quadratic polynomial (
4.9
)
is given by
D(\lambda)=(Z(\lambda)-\tilde{Z}(\lambda))^{2},

hence its zeros are even.

It thus remains to establish that the monodromy cannot be reducible.
Assume the monodromy is reducible at
\lambda_{1}
with
0<|\lambda_{1}|\leq 1
. By the proof of Lemma
5.2
, the monodromy at
-\lambda_{1}

is conjugate to the contragredient of the monodromy at

\lambda_{1}.
By the classification of reducible representations in Section
4.3
, we can therefore assume without loss of generality that the monodromy at
\lambda_{1}
preserves a line.
Using parallel transport, this generates a holomorphic line subbundle
L
of the trivial holomorphic vector bundle
\mathbb{C}^{3}\to\mathbb{CP}^{1}
which passes through eigenlines of the residues
of
d+\eta_{t,a,b,c}(\lambda_{1}).

The corresponding eigenvalue at
z=1
is then
0
, while the eigenvalues at
z=0
and
z=\infty
satisfy
\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{\infty}=0.

Furthermore, the Fuchsian system
d+\eta_{t,a,b,c}(\lambda_{1})
induces a logarithmic connection on
L
, i.e. a meromorphic connection with first-order poles
at
z=0,1,\infty
. By the generalized residue formula for logarithmic connections on a line bundle (see
[
27
, Lemma 3]
),
\mathrm{deg}(L)=0.

Hence,
L
is a holomorphic line subbundle of degree 0, i.e. a constant line in
\mathbb{C}^{3}.
By construction,
L
is an eigenline for
R_{0}
,
R_{1}
and
R_{\infty}.
A direct algebraic computation shows that
this is only possible if
c(\lambda_{1})=0
or
a(\lambda_{1})=0,
which is excluded by assumption. Thus, the monodromy cannot be reducible for
\lambda
with
0<|\lambda|\leq 1
. Consequently, the monodromy along
\mathbb{S}^{1}
stays in the unitary component
\mathcal{C}_{u}.

∎

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
a,b,c\in\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}
,
t\in[0,\tfrac{1}{3})
. Then

\lambda^{-6}{\mathcal{F}},\lambda^{-3}{\mathcal{G}},{\mathcal{H}}\in\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathcal{S}\in\mathbb{R}.

Proof.

That
{\mathcal{H}}\in\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}
follows as
\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}
is a Banach algebra.
By Lemma
5.3
,
A,\lambda^{3}B\in\mathring{\mathcal{W}}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}.

Thus, also
A-A^{*},\lambda^{-3}(B-B^{*})\in\mathring{\mathcal{W}}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Taking the positive parts
(.)^{+}
of
A-A^{*}
and
B-B^{*}
therefore yields
\lambda^{-6}\mathcal{F},\lambda^{-3}\mathcal{G}\in\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}
.
It remains to show
A(\lambda_{0})=\mathcal{S}\in\mathbb{R}.

This is shown similarly to (
5.15
): since A is even and
\zeta
-invariant, one obtains
\overline{A(\lambda_{0})}=A(\lambda_{0}).

∎

5.4.
Setting up the implicit function theorem

We seek
\epsilon>0
and
a,b,c\colon[0,\epsilon)\to\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}
which solve
(
5.14
). We use the implicit function theorem in the Banach spaces of holomorphic functions.
To do so, we first expand the monodromy of
d+\eta_{t,a,b,c}
given by (
5.3
) at
t=0
. In the following, primes always denote differentiation with respect to
t
, even for functions depending on additional variables.

Let
a,b,c\in\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Consider a solution
\Psi_{t}=\Psi_{t,a,b,c}
of (
5.12
).
At
t=0
the potential simplifies to

d+\eta_{0,a,b,c}=d+\mathrm{diag}(0,-\tfrac{1}{3},\tfrac{1}{3})\frac{dz}{z},

and a parallel frame with
\Psi_{0}(\tfrac{1}{2})=\Id
is given by

(5.16)

\Psi_{0}(z)=\mathrm{diag}(1,(2z)^{\tfrac{1}{3}},(2z)^{-\tfrac{1}{3}}).

The branch is chosen holomorphically on the disk of radius
3/4
centered at
1
, normalized by
\Psi_{0}(\tfrac{1}{2})=\Id
.
Thus
M_{1}(0)=\Id
, while the monodromy of
\Psi_{0}
along
\gamma_{0}
is

M_{0}(0)=\mathrm{diag}(1,\zeta,\zeta^{-1}).

Lemma 5.7
.

We have

(5.17)

M_{1}^{\prime}(0)=-2\pi i\,\Psi_{0}(1)^{-1}\,\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&\frac{a}{\lambda^{3}}+b&i\left(b-\frac{a}{\lambda^{3}}\right)\\
-\lambda^{3}b-a&0&ci\\
i\left(a-\lambda^{3}b\right)&-ci&0\\
\end{array}\right)\Psi_{0}(1).

Proof.

Let
Y_{t}(s):=\Psi_{t,a,b,c}(\gamma_{1}(s))
. Then

\frac{d}{ds}Y_{t}+\gamma_{1}^{*}(\eta_{t,a,b,c})\,Y_{t}=0
with

Y_{t}(0)=\Id,

and the monodromy along
\gamma_{1}
is
M_{1}(t)=Y_{t}(1)
.
Differentiating at
t=0
gives

\frac{d}{ds}(Y_{0}^{-1}Y_{0}^{\prime})=-\gamma_{1}^{*}(\Psi_{0}^{-1}\eta^{\prime}_{0,a,b,c}\Psi_{0}).

Integrating from
0
to
1
, and using
Y_{0}(0)=Y_{0}(1)=\Id
and
Y_{0}^{\prime}(0)=0
, yields

M_{1}^{\prime}(0)=Y_{0}^{\prime}(1)=-\int_{\gamma_{1}}\Psi_{0}^{-1}\eta^{\prime}_{0,a,b,c}\Psi_{0}.

Thus, (
5.17
) follows from the residue theorem.
∎

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
a,b,c\in\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}
. Then

(5.18)

X_{0,a,b,c}=Y_{0,a,b,c}=0,\hskip 20.00003ptX^{\prime}_{0,a,b,c}=Y^{\prime}_{0,a,b,c}=0.

Using the definition (
5.2
), we have at
t=0

(5.19)

\begin{split}\widehat{X}_{0,a,b,c}&=\tfrac{2\pi^{2}}{\lambda^{3}}\left(-i\sqrt{3}a^{2}+2ab\lambda^{3}-i\sqrt{3}b^{2}\lambda^{6}+c^{2}\lambda^{3}\right)\\
\widehat{Y}_{0,a,b,c}&=\tfrac{2\pi^{2}}{\lambda^{3}}\left(i\sqrt{3}a^{2}+2ab\lambda^{3}+i\sqrt{3}b^{2}\lambda^{6}+c^{2}\lambda^{3}\right).\end{split}

Proof.

The formulas are pointwise in
\lambda
, so it suffices to regard
a
,
b
, and
c
as complex numbers.
Consider the monodromies
M_{0}(t),M_{1}(t),M_{\infty}(t)
, which satisfy
M_{\infty}(t)M_{1}(t)M_{0}(t)=\mathrm{Id}
for all
t
.
Furthermore, the conjugacy class of
M_{0}(t)
and
M_{\infty}(t)
are independent of
t
and given by
\mathrm{diag}(1,\zeta,\zeta^{-1})
.
We have
M_{0}(0)=\mathrm{diag}(1,\zeta,\zeta^{-1})
and
M_{1}(0)=\mathrm{Id}
.
Hence, the hypotheses of Lemma
4.6
are satisfied, and we obtain (
5.18
).
Denote
M_{1}^{\prime}(0)=(p_{ij})_{i,j}
.
Using Lemma
5.7
together with
\Psi_{0}(1)=\mathrm{diag}(1,2^{1/3},2^{-1/3})
, we compute

p_{12}p_{21}=4\pi^{2}\tfrac{(a+\lambda^{3}b)^{2}}{\lambda^{3}},\hskip 20.00003ptp_{13}p_{31}=-4\pi^{2}\tfrac{(a-\lambda^{3}b)^{2}}{\lambda^{3}},\hskip 20.00003ptp_{23}p_{32}=-4\pi^{2}c^{2}.

Together with Lemma
4.6
this directly
gives (
5.19
).
∎

5.5.
The initial data

We next choose the initial direction for the implicit-function argument.
This should be viewed as choosing a tangent direction at the singular point corresponding
to
t=0
in the moduli space of Fuchsian systems. Geometrically, this choice determines the branch of solutions, and hence the resulting family of surfaces.

Lemma 5.9
.

The constant functions

(5.20)

\begin{split}a_{0}:=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{6}},\quad b_{0}:=-\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{6}},\quad c_{0}:=\pm\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\end{split}

satisfy
a_{0},b_{0},c_{0}\in\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}},
and Condition (i) and (ii) of Lemma
5.1
. The corresponding Fuchsian potential

d+\eta_{t,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0}}
satisfies the monodromy conditions (
5.14
) up to second order at
t=0:

(5.21)

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}_{0,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0}}&=0,\quad\mathcal{G}_{0,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0}}=0,\quad\mathcal{H}_{t,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0}}=0,\quad\mathcal{S}_{0,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0}}=0,\\
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{0,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0}}&=0,\quad\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{0,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0}}=0,\quad\quad\quad\qquad\quad\qquad\;\;\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{0,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0}}=0\\
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{0,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0}}&=0,\quad\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{0,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0}}=0,\quad\quad\quad\qquad\quad\qquad\;\;\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{0,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0}}=0\\
\end{split}

Proof.

Clearly,
4a_{0}b_{0}-c_{0}^{2}=-1,
independently of
t
and
\lambda.

Using Lemma
5.8
, we compute
X_{0,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0}}=Y_{0,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0}}=0
and
X^{\prime}_{0,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0}}=Y^{\prime}_{0,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0}}=0.

Furthermore,
inserting (
5.20
) into (
5.19
) gives

\begin{split}\widehat{X}_{0,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0}}=&-\tfrac{i\pi^{2}\left(\lambda^{6}+1\right)}{\sqrt{3}\lambda^{3}},\qquad\widehat{Y}_{0,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0}}=\tfrac{i\pi^{2}\left(\lambda^{6}+1\right)}{\sqrt{3}\lambda^{3}}.\end{split}

By inspection, (
5.21
) is satisfied.
∎

The two sign choices for
c_{0}
lead to the two branches of solutions produced later by the implicit function theorem.

5.6.
The solution

We next prove the existence of potentials satisfying the intrinsic and extrinsic closing conditions.

In the following, let
a,b,c\in\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}
. By Lemma
5.6
,

\lambda^{-6}\mathcal{F},\ \lambda^{-3}\mathcal{G},\ \mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}},
and

\mathcal{S}\in\mathbb{R}.

Moreover, by (
5.18
), the functions

\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{\mathcal{G}},
and
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}

extend real-analytically across
t=0
. Hence, after restricting to a sufficiently small open interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
containing
0
, we obtain a well-defined real analytic map

(5.22)

\begin{split}\mathcal{M}&\colon I\times\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathbb{R};\\
&(t,a,b,c)\mapsto(\lambda^{-6}\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t,a,b,c}\,,\,\lambda^{-3}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{t,a,b,c}\,,\,\mathcal{H}_{t,a,b,c}\,,\,\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{t,a,b,c})\,,\,\end{split}

where
X,Y
and thus
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},\mathcal{S}
are computed with respect to the connection
d+\eta_{t,a,b,c}
as before.
Since the Riemann–Hilbert map assigning to a Fuchsian system its monodromy is real analytic,
and the involved projections
(.)^{+}
and involutions
(.)^{*}
are bounded linear,
the map
\mathcal{M}
is real analytic
as a composition of real analytic maps.

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
r>1,
and
let
a_{0},b_{0},c_{0}
be as in (
5.20
).
There exists
\epsilon>0
and unique real analytic maps

(5.23)

a,b,c\colon[0,\epsilon)\to\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}

with
a(0)=a_{0}
,
b(0)=b_{0}
, and
c(0)=c_{0}
, satisfying

\mathcal{F}_{t,a(t),b(t),c(t)}=\mathcal{G}_{t,a(t),b(t),c(t)}=\mathcal{H}_{t,a(t),b(t),c(t)}=0\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathcal{S}_{t,a(t),b(t),c(t)}=0

for all
t\in[0,\epsilon).
Furthermore,
a(t,\lambda)\neq 0
and
c(t,\lambda)\neq 0
for all
|\lambda|\leq r
and all
t\in[0,\epsilon).

For
t=1/k<\epsilon
and
c_{0}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
, the monodromy
\rho_{\lambda_{0}}
is conjugate to
\rho
in (
4.3
), while
\rho_{\lambda_{0}}
is conjugate to
(\rho^{-1})^{T}
for

c_{0}=-\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
.

Proof.

By the definition (
5.2
), together with Lemma
5.9
, the vanishing of

\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{\mathcal{G}},
and
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}

near
t=0
is equivalent to the vanishing of

\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G},
and
\mathcal{S}.

To prove existence, it is therefore enough to find
a=a(t),b=b(t),c=c(t)
such that

\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{t,a(t),b(t),c(t)}=\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{t,a(t),b(t),c(t)}={\mathcal{H}}_{t,a(t),b(t),c(t)}=0\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{t,a(t),b(t),c(t)}=0

for sufficiently small
t\geq 0.
By (
5.21
), we have

\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{0,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0}}=\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{0,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0}}={\mathcal{H}}_{0,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0}}=0\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{0,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0}}=0.

We apply the implicit function theorem for the map
\mathcal{M}
in (
5.22
) between the corresponding Banach spaces.
As observed above,
\mathcal{M}
is well-defined, Banach-space real analytic, and satisfies

\mathcal{M}(0,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0})=0.

We claim that the differential at the initial point

d_{(0,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0})}\mathcal{M}\colon\{0\}\times\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathbb{R}

restricted to the
t=0
-slice is an isomorphism. We compute,
using (
5.19
) and
A=\tfrac{1}{2}(X+Y)
,
B=\tfrac{i}{2}(-X+Y)

(5.24)

\begin{split}\tfrac{1}{4\pi^{2}}d_{(0,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0})}\widehat{A}&=b_{0}\,da+a_{0}\,db+c_{0}\,dc\\
-\tfrac{1}{4\sqrt{3}\pi^{2}}d_{(0,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0})}\widehat{B}&=\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}}a_{0}da+\lambda^{3}b_{0}db.\end{split}

Consider the Banach space isomorphisms

(5.25)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{I}=(\mathcal{I}_{0},\mathcal{I}_{+})\colon\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}};\;x+\lambda^{6}f\mapsto(x,f)\\
\mathcal{K}=(\mathcal{K}_{c},\mathcal{K}_{p})&\colon\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}};\;g=x+(\lambda^{6}-1)f\mapsto(x,f).\end{split}

Here
x=g(1)
and
f=(g-g(1))/(\lambda^{6}-1)
; this is well-defined
and bounded because functions in
\mathcal{W}^{\tau}
depend on
\lambda^{6}
.
Decompose (whenever necessary)

da=da^{(0)}+\lambda^{6}da^{+},\hskip 10.00002ptdb=db^{(0)}+\lambda^{6}db^{+},\hskip 10.00002ptdc=dc^{(0)}+\lambda^{6}dc^{+}.

via
\mathcal{I}(da)=(da^{(0)},da^{+}),\dots
. Then, using
a_{0}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{6}}=-b_{0},c_{0}=\pm\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\in\mathbb{R}
and setting
\sigma:=\pm\sqrt{2}
, we obtain

(5.26)

\begin{split}\delta F:=\tfrac{\sqrt{6}}{4\pi^{2}}d_{(0,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0})}\lambda^{-6}\widehat{\mathcal{F}}=&-\,da^{+}+\,db^{+}+\sigma\,dc^{+}\\
\delta G:=-\tfrac{\sqrt{6}}{4\sqrt{3}\pi^{2}}d_{(0,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0})}\lambda^{-3}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}=&da^{+}-da^{(0)}-db\\
\delta H:=\tfrac{1}{2}\sqrt{6}d_{(0,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0})}\mathcal{H}=&2db-2da-\sigma dc\\
\delta S:=\tfrac{\sqrt{6}}{4\pi^{2}}d_{(0,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0})}\widehat{\mathcal{S}}=&-\,da(\lambda_{0})+\,db(\lambda_{0})+\sigma\,dc(\lambda_{0}).\end{split}

We split
\delta H=\delta H^{(0)}+\lambda^{6}\delta H^{+}
and apply
\mathcal{K}
to obtain an infinite-dimensional

(5.27)

\begin{split}dc^{+}&=-\tfrac{1}{3\sigma}(\delta H^{+}-2\delta F)\\
db^{+}&=\mathcal{K}_{p}(-\delta G-\delta F+\sigma\,dc^{+})\\
da^{+}&=-\delta F+db^{+}+\sigma dc^{+}\\
\end{split}

and a finite-dimensional part

(5.28)

\begin{split}\delta H^{(0)}=&-2da^{(0)}+2db^{(0)}-\sigma dc^{(0)}\\
\mathcal{K}_{c}(\delta G+\delta F-\sigma\,dc^{+})=&-da^{(0)}-db^{(0)}\\
\delta S-da^{+}(\lambda_{0})+db^{+}(\lambda_{0})+\sigma dc^{+}(\lambda_{0})=&-da^{(0)}+db^{(0)}+\sigma dc^{(0)},\end{split}

where we used
\lambda_{0}^{6}=-1
in the last equation.
Obviously, (
5.27
) has a unique solution. The coefficient matrix
of the finite-dimensional system (
5.28
) has determinant
6\sigma\neq 0,

so (
5.28
) has a unique solution for any
da^{+},db^{+},dc^{+}
determined by (
5.27
).
This shows that the differential

d_{(0,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0})}\mathcal{M}\colon\{0\}\times\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathbb{R}

is invertible (as a bounded linear map).
By applying the implicit function theorem, the existence and uniqueness result for the maps (
5.23
) follows.

Since
a_{0}
and
c_{0}
are nonzero constants, continuity of

t\mapsto a(t)
and
t\mapsto c(t)

in
\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}
implies, after shrinking
\epsilon
if necessary, that

a(t,\lambda)\neq 0,
and
c(t,\lambda)\neq 0

for all
|\lambda|\leq 1
and all
t\in[0,\epsilon)
.

It remains to determine the
Z
-coordinate of
\rho_{\lambda_{0}}
. Analogously to Lemma
5.8
and Lemma
5.9
, one computes
for the initial data
a_{0}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{6}},b_{0}=-\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{6}},c_{0}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
the Taylor expansion

(5.29)

Z_{t,a(t),b(t),c(t)}=3-12\pi^{2}t^{2}-8\pi^{3}it^{3}+O(t^{4}).

Since the only two solutions of the Lawton equation
P(0,0,Z)=0
are given by
2e^{-2i\pi t}+e^{4i\pi t}
and

2e^{2i\pi t}+e^{-4i\pi t}
corresponding to
the finite representations
\rho
respectively
(\rho^{-1})^{T}
,
and (
5.29
) picks the first one. Hence the claim for
c_{0}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
follows. The case

c_{0}=-\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
works analogously.
∎

6.
New minimal Lagrangian surfaces

In this section we combine Theorem
4
with the monodromy construction of Section
5
to obtain new
compact minimal Lagrangian immersions of Fermat curves. We then discuss their symmetry properties and compute their areas.

6.1.
Construction

Consider the real analytic maps

a,b,c\colon[0,\epsilon)\to\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}

given by Proposition
5.10
, corresponding to one of the two choices

c_{0}=c(0)=\pm\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}

in Lemma
5.9
. For
t\in(0,\epsilon)
set

\eta_{t}:=\eta_{t,a(t),b(t),c(t)}.

By Proposition
5.5
,
the monodromy representation
\rho_{\lambda}
of the family
d+\eta_{t}
is unitarizable for every
\lambda\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
and irreducible for
|\lambda|\leq 1
.

Now let
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
\frac{1}{k}=t<\epsilon
. Proposition
5.10
implies that
the monodromy representation
at the Sym point

\lambda_{0}=\exp\left(\tfrac{2\pi i}{12}\right)

of the Fuchsian system
d+\eta_{t}(\lambda_{0})
is
\rho
in (
4.3
) or
(\rho^{-1})^{T}
, and
has finite image isomorphic to
\Gamma_{deck}
.
Moreover, Lemma
5.1
shows that after the gauge transformation (
5.5
) the connection

d+\eta_{t}
becomes a meromorphic
\hat{\tau}
-twisted DPW-potential
d+\omega_{t}
whose singularities are removable on the branched cover
corresponding to the covering monodromy
\rho_{\lambda_{0}}
, and whose Higgs field is immersive.
Furthermore, the monodromies of
d+\omega_{t}
are conjugate to the monodromies of

d+\eta_{t}(\lambda_{0})
, and hence unitarizable for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{S}^{1}.

Therefore, all hypotheses of Theorem
4
are satisfied (where we use
\hat{\tau}
instead of
\tau
).
By
Proposition
5.10
, the monodromy at
\lambda_{0}
is given
\rho
defined in (
4.3
)
or
(\rho^{-1})^{T}
.
The representations generate a group

\Gamma\cong\Gamma_{deck}\subset\mathrm{Aut}(\Sigma_{k})
and both have the same kernel
\Lambda\subset\pi_{1}(S,z_{0})
.
By
(
4.4
),
the corresponding branched covering is precisely the Fermat curve covering

\Sigma_{k}\to\Sigma_{k}/\Gamma_{deck}\cong\mathbb{CP}^{1}.

Theorem
4
then yields a minimal Lagrangian immersion

f_{k}^{\pm}\colon\Sigma_{k}\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}.

Furthermore, the immersion is equivariant with respect to the
action of the deck group
\Gamma_{deck}
of the Fermat covering.

Theorem 8
.

There exists
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for every
k\geq k_{0}
and each choice
c(0)=c_{0}=\pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
in Lemma
5.9
, there exists a minimal Lagrangian immersion

f_{k}^{\pm}\colon\Sigma_{k}\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}

from the Fermat curve
\Sigma_{k}
of genus
\frac{1}{2}(k-1)(k-2)
. The immersion
f_{k}^{\pm}
is
\Gamma_{deck}
-equivariant.

If
3\nmid k
, then
f_{k}^{\pm}

does not factor through a nontrivial lower-genus quotient of

\Sigma_{k}
. If
k=3n
, then
f_{3n}^{\pm}
factors through the unbranched quotient

\Sigma_{3n}\to X_{n}:=\Sigma_{3n}/\mathbb{Z}_{3}.

Proof.

The existence part follows from the preceding discussion.
To prove the factorization statement, consider the induced projective representation

\underline{\rho}_{\lambda_{0}}\colon\pi_{1}(\mathbb{CP}^{1}\setminus\{0,1,\infty\})\to\SU_{3}\to\PSU_{3}.

If
3\nmid k
, then the kernel of
\underline{\rho}_{\lambda_{0}}
agrees with the kernel of
\rho_{\lambda_{0}}
, and therefore the immersion does not descend to a nontrivial quotient of
\Sigma_{k}
.

If
k=3n
, then the central subgroup
\mathbb{Z}_{3}\subset\SU_{3}
is contained in the image of
\rho_{\lambda_{0}}
and becomes trivial in
\PSU_{3}
. Hence the kernel of
\underline{\rho}_{\lambda_{0}}
is strictly larger than the kernel of
\rho_{\lambda_{0}}
. The corresponding quotient is a fixed-point-free
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
-quotient

(6.1)

\Sigma_{3n}\to X_{n}:=\Sigma_{3n}/\mathbb{Z}_{3}.

By construction, the projective extrinsic closing condition is already satisfied on
X_{n}
, so the immersion factors through
X_{n}
.
∎

Corollary 6.1
.

For every
k\geq k_{0}
and each sign choice
c_{0}=\pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
, the immersion
f_{k}^{\pm}
admits a special Legendrian lift

\hat{f}_{k}^{\pm}\colon\Sigma_{k}\to\mathbb{S}^{5}.

Proof.

The corollary directly follows from Theorem
8
and Theorem
1
, because the projective monodromy
c
is trivial.
∎

Remark 6.2
.

The surfaces obtained in Theorem
8
are constructed using the implicit function theorem in the parameter
t=\tfrac{1}{k}.
If the deformation in
t
exists until
t=\tfrac{1}{3}
, we would obtain a minimal Lagrangian immersion from
X_{1}=\Sigma_{3}/\mathbb{Z}_{3}
of genus 1 that exhibits the same symmetries as the minimal Lagrangian Clifford torus
in
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
. The surfaces in Theorem
8
can be interpreted as the minimal Lagrangian counterparts of the minimal Lawson surfaces
\xi_{k,k}
in the 3-sphere
[
36
]
. Furthermore, it is reasonable to anticipate that the methods employed in
[
11
]
can be applied to establish that the maximal interval
(0,T)
of existence for the implicit function theorem is relatively large, implying that we might expect
the existence of minimal Lagrangian surfaces
f_{k}\colon\Sigma_{k}\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
for relatively low values of
k
.

6.2.
Symmetries

In this subsection we write
f_{k}
for either of the two families
f_{k}^{\pm}
whenever the statement is independent of the sign choice.
The group

\Gamma_{deck}
of deck transformations
acts on the minimal Lagrangian immersion
f\colon\Sigma_{k}\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
via the
\SU_{3}
-representation
\rho^{+}=\rho_{\lambda_{0}}=\rho
in
(
4.3
) for
c_{0}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
or by
\rho^{-}=(\rho^{-1})^{T}
for
c_{0}=-\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
.
Specifically, for every

\gamma\in\Gamma_{deck},

\gamma^{*}f_{k}^{\pm}=\rho^{\pm}(\gamma^{-1})\circ f_{k}^{\pm}.

For
k\geq 4
, the full automorphism group of the Riemann surface
\Sigma_{k}
has order
6k^{2},
and is given by the semi-direct product
\text{Aut}(\Sigma_{k})=(\mathbb{Z}_{k}\times\mathbb{Z}_{k})\rtimes S_{3}
.
The additional
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-factor corresponds to the involution
\delta\colon\Sigma_{k}\to\Sigma_{k}
defined by

[x:y:z]\mapsto[\exp(-\tfrac{2\pi i}{3k})z:y:\exp(\tfrac{2\pi i}{3k})x].

On
\mathbb{CP}^{1}=\Sigma_{k}/\Gamma_{deck}
,
\delta
corresponds to the involution
z\mapsto z^{-1}
.
Note that
\delta
transforms the cubic differential
Q
of the minimal Lagrangian into its negative. Consequently, it cannot be lifted to a
\SU_{3}
-symmetry. On the other hand, it follows from the uniqueness part of the implicit function theorem that
\delta
lifts to an
\mathrm{U}_{3}
-symmetry
\hat{\delta}
of the immersion.
Since
\delta^{2}=\Id
on
\Sigma_{k}
, any ambient lift
\hat{\delta}\in\mathrm{U}_{3}
induces a projective transformation of order two. Hence

\hat{\delta}^{2}=\zeta^{m}\Id

for some
m\in\{0,1,2\}
, and therefore

(\det\hat{\delta})^{2}=\det(\hat{\delta}^{2})=1.

Thus
\det\hat{\delta}=\pm 1
. The case
\det\hat{\delta}=1
would imply
\hat{\delta}\in\SU_{3}
, which is impossible since

\delta^{*}Q=-Q
. Hence
\det\hat{\delta}=-1
.
Since a lift is unique up to multiplication by a cube root of unity, and this does not change the determinant, the determinant of the ambient lift defines a well-defined character

(6.2)

\varepsilon\colon\Aut(\Sigma_{k})\to\{\pm 1\}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}.

Theorem 9
.

For all sufficiently large
k
, the full holomorphic automorphism group
\Aut(\Sigma_{k})
acts on the minimal Lagrangian immersion
f_{k}\colon\Sigma_{k}\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
, and the associated character

\varepsilon\colon\Aut(\Sigma_{k})\to\{\pm 1\}

satisfies

\ker(\varepsilon)=(\mathbb{Z}_{k}\times\mathbb{Z}_{k})\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{3}=\Gamma_{deck}^{op}.

Proof.

By Theorem
8
, the immersion
f_{k}
is
\Gamma_{deck}
-equivariant, hence
\Gamma_{deck}^{op}\subset\ker(\varepsilon)
. The involution
\delta
lifts to a
\mathrm{U}_{3}
-symmetry with determinant
-1
, so
\delta\notin\ker(\varepsilon)
. Since

\Aut(\Sigma_{k})=\Gamma_{deck}^{op}\sqcup\delta\Gamma_{deck}^{op},

the claim follows.
∎

6.2.1.
Real involutions

Anti-holomorphic involutive symmetries of a minimal Lagrangian immersion
f\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
arise from reflections either across a totally real projective plane
\mathbb{RP}^{2}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{2}
or across a complex projective line
\mathbb{CP}^{1}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{2}
. Such a symmetry induces an anti-holomorphic involution
\mu
of
\Sigma
satisfying

\hat{\mu}\circ f=f\circ\mu.

If
\gamma
parametrizes a component of the fixed point set
\mathrm{Fix}(\mu)
, then
\gamma
is a geodesic for the induced metric. In the totally real case the cubic differential
Q
is real along
\gamma
, whereas in the complex-line case it is purely imaginary along

\gamma
. Conversely, by Schwarz reflection, a geodesic arc along which
Q
is real, respectively purely imaginary, extends to the fixed-point set of a symmetry of the corresponding type.

Consider the real involution

\mu\colon\Sigma_{k}\to\Sigma_{k};\,[x:y:z]\mapsto[\bar{x}:\exp(\tfrac{2\pi i}{3k})\bar{y}:\exp(\tfrac{4\pi i}{3k})\bar{z}],

which induces the real involution
\underline{\mu}
given by

[x,y]\mapsto[\bar{y},\bar{x}]

on
\mathbb{CP}^{1}=\Sigma_{k}/(\mathbb{Z}_{k}\times\mathbb{Z}_{k}).

Note that the cubic differential
Q
of the immersions constructed in Theorem
8
is real along the fixed-point set of
\underline{\mu}
because
\lambda_{0}^{3}=i
.
By the reality conditions of the potentials in Section
5
,
\mu
is induced by an extrinsic symmetry
\hat{\mu}
of the minimal Lagrangian immersion
f_{k}
, necessarily induced by the reflection across a real plane.
Specifically,

p_{1}^{1}=[0:1:\exp(\tfrac{\pi i}{3k})],

p_{1}^{2}=\sigma^{-1}(p_{1}^{1}),
and
p_{1}^{3}=\sigma(p_{1}^{1})

are fixed points of
\mu.

Furthermore, from the holomorphic symmetries of
f_{k}
we know that

f_{k}(p_{1}^{1})=[1:0:0]
,
and similarly
f_{k}(p_{1}^{2})=[0:1:0]

and
f_{k}(p_{1}^{3})=[0:0:1].

Moreover,
