\kappa\colon\Sigma_{k}\to\Sigma_{k};\,[x:y:z]\mapsto[\bar{x}:\bar{z}:\bar{y}]

is another real involution of
\Sigma_{k}
that induces
[x:y]\mapsto[\bar{x}:\bar{y}]
on the quotient
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
.
It has
p_{1}^{1}
and
q_{1,1}:=[1:1:1]
as fixed points (both in the same component of the set of fixed points). Furthermore,
Q
is imaginary along the fixed-point set of
\kappa
, and it lifts to a reflection
\hat{\kappa}
across a complex line. By the holomorphic symmetries,
f_{k}(q_{1,1})=[1:1:1]
.
The two real symmetries
\mu
and
\kappa
are related by an element of
\Aut(\Sigma_{k})
whose character (
6.2
) is
-1
.

6.3.
Area formulas

The area of the minimal Lagrangian surfaces
f_{k}\colon\Sigma_{k}\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
constructed in Theorem
8
above can be calculated using Theorem
5
. Note that each of the three singular points
0,1,\infty\in\mathbb{CP}^{1}
can be desingularized on a specific covering, as shown in Lemma
5.1
. We therefore only need to compute the corresponding contributions from these 3 points and multiply them by the number of preimages in
\Sigma_{k}
.

6.3.1.
Contribution from the poles over
z=0

We start with the meromorphic DPW potential (
5.6
)

d+\omega=(d+\eta).g
.
Here

\eta=R_{0}\tfrac{dz}{z}+R_{1}\tfrac{dz}{z-1}

with
R_{0}
and
R_{1}
as in (
5.4
) for
a,b,c\in\,\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}
satisfying

4ab-c^{2}=-1.

For the pullback
\pi_{3}^{*}(d+\omega)
via

\pi_{3}(w)=w^{3}=z,
we can remove the singularity at
w=0
by applying the diagonal gauge
g_{0}=\text{diag}(1,w,1/w)
, see (
5.8
) and (
5.9
). Since
g_{0}
is constant in
\lambda

we get zero contribution in (
3.14
) from the points lying over
z=0.

6.3.2.
Contribution from the singularities over
z=\infty

As in the proof of Lemma
5.1
, we pullback via
w^{3}=z
, and then desingularize
d+\hat{\omega}=\pi_{3}^{*}(d+\omega)
by using the gauge (
5.10
).
A direct computation of the residue term yields

\tr\Res_{w=\infty}(g_{\infty}^{(1)}(g_{\infty}^{(0)})^{-1}\hat{\omega}^{({-1})})=1-3c^{(0)}t

where
c^{(0)}=c(\lambda=0)
as above .
Note that there are
k^{2}
many points lying over

w=\infty.

6.3.3.
Contribution from the singularities over
z=1

As in the proof of Lemma
5.1
, we pull back via
z=\pi_{k}(y)=y^{k}+1,

and desingularize
d+\tilde{\omega}:=\pi_{k}^{*}(d+\omega)
via (
5.11
).

A direct computation shows that each of the
the
3k
points on
\Sigma_{k}

lying over
z=1

contributes with

\tr\Res_{w=0}(g_{k}^{(1)}(g_{k}^{(0)})^{-1}\tilde{\omega}^{(-1)})=2-2c^{(0)}.

Using these preliminary calculations, we obtain:

Theorem 10
.

The area of the minimal Lagrangian immersion
f_{k}^{\pm}\colon\Sigma_{k}\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
of genus

g=\tfrac{1}{2}(k-1)(k-2)
constructed in Theorem
8
is

\mathrm{Area}(f_{k}^{\pm})=6\pi(1-c^{(0)})\,k,

where
c^{(0)}
is the constant term in the
\lambda
expansion of
c=c^{(0)}+\lambda^{6}c^{(1)}+\dots
in
(
5.4
).
Furthermore, there is a (convergent) analytic expansion as
k\to\infty
of the renormalized area as

\tfrac{1}{k}\mathrm{Area}(f_{k}^{\pm})=6\pi\,(1\mp\sqrt{\tfrac{1}{3}}\,)+O(\tfrac{1}{k}),

where the sign depends on the choice of the initial data
c_{0}=\pm\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
in Lemma
5.9
.

Proof.

We first apply the energy formula (
3.14
) to the primitive harmonic map
\psi
for the smooth solutions on

\Sigma_{k}
.

The points over
z=0
contribute 0.
There are
k^{2}
points over
z=\infty
, each contributing
1-3c^{(0)}(t)t
,
and
3k
points over
z=1
, each contributing
2-2c^{(0)}(t)
.

(6.3)

\begin{split}\tfrac{1}{2\pi}E(\psi)&=k^{2}(1-3c^{(0)}t)+3k(2-2c^{(0)})=k^{2}+6k-9kc^{(0)}\\
&=2g-2+9k(1-c^{(0)}).\end{split}

Using the relation
(
2.23
) between the area of
f
and the energy of the primitive harmonic map
\psi

we obtain

\mathrm{Area}(f_{k}^{\pm})=6\pi k(1-c^{(0)}).

For the second part, we observe that using
t=\tfrac{1}{k}

\tfrac{1}{k}\mathrm{Area}(f_{k}^{\pm})=6\pi(1-c^{(0)}(t))

can be interpolated through a real analytic function in
t\sim 0.

Using the initial data

c_{0}=\pm\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
, the result follows.
∎

Corollary 6.3
.

For all sufficiently large
k
, the two immersions
f_{k}^{+}
and
f_{k}^{-}
are not congruent.

Proof.

By Theorem
10
, their areas have different asymptotic expansions.
Hence their areas are different for all sufficiently large
k
, and the surfaces cannot be congruent.
∎

For the lower area choice of initial data
c_{0}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
, we show in
Theorem
13
below
that the first-order coefficients in the
t
-expansion of
a,b,c
vanish. Consequently, we obtain an improved expansion for the lower area surfaces:

\tfrac{1}{k}\mathrm{Area}(f_{k}^{+})=6\pi\,(1-\sqrt{\tfrac{1}{3}}\,)+O(\tfrac{1}{k^{2}}).

With more computational effort, our methods can also be used to calculate the quadratic and higher-order terms in the
area expansion in
\tfrac{1}{k};
compare with
[
11
]
.

6.4.
Willmore functional estimates

The Willmore functional for an immersion
f\colon\Sigma\to M
into a Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
is defined to be

\mathrm{W}(f)=\int_{\Sigma}(|H|^{2}+\bar{K})dA

where
dA
is the induced area form,
H
is the mean curvature vector, and
\bar{K}
is the sectional curvature of the tangent planes
\im(df)
with respect to
g
.
The functional plays an important role in the geometry of surfaces and their applications in general relativity.

The Willmore functional has been studied in detail for Lagrangian surfaces in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
(
[
47
]
) and
in
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
(
[
48
]
).
By analogy with the Willmore conjecture in three-dimensional space, which has been proved by Marques and Neves
[
45
]
,
it is conjectured that the Willmore functional for surfaces in
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
of genus
g>0
is minimized by the Clifford torus

\{[x,y,z]\in\mathbb{CP}^{2}\mid|x|=|y|=|z|=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\}.

If our existence theorem covered the case
t=\tfrac{1}{3}
, the surface constructed in Theorem
8
for
k=3

would be (the 3-fold covering of) the Clifford torus.

Theorem 11
.

Let
\Sigma_{k}
be the Fermat curve of genus
\tfrac{1}{2}(k-1)(k-2).

Consider the holomorphic minimal immersion
F_{k}\colon\Sigma_{k}\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}

and the minimal Lagrangian immersion
f_{k}=f_{k}^{+}\colon\Sigma_{k}\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
provided by Theorem
8
for the initial choice
c_{0}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
. Then

\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{\mathrm{W}(f_{k})}{\mathrm{W}(F_{k})}=\tfrac{1}{2}(3-\sqrt{3})<1.

Proof.

For our normalization of the Fubini–Study metric, a holomorphic curve of degree
k
has area
\pi k
.
Thus the Willmore energy of a holomorphic curve of degree
k
is

\mathrm{W}(F_{k})=4\mathrm{Area}(F_{k})=4\pi\deg(F_{k})=4\pi k.

By
[
48
]
, the Willmore functional for minimal Lagrangian surfaces is also given by the area

\mathrm{W}(f_{k})=\mathrm{Area}(f_{k}).

Consequently, the theorem follows from Theorem
10
.
∎

Let
k=3n.

Consider the unbranched 3-fold covering
\Sigma_{3n}\to X_{n}
in (
6.1
).
By Riemann–Hurwitz, the genus of
X_{n}
is given by

g(X_{n})=\tfrac{1}{2}(3n^{2}-3n+2)\leq\tfrac{1}{2}(3n-1)(3n-2)=g(\Sigma_{3n})

with equality if and only if
n=1
. In that case the genus of both surfaces is 1.
The following lemma shows that there are infinitely many Fermat curves
\Sigma_{\tilde{k}}
of the same genus as
X_{n}=\Sigma_{3n}/\mathbb{Z}_{3}
for suitable
\tilde{k},n\in\mathbb{N}.

Lemma 6.4
.

There are infinitely many pairs

(\tilde{k},n)\in\mathbb{N}
with

3n^{2}-3n+2=(\tilde{k}-1)(\tilde{k}-2).

Proof.

The equation
is equivalent to

(2\tilde{k}-3)^{2}-3(2n-1)^{2}=6.

This is a Pell-type equation in
\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{3}]
. Starting from the solution

(\tilde{k},n)=(3,1),

and multiplying
2\tilde{k}-3+(2n-1)\sqrt{3}
by powers of
7+4\sqrt{3}
, one obtains infinitely many integer solutions.
∎

We denote by
\underline{f}_{n}\colon X_{n}\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
the map such that
f_{3n}=\underline{f}_{n}\circ\pi
for
\pi\colon\Sigma_{3n}\to\Sigma_{3n}/\mathbb{Z}_{3}=X_{n}.

We compare the Willmore energies of these surfaces for the same (large) genus.

Theorem 12
.

Let
\epsilon>0
.
There exists
N_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all

(\tilde{k},n)
with
n>N_{0}
and

3n^{2}-3n+2=(\tilde{k}-1)(\tilde{k}-2)
one has

\mathrm{W}(f_{\tilde{k}})>(\sqrt{3}-\epsilon)\mathrm{W}(\underline{f}_{n}).

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
10
together with the observation that

3\mathrm{W}(\underline{f}_{n})=\mathrm{W}(f_{3n})

and the relation

\tilde{k}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sqrt{12n^{2}-12n+13}+3\right)
, which implies

\lim_{n\to\infty}\tfrac{\tilde{k}}{n}=\sqrt{3}.

∎

Remark 6.5
.

Note that for both minimal Lagrangian surfaces and holomorphic curves, the modified Willmore functional
\mathrm{W}^{-}
(see
[
48
]
for details) coincides with the area.
For
k
large, the area of
f_{k}
is approximately
6\left(1-\sqrt{\frac{1}{3}}\right)>1
times the area of the holomorphic Fermat curve
F_{k}
. Of course, the holomorphic immersion and the minimal Lagrangian immersion
lie in different homotopy classes.
Similarly, when
k=3n
is large, the area of
\underline{f}_{n}
exceeds – by roughly the factor

2\sqrt{3}\left(1-\sqrt{\frac{1}{3}}\right)>1
– the area of the Fermat curve
F_{\tilde{k}}
of the corresponding genus. Our computer experiments suggest that this is true for all
(\tilde{k},n)

satisfying
3n^{2}-3n+2=(\tilde{k}-1)(\tilde{k}-2)
, with a single exception occurring for the Clifford torus.

7.
Embeddedness of the special Legendrian surfaces

In this section, we investigate the special Legendrian lifts (Corollary
6.1
) of the minimal Lagrangian surfaces constructed in Theorem
8
.
Topological obstructions prevent these minimal Lagrangian surfaces from being embedded; see for example
[
60
]
. We show, however, that the special Legendrian lifts
\hat{f}_{k}^{+}
for the initial condition
c_{0}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}

are embedded minimal surfaces in
\mathbb{S}^{5}
.
We expect the second family of special Legendrian surfaces
\hat{f}_{k}^{-}

to have self-intersections.

As a byproduct of our analysis, we obtain a detailed geometric description of the shape of the surfaces
\hat{f}_{k}:=\hat{f}_{k}^{+}
for large
k
.
In the limit
k\to\infty,
the geometry of
\hat{f}_{k}
resembles that of the Lawson minimal surfaces
\xi_{k,k}
in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
: these arise as desingularizations of infinitely many totally geodesic discs foliating the interior and exterior of the Clifford torus and meeting orthogonally; compare with Figure
3
.
In our setting, the blow-up of the tangent space at an intersection point yields a minimal Lagrangian surface in
\mathbb{C}^{2},
analogous to the classical doubly periodic Scherk surface
for
\xi_{k,k}
; see Theorem
14
.

Strategy

We begin by outlining the overall strategy of the proof. The key observation is that the asymptotic behaviour of the DPW potential enables the computation of the limiting surface via explicit expansion of the loop group factorization in
t
. In order to obtain sufficient geometric control it is necessary to compute the DPW potential to second order in the parameter
t.
This refinement is achieved by explicitly solving the monodromy problem of Section
5

to one additional order, with the monodromies themselves expressed via iterated integrals; see Section
7.1
.

In the limit
k\to\infty
corresponding to
t\to 0,
two fundamentally distinct asymptotic regimes emerge. The first occurs on compact subsets of the thrice-punctured sphere
\Sigma_{k}/(\mathbb{Z}_{k}\times\mathbb{Z}_{k})
, where each of the three singularities has order
k
. In this region, we show that the surfaces converge to Scherk-type minimal Lagrangian surfaces in the complex plane, identified with a horizontal subspace of the Hopf fibration. These limit surfaces possess infinite topological type, although their embeddedness can be established without difficulty; see Section
7.3
for details.
The second regime concerns the behaviour near the three singular points on
\Sigma_{k}/(\mathbb{Z}_{k}\times\mathbb{Z}_{k})
. We show in Theorem
15
, that, in neighbourhoods of each singularity, the surfaces converge to a spherical cap of radius
\arctan(\sqrt{2})
, in precise agreement with the area asymptotics in Theorem
10
.

The principal analytical effort in establishing embeddedness lies in controlling the transition between these two asymptotic regimes, as well as the relative positioning of the spherical caps at higher order in
t.
This analysis is carried out in Section
7.7
, relying on several preparatory estimates established earlier in the section, e.g., Proposition
7.8
, Lemma
7.11
, and Theorem
16
.

Figure 3.
Three
spherical caps
with centers
P_{j}
along the coordinate circles
C_{j}
, respectively, meet at one
point
Q
which lies on the Clifford torus.
At the
points
Q
on the Clifford torus, the surface becomes smooth after a blow-up. It is a doubly periodic minimal Lagrangian surface in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
analogous to the doubly periodic Scherk surface, and has three asymptotically

planar ends
.

7.1.
Computation of the parameters to first order

The special Legendrian surface
\hat{f}_{k}
is constructed via loop group factorization from a twisted potential
d+\omega
. The potential
\omega
itself is determined by
t=\tfrac{1}{k}
and
a(t),b(t),c(t)\in\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}
, where
a(t),b(t)
and
c(t)
are obtained in Proposition
5.10

via the implicit function theorem.
In particular, the functions
a,b,c\colon[0,\epsilon)\to\mathcal{W}^{\tau}_{\mathbb{R}}
are real analytic in
t
, and their values at
t=0
are

a_{0}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{6}},

b_{0}=-\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{6}},
and
c_{0}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}.

We now compute their first-order derivatives at
t=0
, by expanding the solution given by
the implicit function.
We do the computations on the 3-fold covering

\pi_{3}\colon\mathbb{CP}^{1}\to\mathbb{CP}^{1},z\mapsto z^{3}.

We start with the potential
d+\eta:=d+\eta_{t,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0}}
defined in (
5.3
), i.e.
we do not use the potential
d+\eta_{t,a(t),b(t),c(t)}
at the moment.
The pullback of
d+\eta
by

\pi_{3}
followed by the desingularization gauge

\tilde{g}=\mathrm{diag}(1,z,z^{-1})

yields

\pi_{3}^{*}(d+\eta).\tilde{g}=d+t\,(Q_{1}\frac{dz}{z-1}+Q_{2}\frac{dz}{z-\zeta}+Q_{3}\frac{dz}{z-\zeta^{2}}),

where as before we have

\zeta=\exp(\tfrac{2\pi i}{3})
. Setting
\hat{\zeta}=\text{diag}(1,\zeta,\zeta^{2})
we have

Q_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{p}{\lambda^{3}}&\frac{q}{\lambda^{3}}\\
-p&0&ic\\
-q&-ic&0\\
\end{pmatrix},\hskip 20.00003ptQ_{2}=\hat{\zeta}^{-1}Q_{1}\hat{\zeta}\,,\hskip 20.00003ptQ_{3}=\hat{\zeta}^{-1}Q_{2}\hat{\zeta}\\

for

p=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{6}}(1-\lambda^{3})

and

q=-i\,\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{6}}(1+\lambda^{3}).

For computational convenience, we apply another coordinate transformation. Set

\hat{\eta}:=-Q_{1}\tfrac{dz}{1-z}+Q_{3}\tfrac{dz}{z}

with residue
Q_{2}
at
z=\infty.
Thus,
the connection
d+t\hat{\eta}
is the pullback of the
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
-equivariant connection

d+t\,\sum_{j}Q_{j}\tfrac{dz}{z-\zeta^{j-1}}

under the Möbius transformation

(7.1)

z\mapsto\tfrac{2iz-i+\sqrt{3}}{\left(\sqrt{3}-i\right)z+2i}

which maps

0
to
\zeta^{2}
,
1
to
1
, and
\infty
to
\zeta.

Our new basepoint
q_{0}
is therefore

q_{0}=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(1+i\sqrt{3}\right),

which is mapped under the Möbius transformation to
0
.
All logarithms below use the principal branch (real on
(0,\infty)
), and all dilogarithms
\mathrm{Li}_{2}

use the standard branch with cut
[1,\infty)
; this fixes the monodromy signs unambiguously.

In order to determine the first-order terms in the expansion of
the real analytic functions
a,b,c
at
t=0

we first consider a solution

\tilde{\Psi}=\tilde{\Psi}_{0}+\tilde{\Psi}_{1}t+\tilde{\Psi}_{2}t^{2}+\dots

of the linear ODE

d\tilde{\Psi}+t\hat{\eta}\tilde{\Psi}=0\hskip 20.00003pt\text{
with initial condition}\hskip 20.00003pt\tilde{\Psi}(\tfrac{1}{2})=\Id.

The choice of the basepoint
\frac{1}{2}
is made in order to apply well-known results about the monodromies of iterated integrals of the (poly)logarithm; see
[
19
]
. To preempt any possible confusion, we remark this is neither the same as nor related to the basepoint
\frac{1}{2}
on the furthest
quotient thrice-punctured sphere where the relative character variety was taken.

Expanding the linear ODE in
t
gives

\begin{split}0&=d\tilde{\Psi}_{0},\qquad 0=d\tilde{\Psi}_{1}+\hat{\eta}\tilde{\Psi}_{0},\qquad 0=d\tilde{\Psi}_{2}+\hat{\eta}\tilde{\Psi}_{1},\qquad\dots\end{split}

Hence,

\tilde{\Psi}_{0}=\Id,

and using the principal branch of the logarithm, which is real on the positive real axis,

(7.2)

\tilde{\Psi}_{1}=-\int_{\tfrac{1}{2}}^{z}\hat{\eta}=-Q_{1}\log(1-z)-Q_{3}\log(z)-(Q_{1}+Q_{3})\log(2)

as well as

\begin{split}\tilde{\Psi}_{2}=&-\int_{\tfrac{1}{2}}^{z}\hat{\eta}\tilde{\Psi}_{1}\\
=&\int_{\tfrac{1}{2}}^{z}(-Q_{1}\tfrac{dz}{1-z}+Q_{3}\tfrac{dz}{z})(Q_{1}\log(1-z)+Q_{3}\log(z))+\int_{\tfrac{1}{2}}^{z}(-Q_{1}\tfrac{dz}{1-z}+Q_{3}\tfrac{dz}{z})(Q_{1}+Q_{3})\log(2)\\
=&\tfrac{1}{2}Q_{1}^{2}\log^{2}(1-z)-Q_{3}Q_{1}\Li 2(z)-Q_{1}Q_{3}\Li 2(1-z)+\tfrac{1}{2}Q_{3}^{2}\log^{2}(z)+\tfrac{1}{2}(Q_{1}^{2}+Q_{3}^{2})\log^{2}(2)\\
&-(Q_{1}Q_{3}+Q_{3}Q_{1})\Li 2(\tfrac{1}{2})+(Q_{1}\log(1-z)+Q_{3}\log(z))(Q_{1}+Q_{3})\log(2)\\
&-(Q_{1}+Q_{3})(Q_{1}+Q_{3})\log^{2}(2)\\
\end{split}

where

\Li 2(\tfrac{1}{2})=\tfrac{1}{12}\left(\pi^{2}-6\log^{2}(2)\right)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{
and}\hskip 20.00003pt\li_{1}(z)=-\log(1-z),\hskip 10.00002ptd\li_{k}(z)=\tfrac{dz}{z}\li_{k-1}(z).

The monodromies along the loop

\tilde{\gamma}_{1}\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,1\};\,s\mapsto 1-\tfrac{1}{2}\exp(2\pi is)

based at
\frac{1}{2}
of the involved functions are given by (see e.g.
[
19
]
)

\begin{split}\tilde{\gamma}_{1}^{*}\log(z)&=\log(z)\\
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}^{*}\log^{2}(z)&=\log^{2}(z)\\
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}^{*}\Li 2(1-z)&=\Li 2(1-z)\\
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}^{*}\log(1-z)&=\log(1-z)+2\pi i\\
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}^{*}\log^{2}(1-z)&=\log^{2}(1-z)+4\pi i\log(1-z)-4\pi^{2}\\
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}^{*}\Li 2(z)&=\Li 2(z)-2\pi i\log(z).\\
\end{split}

From this we obtain

\begin{split}\tilde{\gamma}_{1}^{*}\tilde{\Psi}_{1}=\tilde{\Psi}_{1}-2\pi iQ_{1}\end{split}

and

\begin{split}\tilde{\gamma}_{1}^{*}\tilde{\Psi}_{2}=\dots=&\tilde{\Psi}_{2}+\tilde{\Psi}_{1}(-2\pi iQ_{1})-2\pi^{2}Q_{1}^{2}+2\pi i[Q_{1},Q_{3}]\log(2).\\
\end{split}

Therefore

\begin{split}\tilde{\gamma}_{1}^{*}\tilde{\Psi}=&\tilde{\Psi}(1-2\pi iQ_{1}t+(-2\pi^{2}Q_{1}^{2}+2\pi i[Q_{1},Q_{3}]\log(2))t^{2}+\dots)\end{split}

and hence the expansion of the monodromy along
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}
based at
\tfrac{1}{2}
is

M(\tilde{\gamma}_{1})=1-2\pi iQ_{1}t+(-2\pi^{2}Q_{1}^{2}+2\pi i[Q_{1},Q_{3}]\log(2))t^{2}+\dots).

To compute the monodromy based at
q_{0}
,
we expand the parallel transport
from
\tfrac{1}{2}
to
q_{0}
, i.e., we consider the end point of the parallel transport
P
along the vertical line from
\tfrac{1}{2}
to

q_{0}=\tfrac{1+i\sqrt{3}}{2}
:

\begin{split}P:=&\tilde{\Psi}(q_{0})=\Id+t\tilde{\Psi}_{1}(q_{0})+t^{2}\tilde{\Psi}_{2}(q_{0})+\dots.\end{split}

Thus

\Psi=\tilde{\Psi}P^{-1}

is a solution of

d\Psi+\hat{\eta}\Psi=0

with initial value

\Psi(q_{0})=\Id,

and we obtain for the monodromy along
\tilde{\gamma}_{1}
(based at
q_{0}
and winding around the singular point
z_{0}=1
):

\begin{split}M(\tilde{\gamma}_{1})=&PM(\tilde{\gamma}_{1})P^{-1}\\
=&(\Id+t\tilde{\Psi}_{1}(q_{0})+\dots)(\Id-2\pi iQ_{1}t+t^{2}(-2\pi^{2}Q_{1}^{2}+2\pi i[Q_{1},Q_{3}]\log(2))+\dots)(\Id-t\tilde{\Psi}_{1}(q_{0})+\dots)\\
=&\Id-2\pi iQ_{1}t+t^{2}(-2\pi^{2}Q_{1}^{2}+2\pi i[Q_{1},Q_{3}]\log(2)-2\pi i[\tilde{\Psi}_{1}(q_{0}),Q_{1}])+O(t^{3})\end{split}

where we have by (
7.2
) that

\tilde{\Psi}_{1}(q_{0})=(Q_{1}-Q_{3})\tfrac{\pi i}{3}-(Q_{1}+Q_{3})\log(2).

Plugging in the values of
p,q,c
at
t=0
, we obtain the expansion

\begin{split}M_{t}(\tilde{\gamma}_{1})^{-1}\frac{d}{dt}M_{t}(\tilde{\gamma}_{1})=&\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{i\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\pi\left(\lambda^{3}-1\right)}{\lambda^{3}}&-\frac{\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\pi\left(\lambda^{3}+1\right)}{\lambda^{3}}\\
-i\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\pi\left(\lambda^{3}-1\right)&0&\frac{2\pi}{\sqrt{3}}\\
\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\pi\left(\lambda^{3}+1\right)&-\frac{2\pi}{\sqrt{3}}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}\\
&+t\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{4i\pi^{2}\left(\lambda\,^{6}+1\right)}{3\sqrt{3}\lambda^{3}}&0&0\\
0&\frac{2i\pi^{2}\left(\lambda\,^{6}+1\right)}{3\sqrt{3}\lambda^{3}}&0\\
0&0&\frac{2i\pi^{2}\left(\lambda\,^{6}+1\right)}{3\sqrt{3}\lambda^{3}}\\
\end{pmatrix}\;+O(t^{2}),\end{split}

which is unitary to second order, and satisfies the extrinsic closing condition to second order.
From these computations, we are able to deduce the following theorem.

Theorem 13
.

The linear terms of the expansion in
t
of
a,b,c
of the monodromy problem vanish, i.e.,
a^{\prime}(0)=b^{\prime}(0)=c^{\prime}(0)=0
. The positive unitarizer

R\in{\bf\SL}(\mathbb{D})
at
q_{0}
can be chosen to be diagonal with positive real entries at
\lambda=0.
Moreover,
R(t)
is uniquely
determined by these properties,
and its Taylor expansion at
t=0
is

R(t)=\Id+O(t^{2}).

Proof.

We first prove the uniqueness part. Assume
\tilde{R}=\tilde{R}(t)
is a second unitarizer with the same properties.
We use the fact that the monodromy is irreducible
for generic
\lambda
with
0<|\lambda|\leq 1
. This can be shown similarly to the proof of
Proposition
5.5
. We omit the details. (As opposed to
\Sigma_{k}/\Gamma_{deck}
, the monodromy on
\Sigma_{k}/(\mathbb{Z}_{k}\times\mathbb{Z}_{k})
is reducible for some spectral parameters, for example
for the Sym point
\lambda_{0}.
)
In particular, the monodromy representation is unitary for generic
\lambda\in\mathbb{S}^{1}.

Thus,
(R^{-1}\tilde{R})(\lambda)

is unitary for generic
\lambda\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
, and hence for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{S}^{1}.
Since
R,\tilde{R}\in{\bf\SL}(\mathbb{D})
are both
normalized, the claim follows from the uniqueness of the Iwasawa decomposition.

Recall that
d+t\hat{\eta}
is the desingularization by
\tilde{g}
of the pullback of the potential
d+\eta_{t,a_{0},b_{0},c_{0}}
.
Consider also the desingularization by
\tilde{g}
of the pullback of the potential
d+\eta_{t,a(t),b(t),c(t)}
constructed by the implicit function theorem.
After conjugation with its unique positive unitarizer the latter potential has unitary monodromy at the base point
q_{0}
(corresponding to
z=0
after the Möbius transformation).
By
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
-equivariance with respect to
z\mapsto\zeta z

and by uniqueness of the positive unitarizer,
R
commutes with
\hat{\zeta}
; hence
R
is diagonal.
Its expansion in
t
is given by

R(t)=\Id+t\,\mathrm{diag}(d_{1},d_{2},-d_{1}-d_{2})+O(t^{2})

where
d_{1},d_{2}
are holomorphic functions in
\lambda^{3}
which evaluate to real positive values at
\lambda=0.

As in Lemma
5.7
we compute the contributions of
a^{\prime}(0),b^{\prime}(0),c^{\prime}(0)

in the second order term of the expansion of the monodromy.
We will use the coordinate transformation

a=\tfrac{1}{2}(p+iq)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptb=-\tfrac{-p+iq}{2\lambda^{3}}

and denote the first-order derivatives in
t
at
t=0
by
\dot{p}
and
\dot{q}
.
Then,
\dot{p},\dot{q}
and
\dot{c}
are holomorphic functions in
\lambda^{3}
.
After conjugation with
R(t)
, the expansion of the monodromy
\tilde{M}
of
d+\eta_{t,a(t),b(t),c(t)}
then satisfies

(7.3)

\begin{split}&R^{-1}(t)M_{t}(\gamma_{1})^{-1}\frac{d}{dt}M_{t}(\gamma_{1})R(t)\\
=&\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{i\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\pi\left(\lambda^{3}-1\right)}{\lambda^{3}}&-\frac{\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\pi\left(\lambda^{3}+1\right)}{\lambda^{3}}\\
-i\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\pi\left(\lambda^{3}-1\right)&0&\frac{2\pi}{\sqrt{3}}\\
\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\pi\left(\lambda^{3}+1\right)&-\frac{2\pi}{\sqrt{3}}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}+t\,\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{4i\pi^{2}\left(\lambda\,^{6}+1\right)}{3\sqrt{3}\lambda^{3}}&0&0\\
0&\frac{2i\pi^{2}\left(\lambda\,^{6}+1\right)}{3\sqrt{3}\lambda^{3}}&0\\
0&0&\frac{2i\pi^{2}\left(\lambda\,^{6}+1\right)}{3\sqrt{3}\lambda^{3}}\\
\end{pmatrix}\\
&-4\pi\,t\,\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{i\left(\sqrt{6}\left(\lambda^{3}-1\right)(d_{1}-d_{2})+6\dot{p}\right)}{6\lambda^{3}}&\frac{-\sqrt{6}\left(\lambda^{3}+1\right)(2d_{1}+d_{2})+6i\dot{q}}{6\lambda^{3}}\\
\frac{i\left(\lambda^{3}-1\right)(d_{1}-d_{2})}{\sqrt{6}}-i\dot{p}&0&\frac{d_{1}+2d_{2}}{\sqrt{3}}-\dot{c}\\
-\frac{\left(\lambda^{3}+1\right)(2d_{1}+d_{2})}{\sqrt{6}}-i\dot{q}&\frac{d_{1}+2d_{2}}{\sqrt{3}}+\dot{c}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}\;+O(t^{2})\end{split}

The diagonal part is already in
{\bf su}_{3}^{\hat{\tau}}(\mathbb{S}^{1}).
Since
\dot{c}
and
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
are holomorphic functions on an open neighbourhood of
the unit disc, one can easily deduce that
d_{1}+2d_{2}
and
\dot{c}
must be constants in order for (
7.3
) to be
{\bf su}_{3}^{\hat{\tau}}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-valued. Furthermore, we see that
d_{1}+2d_{2}
is imaginary and
\dot{c}
must be real, and therefore
d_{1}+2d_{2}=0

(as
d_{1}(0),d_{2}(0)\in\mathbb{R}
by assumption). A similar, but slightly more involved derivation shows
that if (
7.3
) is
{\bf su}_{3}^{\hat{\tau}}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
-valued then

\begin{split}\dot{p}&=p_{1}(\lambda^{3}-1),\qquad\dot{q}=q_{1}(\lambda^{3}+1),\quad\text{and}\quad d_{1}=d_{2}=0.\end{split}

In particular,
R(t)=\mathrm{Id}+O(t^{2}).
By (
4.3
), the monodromy on
\Sigma_{k}/(\mathbb{Z}_{k}\times\mathbb{Z}_{k})
at the Sym point
\lambda_{0}=\exp(\tfrac{2\pi i}{12})
is diagonal up to conjugation. Hence,
the three residues of the non-resonant Fuchsian system at the Sym point commute. This then implies

\dot{q}=-i\,\dot{p},

and

\dot{c}=\sqrt{2}\dot{p}.

Expanding the eigenvalues of the residues of
d+\eta_{t,a(t),b(t),c(t)}
in
t
then gives
\dot{p}=0,
as claimed.
∎

7.2.
Limit construction of the surfaces

To prove embeddedness, we need precise control of the surfaces for large
k
.
The integrable-systems setup allows us to compute their limit explicitly.
We carry out the main computations on the
3
-fold cover

\pi_{3}:\mathbb{CP}^{1}\to\mathbb{CP}^{1},\;z\mapsto z^{3},

which is branched over
0
and
\infty
. After pullback and desingularization by
\tilde{g}
, the three singular points
of the potential are located at
1,\zeta,\zeta^{2}
.
We consider the
k^{2}
-fold branched covering
\pi_{k}\colon\Sigma_{k}\to\mathbb{CP}^{1}=\Sigma_{k}/(\mathbb{Z}_{k}\times\mathbb{Z}_{k}),
and the induced
topological covering (compare with (
3.11
))

\widetilde{\pi}_{k}\colon\widetilde{S}:=\widetilde{\mathbb{CP}^{1}\setminus\{1,\zeta,\zeta^{2}\}}\longrightarrow\Sigma_{k}\setminus\pi_{k}^{-1}\{1,\zeta,\zeta^{2}\}.

The composition of
\hat{f}_{k}\circ\widetilde{\pi}_{k}
is well-defined on
\widetilde{S}
for all
k\geq k_{0}.

Furthermore, we can smoothly interpolate between those surfaces on
\widetilde{S}
:
Consider

\tilde{g}=\mathrm{diag}(1,z,z^{-1})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pth=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{\lambda}&0&0\\
0&\lambda&iz\\
0&0&1\\
\end{pmatrix}

and the
\hat{\tau}
-twisted potential

d+\omega_{t}:=(d+\pi_{3}^{*}\eta_{t,a(t),b(t),c(t)}).\tilde{g}h\,.

Note that

\tilde{g}h=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{\lambda}&0&0\\
0&\lambda&iz^{3}\\
0&0&1\\
\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&z&0\\
0&0&\frac{1}{z}\\
\end{pmatrix},

so we work with the pull-back of the
\hat{\tau}
-twisted potential in Lemma
5.1
, see (
5.7
).
Note that on
\mathbb{CP}^{1}\setminus\{1,\zeta,\zeta^{2}\},

the base point
q_{0}
in Theorem
13
corresponds to
z=0
.

Lemma 7.1
.

For each
t\in[0,\epsilon)
, the normalized positive diagonal unitarizer
R(t)

from Theorem
13

at the base point
z=0

is
\hat{\tau}
-twisted.

Proof.

Since

h(0)=\diag(\lambda^{-1},\lambda,1)

is diagonal and unitary, the same loop
R(t)
also unitarizes the monodromy of
d+\omega_{t}
at
z=0
.

Let
M_{t}(\lambda)
denote the monodromy of
d+\omega_{t}
at the base point
z=0
. Because
d+\omega_{t}
is
\hat{\tau}
-twisted, its monodromy satisfies

M_{t}(\xi\lambda)=\hat{\tau}\bigl(M_{t}(\lambda)\bigr).

Hence
\hat{\tau}(R(t,\lambda))
and
R(t,\xi\lambda)
are both normalized positive unitarizers of
M_{t}(\xi\lambda)
. By uniqueness of the normalized positive unitarizer, they coincide:

\hat{\tau}(R(t,\lambda))=R(t,\xi\lambda).

Therefore
R(t)\in{\bf SL}^{\hat{\tau}}(\mathbb{D})
.
∎

Now let
\Omega_{t}
be the parallel frame of
d+\omega_{t}
on
\widetilde{S}
normalized by

\Omega_{t}(z=0)=R(t).

Since both the potential
d+\omega_{t}
and the initial value
R(t)
are
\hat{\tau}
-twisted, uniqueness of solutions of the ODE implies that
\Omega_{t}
is
\hat{\tau}
-twisted as well. We then apply the
\hat{\tau}
-twisted Iwasawa decomposition and write

\Omega_{t}=B_{t}F_{t},\hskip 20.00003ptB_{t}\in{\bf SL}^{\hat{\tau}}_{B}(\mathbb{D}),\hskip 10.00002ptF_{t}\in{\bf SU}^{\hat{\tau}}(\mathbb{S}^{1}).

By Remark
3.13
, the map

(7.4)

\hat{f}_{t}:=F_{t}(\lambda_{0})^{-1}e_{1}\colon\widetilde{S}\to\mathbb{S}^{5}

is a special Legendrian immersion. For
t=\frac{1}{k}
, Corollary
3.21
yields

(7.5)

\hat{f}_{t}=\hat{f}_{k}\circ\widetilde{\pi}_{k}

up to an ambient
\SU_{3}
-transformation.

7.3.
The Scherk-type limit surface

As a first consequence of Theorem
13
, we determine the limit surface for
k\to\infty
on
compact subsets of the thrice-punctured sphere.

We record the following observation: Since
\hat{f}_{k}
is a horizontal lift of
f_{k}\colon\Sigma_{k}\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
through the Hopf fibration
\mathbb{S}^{5}\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
, we have

d_{p}\hat{f}_{k}(T_{p}\Sigma_{k})\subset\mathcal{H}_{q}=q^{\perp}\subset T_{q}\mathbb{S}^{5}.

Theorem 14
.

Let
\mathbb{K}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{1}\setminus\{1,\zeta,\zeta^{2}\}
be simply connected and compact with
0\in\mathbb{K}.

Consider
\mathbb{K}
as a subset of the universal covering
\widetilde{S}
by fixing a lift of
0.

On
\mathbb{K}
, we have uniform convergence

d\hat{f}_{t}\to 0\hskip 20.00003pt(t\to 0).

Since the family is normalized at
z=0
, it follows that
\hat{f}_{t}
converges uniformly on
\mathbb{K}
to a constant map with value
q\in\mathbb{S}^{5}
.
Denoting the derivative with respect to
t
at
t=0
by
\dot{\hat{f}}
, we then have

d\dot{\hat{f}}=\sqrt{\tfrac{3}{2}}\begin{pmatrix}e^{-\tfrac{\pi i}{3}}z\\
-e^{\tfrac{\pi i}{3}}\end{pmatrix}\frac{dz}{z^{3}-1}+\sqrt{\tfrac{3}{2}}\begin{pmatrix}e^{\tfrac{\pi i}{6}}\\
-e^{-\tfrac{\pi i}{6}}\bar{z}\end{pmatrix}\frac{d\bar{z}}{\bar{z}^{3}-1}\in\Omega^{1}({\mathbb{K}},\mathbb{C}^{2})

where we identify
\mathcal{H}_{q}=\mathbb{C}^{2}
with its standard Hermitian structure.

Proof.

The proof is a direct computation, analogous to the CMC and minimal-surface cases in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
\mathbb{S}^{3}
treated in
[
55
,
11
]
.
We work with the continuous parameter
t=\tfrac{1}{k}
.
Recall that the surface
\hat{f}_{t}
is constructed from the
\hat{\tau}
-twisted potential

d+\omega_{t}:=(d+\pi_{3}^{*}\eta_{t,a(t),b(t),c(t)}).\tilde{g}h.

We first need to expand the solution of

(d+\omega_{t})\Omega_{t}=0

with diagonal initial condition

\Omega_{t}(z=0)=R(t)=\Id+O(t^{2}).

Then we split

\Omega_{t}=B_{t}F_{t}

where
B_{t}
is positive and normalized at
\lambda=0
and
F_{t}
is unitary along
\lambda\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
.
To understand the limiting surface, we use (
7.4
) and expand
F_{t}
in time
t
.

By construction, the monodromy of
\Omega_{t}
with base point
z=0
is unitary.
Since the Iwasawa decomposition is unique, the positive part

B_{t}
has trivial monodromy on the surface, both on the thrice-punctured sphere and on the covering
\Sigma_{k}
when
t=\tfrac{1}{k}
.
By Theorem
13
, the Taylor expansion of
\omega_{t}
is given by

\omega_{t}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&0&\frac{i}{\lambda}\\
0&0&0\\
\end{pmatrix}dz+\begin{pmatrix}0&-\frac{\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\left(\lambda^{3}-1\right)}{\lambda\left(z^{3}-1\right)}&-\frac{i\sqrt{6}\lambda z}{z^{3}-1}\\
\frac{\sqrt{6}\lambda z}{z^{3}-1}&-\frac{\sqrt{3}z^{2}}{z^{3}-1}&-\frac{i\sqrt{3}}{\lambda}\\
\frac{i\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\left(\lambda^{3}+1\right)}{\lambda\left(z^{3}-1\right)}&-\frac{i\sqrt{3}\lambda z}{z^{3}-1}&\frac{\sqrt{3}z^{2}}{z^{3}-1}\\
\end{pmatrix}dz\,t+O(t^{3}).

With this, we expand as before

\Omega_{t}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&-\tfrac{i}{\lambda}z\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}(\Id+t\xi_{1}+O(t^{2}))\hskip 20.00003pt\text{with}\hskip 20.00003pt\xi_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&y^{T}\\
x&*\\
\end{pmatrix}

where

y^{T}={\tiny\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac{\left(\lambda^{3}-1\right)\left(\sqrt{3}\left(2\log(1-z)-\log\left(z^{2}+z+1\right)\right)-6\tan^{-1}\left(\tfrac{2z+1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)+\pi\right)}{6\sqrt{2}\lambda},-\tfrac{i\left(\lambda^{3}+1\right)\left(\sqrt{3}\log\left(z^{2}+z+1\right)-2\sqrt{3}\log(1-z)-6\tan^{-1}\left(\tfrac{2z+1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)+\pi\right)}{6\sqrt{2}\lambda^{2}}\end{pmatrix}}

and

x=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\left(\lambda^{3}-1\right)\left(\sqrt{3}\log\left(z^{2}+z+1\right)-2\sqrt{3}\log(1-z)-6\tan^{-1}\left(\frac{2z+1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)+\pi\right)}{6\sqrt{2}\lambda^{2}}\\
-\frac{i\left(\lambda^{3}+1\right)\left(\sqrt{3}\left(2\log(1-z)-\log\left(z^{2}+z+1\right)\right)-6\tan^{-1}\left(\frac{2z+1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)+\pi\right)}{6\sqrt{2}\lambda}\end{pmatrix}.

Consider the expansion into positive and unitary parts

(7.6)

\Omega_{t}=\Omega_{0}(1+t\xi_{1}+\dots)=B_{t}F_{t}=B_{0}(1+tb_{1}+\dots)F_{0}(1+tX_{1}+\dots)

with explicit factorization at
t=0

(7.7)

\begin{split}\Omega_{0}&=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&-iz\lambda^{-1}\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}=B_{0}F_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&\sqrt{z\bar{z}+1}&0\\
0&\frac{i\lambda\bar{z}}{\sqrt{z\bar{z}+1}}&\frac{1}{\sqrt{z\bar{z}+1}}\\
\end{pmatrix}\,\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&\frac{1}{\sqrt{z\bar{z}+1}}&-\frac{iz}{\sqrt{z\bar{z}+1}}\lambda^{-1}\\
0&-\frac{i\bar{z}}{\sqrt{z\bar{z}+1}}\lambda&\frac{1}{\sqrt{z\bar{z}+1}}\\
\end{pmatrix}.\end{split}

We therefore obtain from
(
7.6
)
that

\xi_{1}=F_{0}^{-1}b_{1}F_{0}+X_{1},

where

b_{1}
and
X_{1}
are uniquely determined by the property of being elements in the positive and in the unitary loop algebra, respectively. Solving the linear system yields

b_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\beta_{1}&\beta_{2}\\
\alpha_{1}&*&*\\
\alpha_{2}&*&*\\
\end{pmatrix}

with

\begin{split}\alpha_{1}&=\tfrac{\lambda\left(-\sqrt{3}(z+1)\left(-\log\left(z^{2}+z+1\right)+2\log(1-z)-\log\left(\bar{z}^{2}+\bar{z}+1\right)+2\log(1-\bar{z})\right)-6(z-1)\tan^{-1}\left(\tfrac{2\bar{z}+1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)+6(z-1)\tan^{-1}\left(\tfrac{2z+1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)\right)}{6\sqrt{2z\bar{z}+2}}\\
\alpha_{2}&=\tfrac{i\lambda^{2}\left(\sqrt{3}(\bar{z}-1)\left(-\log\left(z^{2}+z+1\right)+2\log(1-z)-\log\left(\bar{z}^{2}+\bar{z}+1\right)+2\log(1-\bar{z})\right)+6(\bar{z}+1)\tan^{-1}\left(\tfrac{2z+1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)-6(\bar{z}+1)\tan^{-1}\left(\tfrac{2\bar{z}+1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)\right)}{6\sqrt{2z\bar{z}+2}}\\
\beta_{1}&=-\tfrac{\lambda^{2}\left(\sqrt{3}(\bar{z}-1)\left(-\log\left(z^{2}+z+1\right)+2\log(1-z)-\log\left(\bar{z}^{2}+\bar{z}+1\right)+2\log(1-\bar{z})\right)+6(\bar{z}+1)\tan^{-1}\left(\tfrac{2z+1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)-6(\bar{z}+1)\tan^{-1}\left(\tfrac{2\bar{z}+1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)\right)}{6\sqrt{2z\bar{z}+2}}\\
\beta_{2}&=-\tfrac{i\lambda\left(-\sqrt{3}(z+1)\left(-\log\left(z^{2}+z+1\right)+2\log(1-z)-\log\left(\bar{z}^{2}+\bar{z}+1\right)+2\log(1-\bar{z})\right)-6(z-1)\tan^{-1}\left(\tfrac{2\bar{z}+1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)+6(z-1)\tan^{-1}\left(\tfrac{2z+1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)\right)}{6\sqrt{2z\bar{z}+2}}\end{split}

and

X_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&y_{1}&y_{2}\\
x_{1}&*&*\\
x_{2}&*&*\end{pmatrix}

for

\begin{split}y_{1}&=\tfrac{\pi\left(\lambda^{3}-1\right)+\sqrt{3}\left(\log\left(z^{2}+z+1\right)-2\log(1-z)+\lambda^{3}\left(\log\left(\bar{z}^{2}+\bar{z}+1\right)-2\log(1-\bar{z})\right)\right)+6\tan^{-1}\left(\tfrac{2z+1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)-6\lambda^{3}\tan^{-1}\left(\tfrac{2\bar{z}+1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)}{6\sqrt{2}\lambda}\\
y_{2}&=-\tfrac{i\left(\pi\left(\lambda^{3}+1\right)+\sqrt{3}\left(\log\left(z^{2}+z+1\right)-2\log(1-z)+\lambda^{3}\left(2\log(1-\bar{z})-\log\left(\bar{z}^{2}+\bar{z}+1\right)\right)\right)-6\tan^{-1}\left(\tfrac{2z+1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)-6\lambda^{3}\tan^{-1}\left(\tfrac{2\bar{z}+1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)\right)}{6\sqrt{2}\lambda^{2}}\\
x_{1}&=\tfrac{\pi\left(\lambda^{3}-1\right)+\sqrt{3}\left(-\log\left(z^{2}+z+1\right)+2\log(1-z)+\lambda^{3}\left(2\log(1-\bar{z})-\log\left(\bar{z}^{2}+\bar{z}+1\right)\right)\right)+6\tan^{-1}\left(\tfrac{2z+1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)-6\lambda^{3}\tan^{-1}\left(\tfrac{2\bar{z}+1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)}{6\sqrt{2}\lambda^{2}}\\
x_{2}&=-\tfrac{i\left(\pi\left(\lambda^{3}+1\right)+\sqrt{3}\left(-\log\left(z^{2}+z+1\right)+2\log(1-z)+\lambda^{3}\left(\log\left(\bar{z}^{2}+\bar{z}+1\right)-2\log(1-\bar{z})\right)\right)-6\tan^{-1}\left(\tfrac{2z+1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)-6\lambda^{3}\tan^{-1}\left(\tfrac{2\bar{z}+1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)\right)}{6\sqrt{2}\lambda}.\end{split}

By (
7.4
), the horizontal lift
\hat{f}_{t}
of the minimal Lagrangian surface
f_{t}\colon\widetilde{S}\to\mathbb{CP}^{2}
is given by

F^{-1}_{t}(\lambda_{0}\,)\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}\colon\widetilde{S}\to\mathbb{S}^{5}

where
\lambda_{0}=\exp(\tfrac{2\pi i}{12}).

By (
7.7
),
\hat{f}_{t=0}
is constant on simply connected compact subsets
{\mathbb{K}}
, i.e.,
d\hat{f}_{t=0}=0.

Furthermore, taking the derivative with respect to
t
at
t=0
yields

(7.8)

\displaystyle\dot{\hat{f}}\colon\widetilde{S}\to\mathbb{C}^{2}\cong(1,0,0)^{\perp};\hskip 10.00002ptz\mapsto

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac{(-1)^{2/3}\left(\sqrt{3}\left(\log\left(z^{2}+z+1\right)-2\log(1-z)+i\log\left(\bar{z}^{2}+\bar{z}+1\right)-2i\log(1-\bar{z})\right)-6\tan^{-1}\left(\tfrac{2z+1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)+6i\tan^{-1}\left(\tfrac{2\bar{z}+1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)+\pi(1-i)\right)}{6\sqrt{2}}\\
\tfrac{\sqrt[3]{-1}\left(\sqrt{3}\left(\log\left(z^{2}+z+1\right)-2\log(1-z)-i\log\left(\bar{z}^{2}+\bar{z}+1\right)+2i\log(1-\bar{z})\right)+6\tan^{-1}\left(\tfrac{2z+1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)+6i\tan^{-1}\left(\tfrac{2\bar{z}+1}{\sqrt{3}}\right)+\pi(-1-i)\right)}{6\sqrt{2}}\end{pmatrix}

with differential

(7.9)

\begin{split}d\dot{\hat{f}}&=\sqrt{\tfrac{3}{2}}\begin{pmatrix}e^{-\tfrac{\pi i}{3}}z\\
-e^{\tfrac{\pi i}{3}}\end{pmatrix}\frac{dz}{z^{3}-1}+\sqrt{\tfrac{3}{2}}\begin{pmatrix}e^{\tfrac{\pi i}{6}}\\
-e^{-\tfrac{\pi i}{6}}\bar{z}\end{pmatrix}\frac{d\bar{z}}{\bar{z}^{3}-1}\end{split},

as claimed.
∎

It is well-known (
[
23
,
24
]
) that minimal Lagrangian surfaces
f
in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
admit a Weierstrass-type representation in terms of holomorphic 1-forms.
Using a different complex structure
j
on the real vector space
\mathbb{C}^{2}=\mathbb{R}^{4}=\mathbb{H}
,
the surface
f
becomes a holomorphic curve in
\mathbb{C}^{2}\cong(\mathbb{H},j).
In fact,

d\dot{\hat{f}}=(dH_{1},dH_{2})+(-id\bar{H}_{2},id\bar{H}_{1})

for

dH_{1}=\sqrt{\tfrac{3}{2}}e^{-\frac{\pi i}{3}}\frac{zdz}{z^{3}-1}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptdH_{2}=-\sqrt{\tfrac{3}{2}}e^{\frac{\pi i}{3}}\frac{dz}{z^{3}-1}.

Equivalently, we have

(7.10)

\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}&0&0&\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\\
0&\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}&\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}&0\\
0&-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}&\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}&0\\
-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}&0&0&\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\\
\end{pmatrix}d\dot{\hat{f}}=\sqrt{2}\begin{pmatrix}dH_{1}\\
dH_{2}\end{pmatrix}.

As the monodromy of
d\dot{\hat{f}}
is abelian,
\dot{\hat{f}}
is already a well-defined immersion (without nontrivial translational periods) on the universal abelian covering

M:=\widetilde{S}/\Lambda^{com}\to\mathbb{CP}^{1}\setminus\{1,\zeta,\zeta^{2}\}

where
\widetilde{S}\to\mathbb{CP}^{1}\setminus\{1,\zeta,\zeta^{2}\}
is the universal covering, and
\Lambda^{com}:=[\pi_{1},\pi_{1}]\subset\pi_{1}(\mathbb{CP}^{1}\setminus\{1,\zeta,\zeta^{2}\})

is the commutator subgroup.
As the periods of
dH_{1},dH_{2}
vanish on
\Lambda^{com}
,
\dot{\hat{f}}
descends to
M
.

Corollary 7.2
.

The surface
\dot{\hat{f}}\colon M\to\mathbb{C}^{2}
as defined in (
7.8
) with differential (
7.9
) is properly embedded.
We call it the Scherk minimal Lagrangian surface.

Proof.

This follows from (
7.10
). In fact, after applying the same Möbius transformation (
7.1
) as before, the differential of the
j
-holomorphic curve becomes

A\frac{dz}{z}+B\frac{dz}{z-1}
with linearly independent vectors

A:=(\tfrac{1+i\sqrt{3}}{2\sqrt{6}},\tfrac{-1+i\sqrt{3}}{2\sqrt{6}})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{ and }\hskip 20.00003ptB:=(\tfrac{1-i\sqrt{3}}{2\sqrt{6}},\tfrac{-1-i\sqrt{3}}{2\sqrt{6}}).

Hence on the universal abelian cover, where
\log z
and
\log(z-1)
are single-valued,

(H_{1},H_{2})=A\log z+B\log(z-1)+\text{const}

Since
A
and
B
are linearly independent, this is an affine embedding of the universal abelian cover into
\mathbb{C}^{2}
. That gives injectivity immediately, and properness follows from the additional fact that

p\mapsto\bigl(\log z(p),\log(z-1)(p)\bigr)

is a holomorphic embedding of M into
\mathbb{C}^{2}
.
∎

7.4.
The limiting structure of the Riemann surfaces, and the normalization in space

Recall that the underlying Riemann surface
\Sigma_{k}
is the Fermat curve

\{[X:Y:Z]\in\mathbb{CP}^{2}\mid X^{k}+\zeta\,Y^{k}+\zeta^{2}\,Z^{k}=0\}

where
\zeta=\exp(\tfrac{2\pi i}{3}).

The group

\Gamma_{deck}

of deck transformations of
\Sigma_{k}\to\mathbb{CP}^{1}\cong\Sigma_{k}/\Gamma_{deck}

acts by extrinsic symmetries on minimal Lagrangian immersions into
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
as well as on their special Legendrian lifts in
\mathbb{S}^{5}.

On the holomorphic curve
\Sigma_{k}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{2}
, this group
is generated by

\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\varphi_{3}:=(\varphi_{2}\varphi_{1})^{-1}
and
\sigma
defined in (
4.1
).
Their orders are
k,\,k,\,k
, and
3
, respectively.

The monodromy of
\nabla_{\lambda_{0}}
at
\lambda_{0}=\exp(\tfrac{2\pi i}{12})

on the thrice-punctured sphere is the representation
\rho
in (
4.3
) generated by

\sigma\cong M_{0}
and
\varphi_{1}\cong M_{1}.
Furthermore, the symmetries
\varphi_{j}
for
j=1,2,3
pairwise
commute, while
\varphi_{1}
and
\sigma
do not commute; for example,
\varphi_{2}=\sigma^{-1}\circ\varphi_{1}\circ\sigma.

We label the special points on

\Sigma_{k},
i.e., the fixed points of certain elements of
\langle\varphi_{1},\sigma\rangle\cong\Gamma_{deck}^{op}
as follows:

\begin{split}p^{1}_{1}&=[0,1,\exp(\tfrac{\pi i}{3k})],\quad\quad q_{1,1}=[1,1,1]\\
p^{1}_{\ell}&=\varphi_{3}^{\ell-1}\,p^{1}_{1},\quad p^{2}_{\ell}=\sigma^{-1}\,p^{1}_{\ell},\quad p^{3}_{\ell}=\sigma\,p^{1}_{\ell},\quad q_{j,\ell}=\varphi_{2}^{\ell-1}\varphi_{1}^{j-1}\,q_{1,1}\end{split}

where
\ell,j\in\{1,\dots,k\}.
Correspondingly, we denote the symmetries of
\mathbb{S}^{5}
as follows:

(7.11)

\tilde{\varphi}_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&\exp(\tfrac{2\pi i}{k})&0\\
0&0&\exp(-\tfrac{2\pi i}{k})\end{pmatrix},\;\tilde{\varphi}_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}\exp(\tfrac{2\pi i}{k})&0&0\\
0&\exp(-\tfrac{2\pi i}{k})&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix},\;\tilde{\varphi}_{3}=(\tilde{\varphi}_{2}\tilde{\varphi}_{1})^{-1},\;\tilde{\sigma}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0\\
0&0&1\\
1&0&0\end{pmatrix}.

Note that
\tilde{\varphi}_{1}=M_{1}^{-1}
and
\tilde{\sigma}=M_{0}^{-1}
and recall (e.g. (
2.24
)) that the deck transformations
\gamma\in\Gamma_{deck}

act via
\rho(\gamma)^{-1}
on the special Legendrian immersion.

Remark 7.3
.

We will see that the surfaces
\hat{f}_{k}
converge to infinitely many spherical caps, which are
generated
by the infinitesimal symmetries
\tilde{\varphi}_{j}
for
k\to\infty
. Moreover, the surfaces
\hat{f}_{k}

are normalized through the condition that
the distinguished points
p^{j}_{\ell}
and
q_{j,\ell}
, each fixed by an appropriate nontrivial element of
\Gamma_{deck}
,
are mapped to fixed points of
the corresponding
\SU_{3}
transformations acting on
\mathbb{S}^{5}.
Because the
\SU_{3}
-representation
\rho

of
\Gamma_{deck}
is irreducible,
and the special Legendrian lift is unique up to global multiplication by a cube root of unity, the images
of
p^{j}_{\ell}
and
q_{j,\ell}
in
\mathbb{S}^{5}
are uniquely determined up to overall multiplication by
\zeta
or
\zeta^{2},

which can be ignored as this action is discrete.

7.4.1.
The coordinate
w

We consider
the order
k
automorphism

\varphi:=\varphi_{1}\circ\varphi_{2}^{-1}.

If
k
is odd, the automorphism
\varphi
has no fixed points. This yields an unramified cover

\Sigma_{k}\to\underline{\Sigma}_{k}
where
\underline{\Sigma}_{k}
is the hyperelliptic curve given by

v^{2}=w^{k}-1.

The Weierstrass points of
\underline{\Sigma}_{k}
are the
k
th roots of unity and
\infty.
Removing the preimages of the unit circle yields three disjoint discs

\mathbb{D}_{\pm},\mathbb{D}_{\infty}\subset\underline{\Sigma}_{k}.

Each of these three discs has exactly
k
connected components as preimages in
\Sigma_{k}
. Although
\underline{\Sigma}_{k}
does not admit a well-defined
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
action
permuting
\mathbb{D}_{\pm},\mathbb{D}_{\infty},

the corresponding preimages in
\Sigma_{k}
are mapped by
\hat{f}_{k}
to extrinsically isometric regions in
\mathbb{S}^{5}
.
Note that

w\colon\mathbb{D}_{\pm}\to\{w\in\mathbb{C}\mid|w|<1\}

is a natural coordinate on
\mathbb{D}_{\pm}.
Indexing the discs by
\{1,2,3\}
(up to permutation, i.e. after applying the symmetry
\sigma
), we also obtain a natural coordinate, still denoted by
w
, on
\mathbb{D}_{\infty}
, whose image is again the unit disc.
The preimages of the three discs
\mathbb{D}_{\pm}
,
\mathbb{D}_{\infty}
in
\Sigma_{k}
consist of
3k
many connected components.
They are labeled by
\mathbb{D}^{j}_{\ell}
according to their centers,

p_{\ell}^{j}\in\mathbb{D}^{j}_{\ell}\subset\Sigma_{k},

where
j\in\{1,2,3\}
and
\ell\in\{1,2,...,k\}
.
Note that for every
k
, the union of these
3k
discs is open and dense in
\Sigma_{k}
.
For all of them, we use
w
as a natural coordinate which maps them to the unit disc.
Note that on
\mathbb{D}^{j}_{\ell}
,
j=1,2,3
, we have

(7.12)

z=\zeta^{j-1}-w^{k}

where
z
is the standard affine coordinate on
\mathbb{CP}^{1}=\Sigma_{k}/(\mathbb{Z}_{k}\times\mathbb{Z}_{k})
, normalized by the condition that the
three branch values
of the degree
k^{2}
map
\Sigma_{k}\to\mathbb{CP}^{1}

are at
1,\zeta,\zeta^{2}.
Furthermore

w_{\mid\mathbb{D}^{j}_{\ell}}\circ\varphi_{j}=\exp(\tfrac{2\pi i}{k})w_{\mid\mathbb{D}^{j}_{\ell}},

while
w
is invariant under some appropriate
\varphi_{\tilde{j}},
e.g., on
\mathbb{D}^{1}_{\ell}
the coordinate
w
is invariant under
\varphi_{3}
(as
\varphi_{3}
maps

\mathbb{D}^{1}_{\ell}
to
\mathbb{D}^{1}_{\ell+1}
).

Similarly, for even
k
, we also obtain
3k
disjoint discs
\mathbb{D}^{j}_{\ell}\subset\Sigma_{k}
with centers
p^{j}_{\ell}
. On each
\mathbb{D}^{j}_{\ell}
there exists
a coordinate
w
which maps biholomorphically to the unit disc and satisfies (
7.12
). Furthermore, the union of the
3k
discs
is open and dense in
\Sigma_{k}.

The special Legendrian caps

Note that the unique (up to multiplication by
\zeta
or
\zeta^{2}
) totally geodesic special Legendrian closed cap
\hat{\mathbb{D}}_{1}^{1}

in
\mathbb{S}^{5}
of radius
\arctan(\sqrt{2})

with center on
\mathbb{S}^{1}\oplus 0\oplus 0\subset\mathbb{S}^{5}\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}
and with a boundary point that is a fixed point of
\tilde{\sigma}
(defined in (
7.11
)) is
contained in and therefore determined by the plane

E=\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{R}}(\,\begin{pmatrix}i\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0\\
i\\
i\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\\
-1\end{pmatrix}\,)\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}.

Explicitly

\hat{\mathbb{D}}_{1}^{1}=\{\cos(u)\begin{pmatrix}i\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}+\sin(u)(\tfrac{\cos(v)}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
i\\
i\end{pmatrix}+\tfrac{\sin(v)}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\\
-1\end{pmatrix})\,\mid\,u\in[0,\arctan(\sqrt{2})],\,v\in[0,2\pi]\,\}.

As a consequence of the above discussion, we define

P^{1}_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}i\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptQ_{1,1}=\tfrac{i}{\sqrt{3}}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
1\\
1\end{pmatrix}

as well as

(7.13)

P^{1}_{\ell}=\tilde{\varphi}_{3}^{\ell-1}\,P^{1}_{1},\hskip 10.00002ptP^{2}_{\ell}=\tilde{\sigma}^{-1}\,P^{1}_{\ell},\hskip 10.00002ptP^{3}_{\ell}=\tilde{\sigma}^{-2}\,P^{1}_{\ell},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptQ_{j,\ell}=\tilde{\varphi}_{2}^{\ell-1}\tilde{\varphi}_{1}^{j-1}\,Q_{1,1}.

Finally, we define the following
3k
spherical caps with centers
P^{1}_{\ell},P^{2}_{\ell},P^{3}_{\ell}
,
\ell=1,\dots,k
,

\hat{\mathbb{D}}^{1}_{\ell}=\tilde{\varphi}_{3}^{\ell-1}\,\hat{\mathbb{D}}_{1}^{1},\hskip 10.00002pt\hat{\mathbb{D}}^{2}_{\ell}=\tilde{\sigma}^{-1}\hat{\mathbb{D}}^{1}_{\ell},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\hat{\mathbb{D}}^{3}_{\ell}=\tilde{\sigma}\,\hat{\mathbb{D}}^{1}_{\ell}.

As discussed in Remark
7.3
, the very form of the symmetries already fixes the surfaces
\hat{f}_{k}
in
\mathbb{S}^{5}
up to multiplication by
\zeta
or
\zeta^{2}
.
It is not clear whether all the points in (
7.13
) are
contained in the image of the normalized surface
\hat{f}_{k}
for every
finite
k\geq k_{0}
. However, their projections to
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
lie on the
corresponding minimal Lagrangian surfaces
f_{k}
. The points themselves
appear in the limiting surface
\hat{f}_{0}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\hat{f}_{k}
, as
we prove below.

In fact, it follows from our analysis below that the limit of
\hat{f}_{k}
for
k\to\infty
is given by

•

the closed discs
\hat{\mathbb{D}}^{1}_{\ell}
,
\hat{\mathbb{D}}^{2}_{\ell}
, and
\hat{\mathbb{D}}^{3}_{\ell}
for
\ell=1,2,\dots
with centers
equidistributed on the three circles
\mathbb{S}^{1}\oplus 0\oplus 0
,
0\oplus\mathbb{S}^{1}\oplus 0
and
0\oplus 0\oplus\mathbb{S}^{1};

•

the boundaries of the discs which all lie on the Clifford torus generated from
Q_{1,1}
by applying the symmetries
(\tilde{\varphi}_{1})^{j}\circ(\tilde{\varphi}_{2})^{\ell}
for
j,\ell=1,2,\dots\;\,
.

Remark 7.4
.

For the model caps defined above, and for fixed finite
k
,

\hat{\mathbb{D}}^{j}_{\ell}\cap\hat{\mathbb{D}}^{j}_{\tilde{\ell}}=\emptyset\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for all}\hskip 10.00002ptj=1,2,3,\ \ell\neq\tilde{\ell},

while for
j_{1}\neq j_{2}
and all
\ell,\tilde{\ell}
the closures of the discs

\hat{\mathbb{D}}^{j_{1}}_{\ell}
and
\hat{\mathbb{D}}^{j_{2}}_{\tilde{\ell}}
intersect in precisely one point
a\in\mathbb{S}^{5}
, which is contained in the intersection of their boundaries. Furthermore, there is a unique
\hat{\ell}\in\{1,\dots,k\}

such that for
\{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}\}=\{1,2,3\}

\hat{\mathbb{D}}^{j_{3}}_{\hat{\ell}}\cap\hat{\mathbb{D}}^{j_{1}}_{\ell}\cap\hat{\mathbb{D}}^{j_{2}}_{\tilde{\ell}}=\{a\}.

7.5.
The spherical part

In this subsection we determine the limiting behaviour of the special Legendrian surfaces
\hat{f}_{k}
restricted to the discs
as
k\to\infty
.
We first derive the asymptotic expansion of the normalized parallel frame, which then yields the limit of
\hat{f}_{k}
on the discs.
We finally establish uniform convergence of the surfaces up to the boundary of the discs.

We continue to work with the interpolating family

\hat{f}_{t}=F_{t}(\lambda_{0})^{-1}e_{1}\colon\widetilde{S}\to\mathbb{S}^{5}

from (
7.4
). For
t=\frac{1}{k}
, this agrees, up to an ambient
\SU_{3}
-transformation, with
\hat{f}_{k}\circ\widetilde{\pi}_{k}
.
To analyze the component lying over
z=1
, we use its natural coordinate
w

on the discs
{\mathbb{D}}^{1}_{\ell}\subset\Sigma_{k}
introduced in (
7.12
). For
t=\frac{1}{k}
this is characterized by

z=1-w^{k}.

For the continuous
t
-family it is convenient to write instead

z=1-e^{u},\hskip 20.00003ptw=e^{tu}.

Consider the limit

(7.14)

\tilde{\Omega}(w):=\lim_{t\to 0}\Omega_{t}(1-w^{\tfrac{1}{t}}).

Lemma 7.5
.

The limit in (
7.14
)
converges locally uniformly on
\{w\in\mathbb{C}\mid 0<|w|<1\}
to

\tilde{\Omega}(w)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&-\frac{i}{\lambda}\\
0&0&1\\
\end{pmatrix}\exp(-A_{1}\log w)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for}\hskip 10.00002ptA_{1}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\frac{\lambda^{3}-1}{\sqrt{6}\lambda}&-\frac{i\left(\lambda^{3}+1\right)}{\sqrt{6}\lambda^{2}}\\
\frac{\lambda^{3}-1}{\sqrt{6}\lambda^{2}}&0&\frac{i}{\sqrt{3}\lambda}\\
\frac{i\left(\lambda^{3}+1\right)}{\sqrt{6}\lambda}&-\frac{i\lambda}{\sqrt{3}}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}.

Furthermore, (
7.14
)
converges uniformly for
w\in[\tfrac{1}{2},1]\subset\mathbb{R}.

Remark 7.6
.

The singularity of
\tilde{\Omega}
at
w=0
will be removed through the loop group factorization.

Proof.

By Theorem
13
, the functions
a,b,c
are, up to second order in
t
, given by the central values, i.e.,

a(t)=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{6}}+O(t^{2}),\;b(t)=-\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{6}}+O(t^{2}),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptc(t)=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}+O(t^{2}).

Define

\phi:=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&-\frac{iz}{\lambda}\\
0&0&1\\
\end{pmatrix}

and
\mu_{t}
via
d+\mu_{t}=(d+\omega_{t}).\phi.

Thus, we have
on the thrice-punctured sphere with coordinate
z

(7.15)

\begin{split}\mu_{t}&=\omega_{t}.\phi=t\begin{pmatrix}0&-\frac{\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\left(\lambda^{3}-1\right)}{\lambda\left(z^{3}-1\right)}&-\frac{i\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\left(\lambda^{3}+1\right)z}{\lambda^{2}\left(z^{3}-1\right)}\\
\frac{\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\left(\lambda^{3}-1\right)z}{\lambda^{2}\left(z^{3}-1\right)}&0&\frac{i\sqrt{3}}{\lambda\left(z^{3}-1\right)}\\
\frac{i\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\left(\lambda^{3}+1\right)}{\lambda\left(z^{3}-1\right)}&-\frac{i\sqrt{3}\lambda z}{z^{3}-1}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}dz+O(t^{3}).\end{split}

Set

\Psi_{t}:=\phi^{-1}\Omega_{t}.

Since
\phi(z=0)=\Id,
it then satisfies the ODE

d\Psi_{t}+\mu_{t}\Psi_{t}=0\hskip 20.00003pt\text{
with initial value}\hskip 20.00003pt\Psi_{t}(z=0)=R(t)=\Id+O(t^{2}).

Using
z=1-w^{\tfrac{1}{t}}
,
we then obtain on every compact subset of the unit disc
in the
w
-coordinate (including the pole at
w=0
)
uniform convergence

(7.16)

\mu_{t}=A_{1}\frac{dw}{w}+O(t^{2}).

Thus, consider

\phi_{1}(z):=\exp(-tA_{1}\log(1-z))=\exp(-A_{1}\log w).

For each
\lambda
, the matrix
A_{1}
is conjugate to
\diag(1,0,-1)
; in particular,

\exp(-2\pi iA_{1})=\Id
, so the expression
\exp(-A_{1}\log w)
is single-valued on
0<|w|<1
.

The map
\phi_{1}(z)
is uniformly bounded in
\Lambda\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{C})
on the real interval

z\in[0,1-\tfrac{1}{2^{1/t}}]

and satisfies

(7.17)

\phi_{1}(z=0)=\Id=\Psi_{t}(z=0).

Write

\Psi_{t}=\phi_{1}Y_{t}.

As in
[
11
, Section 3.5, Lemma 24]
we use the variation of constants method and Grönwall’s inequality to prove

Y_{t}(z=1-\tfrac{1}{2^{1/t}})\to_{t\to 0}\Id.

In fact, we obtain from (
7.15
)

(7.18)

dY_{t}=Q_{t}Y_{t}

where
Q_{t}
is uniformly bounded (note that
Q_{0}
can be computed explicitly in terms of the residues at
z=\xi
and
z=\xi^{2}
of the potential
\mu_{t}
, for details see Section
7.6
below).
Integrating (
7.18
), using (
7.17
) and taking norms, we obtain for
z\in[0,1-\tfrac{1}{2^{1/t}}]

\parallel Y_{t}(z)-\Id\parallel\leq C|t|\int_{0}^{z}\parallel Y_{t}(z)\parallel

for some constant
C>0.

Grönwall’s inequality yields

\parallel Y_{t}(z=1-\tfrac{1}{2^{1/t}})-\Id\parallel\leq C|t|\exp(C|t|)\to_{t\to 0}0.

Hence,

\lim_{t\to 0}\Psi_{t}(z=1-\tfrac{1}{2^{1/t}})=\lim_{t\to 0}\phi_{1}(z=1-\tfrac{1}{2^{1/t}})=\exp(A_{1}\log 2).

Furthermore, we obtain from the locally uniform equation (
7.16
) with respect to the coordinate
w
with
z=1-w^{1/t}
and on compact subsets of
|w|<1
that

\lim_{t\to 0}\Psi_{t}=\exp(-A_{1}\log w)

or equivalently

\tilde{\Omega}(w)=\phi(z=1)\exp(-A_{1}\log w)

as claimed.

That (
7.14
)
converges uniformly for
w\in[\tfrac{1}{2},1]\subset\mathbb{R}

then clearly follows from the first part of the proof, i.e., from our uniform estimate along the
z
-segment
[0,1-2^{-\tfrac{1}{t}}],

together with the locally uniform estimate for
0<|w|<1.

∎

With this preparation, we can compute the limit surface:

Theorem 15
.

For each
k
, let
\mathbb{D}_{1}^{1}\subset\Sigma_{k}
be the distinguished disc containing
p_{1}^{1}
, and let

w:\mathbb{D}_{1}^{1}\to\mathbb{D}:=\{\,|w|<1\,\}

be its natural coordinate.
Then, with the normalization fixed in Section
7.4
,

\hat{f}_{k}\circ w^{-1}\colon\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{S}^{5}

converge locally uniformly, as
k\to\infty
, to

(7.19)

\hat{f}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{2i\left(\sqrt{3}+2\right)}{x^{2}+y^{2}+\sqrt{3}+2}-i\\
\frac{\left(\sqrt{3}+1\right)(ix+y)}{x^{2}+y^{2}+\sqrt{3}+2}\\
\frac{\left(\sqrt{3}+1\right)(ix-y)}{x^{2}+y^{2}+\sqrt{3}+2}\end{pmatrix}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{with}\hskip 10.00002ptw=x+iy.

Remark 7.7
.

Note that the closure of
\hat{f}({\mathbb{D}})
is exactly
\hat{\mathbb{D}}_{1}^{1},

and that the area of this spherical cap is exactly

2\pi(1-\sqrt{\tfrac{1}{3}}),

which perfectly fits with Theorem
10
.

Proof.

We factor
\tilde{\Omega}(w)
into positive and unitary parts. Write

w=r\exp(i\varphi)

to obtain

\begin{split}&\tilde{\Omega}(w)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&-\frac{i}{\lambda}\\
0&0&1\\
\end{pmatrix}\exp(-A_{1}\log r)\exp(-iA_{1}\varphi)\end{split}

where the last factor

F_{\varphi}:=\exp(-iA_{1}\varphi)

maps into

{\bf SU}^{\hat{\tau}}(\mathbb{S}^{1})

because
iA_{1}\in{\bf su}_{3}^{\hat{\tau}}(\mathbb{S}^{1}).

Hence, it is enough to factor

\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&-\frac{i}{\lambda}\\
0&0&1\\
\end{pmatrix}\exp(-A_{1}\log r)=BF_{0}

into its (twisted) positive part
B
and its unitary part
F_{0}
.
We only state the result of the computation:

(7.20)

\begin{split}&B=\begin{pmatrix}1&-\frac{\left(\sqrt{3}+1\right)\lambda^{2}\left(r^{2}-1\right)}{\sqrt{2}\left(r^{2}+\sqrt{3}+2\right)}&-\frac{i\sqrt{2}\left(\sqrt{3}+1\right)\lambda\left(r^{2}-1\right)}{r^{2}+\sqrt{3}+2}\\
\frac{\lambda\left(r^{2}-1\right)}{\sqrt{3}r}&\frac{-\frac{\sqrt{3}\left(4\sqrt{3}+7\right)\lambda^{3}\left(r^{2}-1\right)^{2}}{r^{2}+\sqrt{3}+2}+\left(2\sqrt{3}+3\right)r^{2}+7\sqrt{3}+12}{6\left(\sqrt{3}+2\right)^{3/2}r}&-\frac{i\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}}\lambda^{2}\left(r^{2}-1\right)^{2}}{r\left(r^{2}+\sqrt{3}+2\right)}\\
0&-\frac{i\left(7\sqrt{3}+12\right)\lambda r}{\left(\sqrt{3}+2\right)^{3/2}\left(r^{2}+\sqrt{3}+2\right)}&\frac{2\sqrt{3\left(\sqrt{3}+2\right)}r}{r^{2}+\sqrt{3}+2}\\
\end{pmatrix}\\
&\\
&F_{0}=\\
&\begin{pmatrix}\frac{r\left(\sqrt{3}-r+2\sqrt{3}+6\right)+2\sqrt{3}+3}{3\left(r^{2}+\sqrt{3}+2\right)}&-\frac{\left(\lambda^{3}+1\right)(r-1)\left(\left(\sqrt{3}+3\right)r+5\sqrt{3}+9\right)}{6\sqrt{\sqrt{3}+2}\lambda\left(r^{2}+\sqrt{3}+2\right)}&-\frac{i\left(\lambda^{3}-1\right)(r-1)\left(\left(\sqrt{3}+3\right)r+5\sqrt{3}+9\right)}{6\sqrt{\sqrt{3}+2}\lambda^{2}\left(r^{2}+\sqrt{3}+2\right)}\\
\frac{\lambda(r-1)\left(\sqrt{3}r+2\sqrt{3}+3\right)}{3\left(r^{2}+\sqrt{3}+2\right)}&\frac{\sqrt{3}r^{2}-\left(2\sqrt{3}+3\right)\lambda^{3}(r-1)^{2}+6r+4\sqrt{3}r+7\sqrt{3}+12}{6\sqrt{\sqrt{3}+2}\left(r^{2}+\sqrt{3}+2\right)}&-\frac{i\left(\sqrt{3}r^{2}+\left(2\sqrt{3}+3\right)\lambda^{3}(r-1)^{2}+6r+4\sqrt{3}r+7\sqrt{3}+12\right)}{6\sqrt{\sqrt{3}+2}\lambda\left(r^{2}+\sqrt{3}+2\right)}\\
-\frac{i(r-1)\left(r+\sqrt{3}+2\right)}{\sqrt{3}\lambda\left(r^{2}+\sqrt{3}+2\right)}&\frac{\frac{i\left(2\sqrt{3}+3\right)(r-1)^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}-i\lambda\left(r\left(\sqrt{3}r+4\sqrt{3}+6\right)+7\sqrt{3}+12\right)}{6\sqrt{\sqrt{3}+2}\left(r^{2}+\sqrt{3}+2\right)}&\frac{\frac{\left(2\sqrt{3}+3\right)(r-1)^{2}}{\lambda^{3}}+\sqrt{3}r\left(r+2\sqrt{3}+4\right)+7\sqrt{3}+12}{6\sqrt{\sqrt{3}+2}\left(r^{2}+\sqrt{3}+2\right)}\\
\end{pmatrix}.\end{split}

Note that
\exp(iA_{1}\varphi)(\lambda_{0})
, where
\lambda_{0}=\exp(\tfrac{2\pi i}{12}),
is an
\SU_{3}
rotation with eigenvalues
1,\exp(i\varphi),\exp(-i\varphi)
and corresponding eigenvectors

\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
-(-1)^{11/12}\\
-(-1)^{1/12}\end{pmatrix},\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\begin{pmatrix}-(-1)^{7/12}\\
\sqrt[6]{-1}\\
1\end{pmatrix},\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
-(-1)^{\tfrac{1}{4}}\\
-(-1)^{\tfrac{3}{4}}\end{pmatrix}.

Up to
\SU_{3}
, the special Legendrian surface in
\mathbb{S}^{5}\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}
is therefore given by

\tilde{f}=(F_{0}\cdot F_{\varphi})^{-1}(\lambda_{0})\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}.

To align the surface with our previous normalization (coming from a specific form of its symmetries, see Section
7.4
), the point
z=0
needs to be mapped to

\tfrac{i}{\sqrt{3}}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
1\\
1\end{pmatrix}
and
the limit
w\to 0,
equivalently
z\to 1,
is mapped to

\begin{pmatrix}i\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}
. To achieve this, we apply the
(constant)
\SU_{3}
transformation

T=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{i}{\sqrt{3}}&\frac{\left(\frac{1}{6}+\frac{i}{6}\right)\left(\sqrt{3}+3i\right)}{\sqrt{2}}&-\frac{(-1)^{5/12}}{\sqrt{3}}\\
\frac{i}{\sqrt{3}}&-\frac{1+i}{\sqrt{6}}&-\frac{1-i}{\sqrt{6}}\\
\frac{i}{\sqrt{3}}&\frac{\left(\frac{1}{6}+\frac{i}{6}\right)\left(\sqrt{3}-3i\right)}{\sqrt{2}}&\frac{(3+3i)+(1-i)\sqrt{3}}{6\sqrt{2}}\\
\end{pmatrix},

to obtain

\hat{f}=(T(F_{\varphi})^{-1}(\lambda_{0})T^{-1})\cdot(TF_{0}^{-1}(\lambda_{0}))\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}.

Note that

T(F_{\varphi})^{-1}(\lambda_{0})T^{-1}=\mathrm{diag}(1,e^{-i\varphi},e^{i\varphi})

and from (
7.20
) we then obtain

(TF_{0}^{-1}(\lambda_{0}))\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}=i\begin{pmatrix}\frac{2\left(\sqrt{3}+2\right)}{r^{2}+\sqrt{3}+2}-1\\
\frac{\left(\sqrt{3}+1\right)r}{r^{2}+\sqrt{3}+2}\\
\frac{\left(\sqrt{3}+1\right)r}{r^{2}+\sqrt{3}+2}\end{pmatrix}.

Therefore, the parametrized limit surface on the disc
\{w\in\mathbb{C}\mid 0<|w|<1\}\subset\Sigma_{k}
is given in conformal coordinates
w=x+iy

by (
7.19
).

It remains to show that this locally uniform limit on
\{w\in\mathbb{C}\mid 0<|w|<1\}\subset\Sigma_{k}
extends uniformly to
w=0.

By the construction in Lemma
5.1
and by adapting the gauges (
5.11
),
the pole of (
7.16
) at
w=0
is apparent for all small
t
:
there exists a smooth family of meromorphic gauge transformations
g_{t}\colon\{w\in\mathbb{C}\mid 0<|w|<\tfrac{1}{2}\}\to{\bf SL}^{\hat{\tau}}(\mathbb{D})

(with pole at
w=0
)
such that

(d+\mu_{t}).g_{t}=d+\tilde{A}(w)dw+t^{2}R_{t}(w)dw

for holomorphic
\tilde{A}(w)
and
R_{t}(w)
with
uniform bound
|R_{t}(w)|<\tilde{C}
for all
|w|<\tfrac{1}{2}
and small
t.

Applying Grönwall’s inequality and the Iwasawa decomposition proves the result.
∎

We improve the previous result to include the boundary of the disc.

Proposition 7.8
.

With the same notations as in Theorem
15
,
the surfaces
\hat{f}_{k}\circ w^{-1}\colon{\mathbb{D}}\to\mathbb{S}^{5}
converge uniformly to
\hat{f}
in Theorem
15
.

Proof.

To extend convergence to the boundary, we control the behaviour for complex
w
near
|w|=1.

Let
y_{k}\in{\mathbb{D}}\subset\Sigma_{k}
, be a sequence of points with
y:=\lim_{k}y_{k}\in\partial{\mathbb{D}}

such that

\lim_{k}\hat{f}_{k}(y_{k})\neq\hat{f}(y).

Recall that each surface
\hat{f}_{k}
has a rotational
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
-fold symmetry around the origin, where
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
acts on the disc
{\mathbb{D}}
in the obvious manner by a
k
-fold rotation
w\mapsto\exp(\tfrac{2\pi i}{k})w
.
Hence, without loss of generality (i.e., after applying the symmetries if necessary)

\arg(y_{k})\in[0,\tfrac{2\pi}{k})

which gives
y=\lim_{k}y_{k}=1
as the limit.

Using
t=1/k
, we obtain
from Lemma
7.5
, that
\tilde{\Omega}
is the uniform limit of

\Omega_{1/k}
along the real line
w\in(0,1]
for
k\to\infty
.
In particular,
\lim_{k}\hat{f}_{k}(y_{k})=\hat{f}(y)
is true if
y_{k}\in(0,1]
for all
k
and
\lim y_{k}=1
(as a consequence of
Theorem
15
). We introduce a new coordinate
u
to control the case when
y_{k}
is not real.
For small
1/k=t>0
, and
0<|w|<1
write

(7.21)

w=\exp(tu),

where

u\in\mathbb{R}^{<0}\times i[0,2\pi t^{-1})
.
In particular
z=1-e^{u}.

Because
\arg(y_{k})\in[0,\tfrac{2\pi}{k})
the points
y_{k}
correspond (via the coordinate transformation (
7.21
)) to points

u_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{<0}\times i[0,2\pi).

On this domain, the DPW potential
\mu_{t}
has a uniform expansion as

\mu_{t}=t\begin{pmatrix}0&-\frac{\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}(\lambda^{3}-1)}{\lambda(e^{u}(e^{u}-3)+3)}&\frac{i\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}(\lambda^{3}+1)(e^{u}-1)}{\lambda^{2}(e^{u}(e^{u}-3)+3)}\\
-\frac{\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}(\lambda^{3}-1)(e^{u}-1)}{\lambda^{2}(e^{u}(e^{u}-3)+3)}&0&\frac{i\sqrt{3}}{\lambda(e^{u}(e^{u}-3)+3)}\\
\frac{i\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}(\lambda^{3}+1)}{\lambda(e^{u}(e^{u}-3)+3)}&\frac{i\sqrt{3}\lambda(e^{u}-1)}{e^{u}(e^{u}-3)+3}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}du+O(t^{3}).

In particular, there exists a constant
C
such that (with respect to the loop-algebra norm)

|\mu_{t}\tfrac{1}{du}|\leq Ct\hskip 10.00002pt\forall u\in\mathbb{R}^{<0}\times i[0,2\pi).

Set

u_{k}=x_{k}+i\varphi_{k}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathrm{where}\hskip 10.00002ptx_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{<0},\,\varphi_{k}\in[0,2\pi).

Hence, we can apply Grönwall’s inequality once more to deduce that

|\ \Omega_{t}(x_{k}+i\varphi_{k})-\Omega_{t}(x_{k})|<\tilde{C}\,t.

Combining Theorem
15
with the real-analyticity of the Iwasawa factorization
and the fact that
\Omega_{t}(x_{k}+i\varphi_{k})
stays bounded if the corresponding sequence in
\mathbb{D}
satisfies
y_{k}\to 1

completes the proof.
∎

In summary, the local limit on each disc
{\mathbb{D}}^{j}_{\ell}
is a totally geodesic special Legendrian cap, and the collection of these caps assembles – according to the group symmetries – into the global limiting configuration described in Remark
7.4
.

7.6.
First order expansion on the disc
{\mathbb{D}}

To interpolate between the two asymptotic regimes – spherical caps on the discs
\mathbb{D}
at zeroth order and Scherk-type surfaces on simply connected domains of the thrice-punctured sphere at first order – we must analyze the first-order variation of the limiting spherical caps. This analysis is an essential ingredient in establishing embeddedness.

We first determine the behaviour of

\Omega_{t}
up to first order
t
in the disc
{\mathbb{D}}
.
As before, we use

\Psi_{t}=\phi^{-1}\Omega_{t}

which solves

d\Psi_{t}+\mu_{t}\Psi_{t}=0

with initial value
\Psi_{t}(z=0)=\Id+O(t^{2}).

Set

\nu:=tA_{1}\frac{dz}{z-1}

with primitive

\int_{0}^{z}\nu=tA_{1}\log(1-z).

Define

\phi_{1}=\exp(-\int_{0}^{z}\nu)

which then satisfies

d\phi_{1}+\nu\phi_{1}=0.

Set

\alpha_{t}=\mu_{t}-\nu.

Then a direct computation yields

(7.22)

\begin{split}(d+\mu_{t}).\phi_{1}&=d+\phi_{1}^{-1}(\mu_{t})\phi_{1}+\phi_{1}^{-1}d\phi_{1}\\
&=d+{\phi_{1}}^{-1}\alpha_{t}\phi_{1}.\end{split}

Lemma 7.9
.

Let
u
be the coordinate as above with

z=1-e^{u}.

Then

{\phi_{1}}^{-1}\alpha_{t}\phi_{1}=t\beta_{1}+t^{2}\beta_{2}+t^{3}\hat{\beta}(t)

where

\begin{split}\beta_{1}&=\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{\left(\lambda^{3}-1\right)e^{u}\left(e^{u}-3\right)}{\sqrt{6}\lambda\left(e^{u}\left(e^{u}-3\right)+3\right)}&\frac{i\left(\lambda^{3}+1\right)e^{2u}}{\sqrt{6}\lambda^{2}\left(e^{u}\left(e^{u}-3\right)+3\right)}\\
-\frac{\left(\lambda^{3}-1\right)e^{2u}}{\sqrt{6}\lambda^{2}\left(e^{u}\left(e^{u}-3\right)+3\right)}&0&-\frac{ie^{u}\left(e^{u}-3\right)}{\sqrt{3}\lambda\left(e^{u}\left(e^{u}-3\right)+3\right)}\\
-\frac{i\left(\lambda^{3}+1\right)e^{u}\left(e^{u}-3\right)}{\sqrt{6}\lambda\left(e^{u}\left(e^{u}-3\right)+3\right)}&\frac{i\lambda e^{u}\left(e^{u}-3\right)}{\sqrt{3}\left(e^{u}\left(e^{u}-3\right)+3\right)}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}du\,,\\
\beta_{2}&=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\left(\lambda\,^{6}+1\right)u}{\lambda^{3}(-2\sinh(u)+4\cosh(u)-3)}&0&0\\
0&-\frac{\left(\lambda\,^{6}+1\right)u}{2\lambda^{3}(-2\sinh(u)+4\cosh(u)-3)}&0\\
0&0&-\frac{\left(\lambda\,^{6}+1\right)u}{2\lambda^{3}(-2\sinh(u)+4\cosh(u)-3)}\\
\end{pmatrix}du\,.\end{split}

Moreover, there exists a constant
C_{3}
with

||\hat{\beta}(t,u)(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial u})\,||<C_{3}

for all small
t>0
and for all

(7.23)

u\in\mathbb{C}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{with}\hskip 10.00002pt-\tfrac{1}{t}\log(2)<\operatorname{Re}(u)<0.

Proof.

As in the proof of Lemma
7.5
,
\phi_{1}
is bounded for all
t>0
and all
u
satisfying (
7.23
).
We expand (where
\hat{\alpha}
extends real-analytically to
t=0
)

\alpha_{t}=t\alpha_{1}+t^{3}\hat{\alpha}(t).

By construction
\alpha_{1}
and
\hat{\alpha}
are uniformly bounded for all (small)
t>0
and all
u
with
\operatorname{Re}(u)<0.

The very form of
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
then follows by direct computation.

The norm of
\hat{\beta}(t)
can be estimated because the norm of
\phi_{1}(u)=\phi_{1}(t,u)
is uniformly bounded for all
t>0
and for all

u\in\mathbb{C}
with
-\tfrac{1}{t}\log(2)<\operatorname{Re}(u)<0,
and
because
\alpha_{1}
and
\hat{\alpha}(t)

are uniformly bounded by construction.
∎

Proposition 7.10
.

There exists a constant
C_{4}>0

and a smooth map

\hat{U}\colon(0,\epsilon)\times\{u\in\mathbb{C}\mid\operatorname{Re}(u)<0\}\to{\bf sl}_{3}^{\hat{\tau}}(\mathbb{S}^{1})

such that
for all small
t>0
and all

u\in\mathbb{C}
with
-\tfrac{1}{t}\log(2)<\operatorname{Re}(u)<0
the following holds

(7.24)

\Omega_{t}(u)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&-\frac{i}{\lambda}\\
0&0&1\\
\end{pmatrix}\exp(-tA_{1}u)\,\left(\Id-t\,U+t^{2}\,\hat{U}(t,u)\,\right)

where

\begin{split}&U=\\
&\hskip-28.45274pt{\tiny\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{\left(\lambda^{3}-1\right)\left(\log\left(-3e^{u}+e^{2u}+3\right)+2\sqrt{3}\tan^{-1}\left(\frac{3-2e^{u}}{\sqrt{3}}\right)\right)}{2\sqrt{6}\lambda}&\frac{i\left(\lambda^{3}+1\right)\left(\log\left(-3e^{u}+e^{2u}+3\right)-2\sqrt{3}\tan^{-1}\left(\frac{3-2e^{u}}{\sqrt{3}}\right)\right)}{2\sqrt{6}\lambda^{2}}\\
-\frac{\left(\lambda^{3}-1\right)\left(\log\left(-3e^{u}+e^{2u}+3\right)-2\sqrt{3}\tan^{-1}\left(\frac{3-2e^{u}}{\sqrt{3}}\right)\right)}{2\sqrt{6}\lambda^{2}}&0&-\frac{i\left(\log\left(-3e^{u}+e^{2u}+3\right)+2\sqrt{3}\tan^{-1}\left(\frac{3-2e^{u}}{\sqrt{3}}\right)\right)}{2\sqrt{3}\lambda}\\
-\frac{i\left(\lambda^{3}+1\right)\left(\log\left(-3e^{u}+e^{2u}+3\right)+2\sqrt{3}\tan^{-1}\left(\frac{3-2e^{u}}{\sqrt{3}}\right)\right)}{2\sqrt{6}\lambda}&-\frac{1}{6}i\lambda\left(6\tan^{-1}\left(\frac{3-2e^{u}}{\sqrt{3}}\right)-\sqrt{3}\log\left(-3e^{u}+e^{2u}+3\right)\right)&0\\
\end{pmatrix}}\end{split}

and

||\hat{U}(t,u)||<C_{4}.

Proof.

By construction, i.e., from (
7.22
),

\Omega_{t}(u)=\phi\;\phi_{1}\,\phi_{2}

where
\phi
and
\phi_{1}
are as above (i.e. the first two factors in (
7.24
)), and

\phi_{2}
solves

d\phi_{2}+\beta\phi_{2}=0\hskip 10.00002pt\hskip 10.00002pt\text{with}\hskip 10.00002pt\hskip 10.00002pt\phi_{2}(z=0)=R(t)=\Id+O(t^{2}).

Note that the primitives

U=\int_{0}^{u}\beta_{1}\hskip 10.00002pt\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\hskip 10.00002pt\int_{0}^{u}\beta_{2}

of
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
are uniformly bounded for small
t>0
and for
u
satisfying (
7.23
).
Therefore, the statement follows from Lemma
7.9
using Grönwall inequality once more.
∎

Define

(7.25)

W:=-\lim_{u\to-\infty}U(u)=-\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{\left(\lambda^{3}-1\right)\left(\pi+\sqrt{3}\log(3)\right)}{6\sqrt{2}\lambda}&-\frac{i\left(\lambda^{3}+1\right)\left(\pi-\sqrt{3}\log(3)\right)}{6\sqrt{2}\lambda^{2}}\\
\frac{\left(\lambda^{3}-1\right)\left(\pi-\sqrt{3}\log(3)\right)}{6\sqrt{2}\lambda^{2}}&0&-\frac{i\left(\pi+\sqrt{3}\log(3)\right)}{6\lambda}\\
-\frac{i\left(\lambda^{3}+1\right)\left(\pi+\sqrt{3}\log(3)\right)}{6\sqrt{2}\lambda}&-\frac{1}{6}i\lambda\left(\pi-\sqrt{3}\log(3)\right)&0\\
\end{pmatrix}.

We then obtain the following refinement of Lemma
7.5
:

Lemma 7.11
.

Consider the coordinate
w
with
z=1-w^{\tfrac{1}{t}}
on the disc
{\mathbb{D}}
.
It holds locally uniformly on
\{w\in\mathbb{C}\mid 0<|w|<1\}
and for all small
t>0:

\Omega_{t}(w)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&-\frac{i}{\lambda}\\
0&0&1\\
\end{pmatrix}\exp(-A_{1}\log w)(\Id+tW+O(t^{2})\,).

Proof.

Recall that in the domain
\{w\in\mathbb{C}\mid|w|<1\}
it holds locally uniformly

\mu_{t}=\nu+O(t^{2}).

Since
w=\exp(tu)
and for any
w_{0}
with
|w_{0}|<1

\int_{w=1}^{w_{0}}w^{*}\beta_{1}=\int_{u=0}^{-\infty}\beta_{1}=\lim_{u\to-\infty}U(u)=-W

we obtain the statement directly from Proposition
7.10
.
∎

Using Lemma
7.11
, we can study the first-order deformation in
t
of the special Legendrian surfaces
\hat{f}_{k}
,
k=t^{-1}
,
at
t=0.

Namely, the following theorem shows that on the
w
-disc the first-order deformation of the surface is just given by a conformal reparametrization
by an inward-pointing conformal vector field.

Theorem 16
.

Consider
\hat{f}_{t}\colon{\mathbb{D}}\to\mathbb{S}^{5}

as in Theorem
15
. Then

\dot{\hat{f}}_{t=0}=d\hat{f}(X),\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 10.00002pt\text{
where}\hskip 10.00002ptX=-\tfrac{\log(3)}{2}r\tfrac{\partial}{\partial r}=-\tfrac{\log(3)}{2}(x\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x}+y\tfrac{\partial}{\partial y})

is a conformal vector field on the disc with conformal coordinate
w=x+iy
.

Remark 7.12
.

This first-order deformation is
locally uniform, but not uniform, on the disc
{\mathbb{D}}.
We will use Proposition
7.10
to improve the statement.

Proof.

Consider the solution
\Omega_{t}
of
d\Omega_{t}+\omega_{t}\Omega_{t}=0
with appropriate initial condition
R(t)

which guarantees unitary monodromy. Consider the Iwasawa decomposition

\Omega_{t}=B_{t}F_{t}
where
B_{t}
is (normalized) positive in the loop group and
F_{t}
is unitary.
We have computed
B_{0}
and
F_{0}
in the proof of Theorem
15
.
Expand
B_{t}=B_{0}(1+tP+\dots)
and
F_{t}=F_{0}(1+t\widetilde{X}+\dots)

where

P\colon{\mathbb{D}}\to{\bf sl}_{3}^{\hat{\tau}}(\mathbb{D})\hskip 10.00002pt\text{
and}\hskip 10.00002pt\widetilde{X}\colon{\mathbb{D}}\to{\bf su}_{3}^{\hat{\tau}}(\mathbb{S}^{1}),

and use

\begin{split}\Omega_{t}&=\Omega_{0}(1+tW+\dots)=B_{0}F_{0}(1+tW+\dots)=B_{0}(1+tP+\dots)F_{0}(1+t\widetilde{X}+\dots)\end{split}

to obtain the equation

F_{0}W=PF_{0}+F_{0}\widetilde{X}.

Recall from (
7.25
) that
W\in{\bf sl}_{3}^{\hat{\tau}}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
is independent of
w\in\mathbb{D}.

Split

W=W_{u}+W_{s}

with

W_{u},(iW_{s})\in{\bf su}_{3}^{\hat{\tau}}(\mathbb{S}^{1}).

We can ignore
W_{u}
in the following since it is unitary and independent of
w,
i.e., it only corresponds to an infinitesimal symmetry of
\mathbb{S}^{5}.

Then
W_{s}
satisfies
(W_{s})^{*}=W_{s}
. This property is preserved under conjugation with elements of
\Lambda\SU_{3}.
Consider

(7.26)

\begin{split}\widetilde{W}:=F_{0}W_{s}F_{0}^{-1}=P+F_{0}\hat{X}F_{0}^{-1}\end{split}

where
\hat{X}=\widetilde{X}-W_{u}
, and where the left hand side
\widetilde{W}
is symmetric. Splitting

\widetilde{W}=\widetilde{W}^{+}+\widetilde{W}^{0}+\widetilde{W}^{-}

into positive, constant and negative parts and using
F_{0}\hat{X}F_{0}^{-1}\in{\bf su}_{3}^{\hat{\tau}}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
therefore gives:

P=2\widetilde{W}^{+}+\widetilde{W}^{0}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptF_{0}\hat{X}F_{0}^{-1}=-\widetilde{W}^{+}+\widetilde{W}^{-}.

Then, (neglecting the infinitesimal rotational part) a direct computation yields

\begin{split}\dot{\hat{f}}^{t=0}&=\dot{(F_{t})^{-1}(\lambda_{0})}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}=-(\hat{X}F_{0}^{-1})(\lambda_{0})\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}\\
&=\tfrac{\left(\sqrt{3}+2\right)\log(3)}{2\left(x^{2}+y^{2}+\sqrt{3}+2\right)^{2}}\begin{pmatrix}4i(x^{2}+y^{2})\\
(\sqrt{3}-1)(ix+y)(x^{2}+y^{2}-\sqrt{3}-2)\\
(\sqrt{3}-1)(ix-y)(x^{2}+y^{2}-\sqrt{3}-2)\end{pmatrix}=d{\hat{f}}^{t=0}(-\tfrac{\log(3)}{2}(x\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x}+y\tfrac{\partial}{\partial y}))\end{split}

as claimed.
∎

7.7.
Proof of Embeddedness

Before proving embeddedness of the Legendrian lifts, we note that the projected minimal Lagrangian immersions in
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
cannot be embedded. Indeed, for any compact oriented Lagrangian surface
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{CP}^{2}
one has

[\Sigma]\cdot[\Sigma]=-\chi(\Sigma)=2g-2,

see for example
[
60
]
. Since
g(\Sigma_{k})=\frac{1}{2}(k-1)(k-2)
, the algebraic self-intersection number of
f_{k}(\Sigma_{k})
equals
k^{2}-3k
.
It can be shown that the
3k
many points

p^{j}_{\ell}\in\Sigma_{k}
are mapped via
f_{k}
to the three points
[1,0,0],

[0,1,0]
and
[0,0,1]
, respectively, and each
of the three
contributes with self-intersection number
-k.

Similarly, each of the

k^{2}
-many preimages of
0\in\mathbb{CP}^{1}\in\Sigma_{k}/\Gamma_{deck}
is mapped to the same point in
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
as
exactly one of the
k^{2}
-many preimages of
\infty\in\mathbb{CP}^{1}\in\Sigma_{k}/\Gamma_{deck}
, and the corresponding
self-intersection number is
1.
Using the symmetries of
\hat{f}_{k}
, one sees that these points get separated through
the horizontal special Legendrian lift
\hat{f}_{k}.
In fact, we have:

Theorem 17
.

For all large
k
and
c_{0}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}
, the special Legendrian surfaces
\hat{f}_{k}=\hat{f}_{k}^{+}\colon\Sigma_{k}\to\mathbb{S}^{5}
constructed in Corollary
6.1
and Theorem
8
are embedded.

Proof.

The proof is by contradiction based on Theorem
14
, Theorem
15
and Theorem
16
. Let

a_{k}\neq b_{k}\in\Sigma_{k}

be a sequence of distinct points satisfying

c_{k}:=\hat{f}_{k}(a_{k})=\hat{f}_{k}(b_{k})\in\mathbb{S}^{5}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all sufficiently large }k.

Since
\mathbb{S}^{5}
is compact, after taking a subsequence if necessary, the sequence
c_{k}
has a limit point, denoted by
c.

We make use of the notations introduced in Section
7.4
.
In particular, we recall that
the centers of the discs
{\mathbb{D}}^{j}_{\ell}
,
j=1,2,3
, are mapped to equidistant points
on the circles

C_{1}=\mathbb{S}^{5}\cap\mathbb{C}\oplus 0\oplus 0,\hskip 10.00002ptC_{2}=\mathbb{S}^{5}\cap 0\oplus\mathbb{C}\oplus 0,\hskip 10.00002ptC_{3}=\mathbb{S}^{5}\cap 0\oplus 0\oplus\mathbb{C}

respectively.
Composing, for each
k
, with a suitable element of
\Gamma_{deck}
and the corresponding
\SU_{3}
-symmetry of
\mathbb{S}^{5}
, we may assume

a_{k}\in\overline{\mathbb{D}^{1}_{1}},\hskip 20.00003pt\arg w(a_{k})\in\Bigl[0,\frac{2\pi}{k}\Bigr).

Since these ambient symmetries are isometries of
\mathbb{S}^{5}
, the equality

\hat{f}_{k}(a_{k})=\hat{f}_{k}(b_{k})
is preserved.
In particular, Proposition
7.8
then implies that
c
is contained in the image of the geodesic segment
from
(i,0,0)
to
\tfrac{i}{\sqrt{3}}(1,1,1).

We distinguish two cases.

Case 1:
c\neq\tfrac{i}{\sqrt{3}}(1,1,1)

Using Proposition
7.8
, we have
a_{k}\in{\mathbb{D}}^{1}_{1}
. Let
b_{k}\in\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{j(k)}_{\ell(k)}

where
j(k)\in\{1,2,3\}
and
\ell(k)\in\{1,\dots,k\}.

For
k
large, Proposition
7.8
(together with the symmetries) implies that
j(k)=1
.
Moreover, if

\lim_{k\to\infty}|w(b_{k})|=1

it follows that the limit

\lim_{k\to\infty}\hat{f}_{k}(b_{k})\in\{\psi^{s}\phi^{t}\tfrac{i}{\sqrt{3}}(1,1,1)\mid\;s,t\in[0,1]\}=:\text{Cliff}

is contained in the Clifford torus
2
2

2

This Clifford torus is also minimal Legendrian, but with different Legendrian phase.
in
\mathbb{S}^{5}
, where

\psi^{s}=\text{diag}(1,\exp(2\pi is),\exp(-2\pi is))
and
\phi^{t}=\text{diag}(\exp(2\pi it),1,\exp(-2\pi it))

are the continuous symmetries corresponding to the discrete symmetries
\tilde{\varphi}_{1}
and
\tilde{\varphi}_{3},
respectively. Hence, there exists
r<1
such that for all large enough
k

|w(b_{k})|<r.

By construction, i.e.
because the infinitesimal generator of
\phi^{t}
is transverse to the compact cap,
(compare also with Remark
7.4
), there is a constant
c_{0}>0
such that for small
t>0

(7.27)

\text{dist}_{\mathbb{S}^{5}}(\,\hat{f}({\mathbb{D}}),\phi^{t}(\hat{f}({\mathbb{D}}))\,)\geq c_{0}t.

Denote

{\mathbb{D}}^{1}_{\ell}(r):=\{b\in{\mathbb{D}}_{\ell}^{1}\mid|w(b)|<r<1\}.

From Proposition
7.8
and Theorem
16
together with the symmetries
and (
7.27
)
we therefore
obtain

(7.28)

\text{dist}_{\mathbb{S}^{5}}(\,\hat{f}_{k}({\mathbb{D}}^{1}_{1}(r)),\hat{f}_{k}({\mathbb{D}}^{1}_{\ell}(r))\,)\geq c_{1}\tfrac{1}{k}

for a suitable
c_{1}>0
,
k
large and every
\ell\in\{2,\dots,k\}.
We therefore conclude that
b_{k}\in{\mathbb{D}}^{1}_{1}(r)
for all large
k.

Therefore, Proposition
7.8
and Theorem
16

imply
a_{k}=b_{k}
for large
k,
showing that Case 1 is not possible.

Case 2:
c=\tfrac{i}{\sqrt{3}}(1,1,1)

Similar to the proof of (
7.28
), we assume without loss of generality that

b_{k}\in\bar{\mathbb{D}}^{j(k)}_{\ell(k)}
for all large
k
with

\ell(k)/k\to 0
,
j(k)\in\{1,2,3\}
and

\lim_{k}\arg(w(b_{k}))=0

where
w
is the natural holomorphic coordinate on the corresponding discs.
Likewise, this can also be deduced from Corollary
7.2
.

We distinguish three cases by whether the projections of
a_{k},b_{k}
remain in a fixed compact subset of the base
\mathbb{CP}^{1}\setminus\{1,\zeta,\zeta^{2}\}
.

(i)

the points
a_{k}
and
b_{k}
project to some compact subset
\mathbb{K}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{1}\setminus\{1,\zeta,\zeta^{2}\}=\Sigma_{k}/(\mathbb{Z}_{k}\times\mathbb{Z}_{k})
for all large
k;

(ii)

for every compact subset
\mathbb{K}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{1}\setminus\{1,\zeta,\zeta^{2}\}
both
a_{k}
and
b_{k}
are not contained in
\mathbb{K}
for all large
k;

(iii)

for every (large enough) compact subset
\mathbb{K}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{1}\setminus\{1,\zeta,\zeta^{2}\}
exactly one of
a_{k}
and
b_{k}
is contained in
\mathbb{K}
for all large
k.

We will show in all three cases that
a_{k}=b_{k}
for large
k
. Going to subsequences if necessary, this then proves the general case.

Case 2 (i)

In this case, there exists
\delta>0
such that

\text{dist}(\hat{f}_{k}(a_{k}),c)<\delta\tfrac{1}{k}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\text{dist}(\hat{f}_{k}(b_{k}),c)<\delta\tfrac{1}{k}
